What’s up with the new federal permitting reform bill?142 min50 insights2026-10-14
▶ Watch on YouTube
David Roberts and Arnold Ventures' Daniel Palin walk through the 417-page bipartisan BAAJA federal permitting bill: strong transmission planning and FERC backstop siting, data center 'and pricing', geothermal and grid-tech provisions, fossil/pipeline and Clean Water Act concessions, NEPA no-vacatur/no-injunction reform, and controversial permit-certainty sections 1401-1403. Palin argues the net emissions sign is negative and puts passage odds at 50/50 in the lame duck.
Bill architecture and the clean-vs-fossil bet · 6Transmission planning, backstop siting and utility discipline · 11Data centers and grid cost allocation · 6Geothermal as the sleeper winner · 3Fossil, mining and water provisions: limited real impact · 9NEPA judicial reform and permit certainty · 8Politics, passage odds and emissions uncertainty · 7
Bill architecture and the clean-vs-fossil bet 6
The bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act (BAAJA) permitting bill was negotiated by Senators Capito and Whitehouse (EPW) and Lee and Heinrich (ENR); it is 417 pages with 71 sections and is more ambitious in almost every direction than analysts expected.
data_point🗣 David Roberts (host)conviction: highBAAJAShelley Moore CapitoSheldon WhitehouseMike LeeMartin Heinrich
So what: Largest federal permitting package attempted; scope exceeds the 2022 Manchin-Schumer, 2023 FRA and Manchin-Barrasso EPRA efforts.
“this is a 417page bill with 71 sections” ▶ 14:20
Clean energy industry boosters and investors and fossil energy groups both love the bill, while many climate organizations urge Democrats to vote against it and the House Freedom Caucus objects to nearly any pro-renewable provisions.
data_point🗣 David Roberts (host)conviction: highFreedom Caucusclimate organizations
So what: Coalitions split internally; investor sentiment on both clean and fossil sides is positive.
“clean energy industry boosters and investors love it. Fossil energy groups also love it” ▶ 1:31
Negotiators describe the bill explicitly as a bet: each party believes its preferred technologies (renewables for Democrats, fossil for Republicans) will outperform on a level playing field once permitting barriers are reduced.
framework🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: highSheldon WhitehouseMartin Heinrich
So what: The bill's political logic is tech-neutral deregulation; outcome depends on relative economics of clean vs fossil.
“almost as a bet, where I bet you that on a level playing field, my technologies will outperform your technologies” ▶ 10:06
Palin argues manufactured technologies (solar modules, roughly 1,000x cheaper since 1991) structurally benefit from easier building versus drilled resources where geology competes with technology (oil roughly flat in real cost for decades), so permitting reform increasingly advantages clean tech each decade if Wright's law holds.
thesis🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: highsolarWright's lawoil
So what: Long-horizon tailwind for modular clean tech from any build-faster policy.
“there is a nifty asymmetry between technologies that you have to drill for where geology competes against technology and technologies that you mass-produce” ▶ 8:01
The bill also contains low-profile administrative-capacity 'Easter eggs': pay/hiring flexibility for power marketing administrations like Bonneville and WAPA, special FERC hiring authority for economists and lawyers, a geothermal ombudsman with 25% retention allowances, $20M/yr for tribal historic preservation digitization, and $20M/yr for five years for streamlined distributed-energy permitting.
data_point🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: mediumBonneville Power AdministrationWestern Area Power AdministrationFERCOffice of Personnel Management
So what: Partial answer to critics (e.g. Adelman and Plume) that reform should fund agency capacity, not just cut review scope; still small next to appropriations decisions.
Freezing permits and slowing building favors the incumbent system, which is predominantly fossil, so permit uncertainty and tit-for-tat cancellations are negative-sum and mostly result in building nothing because projects outlast four-year administrations.
contrarian🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: highfossil fuels
So what: Climate advocates opposing build-faster reforms may be protecting a fossil status quo.
“The incumbent system today is predominantly fossil” ▶ 1:35:23
Transmission planning, backstop siting and utility discipline 11
Under FERC Order 1000 (2011), 11 transmission planning regions with 15 region-to-region borders have had roughly 225 region-pair-years of interregional coordination, which produced exactly zero interregional transmission lines.
data_point🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: highFERCOrder 1000
So what: Quantifies the failure of the status quo interregional process the bill replaces.
“It is actually identically zero. We've built zero transmission lines as a result of that process” ▶ 23:27
The bill mandates periodic interregional (and regional) planning with best practices, requiring regions to identify needs and select transmission solutions that meet statutory affordability and reliability metrics.
thesis🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: highFERCRTOsISOs
So what: Creates a pipeline of interregional lines; beneficiaries include transmission developers and equipment suppliers.
Section 21101 overhauls FERC backstop siting to put applicants in the driver's seat: a non-incumbent developer can bring a line to FERC, which must permit it and allocate costs on a beneficiary-pays basis if it passes a cost-benefit test and reliability guardrails.
thesis🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: highFERCSection 21101
So what: Independent transmission developers gain a viable path that bypasses stalled utility planning.
The backstop's main value is as a threat: utilities would rather run regional planning where they have a privileged seat than let outsiders bring lines straight to FERC, so the backstop disciplines planning rather than flooding FERC, which lacks capacity to evaluate many lines.
causal🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: highFERCutilities
So what: Expect behavior change in RTO planning even with few actual backstop applications.
“it will be a massive incentive check on utilities to say we're not going to turn this into any sort of paper exercise” ▶ 27:29
Utilities often resist interregional lines despite rate-base incentives because such lines are typically bid out competitively and enable generation elsewhere that lowers prices in their territory.
causal🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: mediumutilitiesRTOs
So what: Explains why incumbent utilities are a likely lobbying force against the transmission titles.
Excluding state policy benefits (emission cuts, RPS compliance) from transmission cost allocation is, in Palin's view, the only way interstate planning works, because red states and red counties host most lines; there is enough raw affordability and reliability value to justify many lines anyway.
contrarian🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: highrenewable portfolio standardsDOE
So what: Lines will be justified on economics, not climate; the case remains strong per DOE's transmission need study.
“the only way that we are ever going to get transmission planning at the interstate level to work” ▶ 29:22
Transmission provides three benefits: headroom (most important, letting new generation or load connect without years-long studies and huge network-upgrade costs), smoothing of variable resources and weather events, and access to prime resources.
framework🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: hightransmission
So what: Grid headroom is the binding constraint for new gas, wind, solar and data center connections alike.
Only about 1.3 GW of transfer capability exists across the seam between the Western and Eastern interconnections, although the Western Interconnection serves about 20% of US load and holds the best geothermal resources.
data_point🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: highWestern InterconnectionEastern Interconnectiongeothermal
So what: Seam capacity is a bottleneck for scaling western geothermal nationally.
“you're going to need more than the poulry 1.3 gawatt of transfer capability that we currently have across the seam” ▶ 32:43
States gain sole discretion to refer their utility to FERC for investigation of imprudent local transmission spending, with FERC market-manipulation-style subpoena powers and penalties including fines up to $5 million per facility, ROE cuts and loss of the prudence presumption.
data_point🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: highFERCutilities
So what: Targets overbuilt local transmission in rate base; negative for utility returns on local wires capex.
“fines up to $5 million for imprudent facilities, reductions in returns on equity” ▶ 35:14
The bill creates a best-available-conductor standard and gives utilities a presumption of prudence for advanced conductors when benefit-cost ratio exceeds one, and cuts NEPA burden to near zero for such upgrades in existing rights of way.
thesis🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: highadvanced conductorsgrid-enhancing technologiesNEPA
So what: Demand catalyst for reconductoring and grid-enhancing technology vendors.
A sleeper interconnection-automation provision (from a prior DOE AI bill) directs FERC to require RTOs to modernize interconnection processes using cloud computing, AI and machine learning, resolving whether FERC would act on its own.
prediction🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: mediumFERCRTOsAI
So what: Upside for interconnection-automation software startups if they deliver.
“So that's like a sleeper provision that I see as having a lot of upside” ▶ 38:05
Data centers and grid cost allocation 6
Section 21107 imposes 'and pricing' on computational loads: data centers must pay both the incremental cost of network upgrades and the average embedded grid cost, rather than one or the other under FERC's 1994 policy.
data_point🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: highdata centersFERCSection 21107
So what: Potentially billions of dollars shifted from data centers to ratepayers and grid infrastructure.
“It says computational loads and that's called and pricing. You pay the incremental cost and you pay the average embedded cost.” ▶ 41:40
Palin defends charging data centers more than their fair share for network costs because they drive up many hidden costs (distribution, energy, capacity, ancillary services, equipment and commodity prices) that policymakers don't capture.
thesis🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: mediumdata centers
So what: Political response to affordability angst; sets a precedent for differentiated large-load tariffs.
The bill lets states allocate interconnection rights via open-season auctions rather than first-come queues, an idea associated with Travis Kavulla; Palin expects it to apply mainly to very large loads like data centers.
framework🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: mediumTravis Kavulladata centers
So what: Auction revenue could flow to ratepayers; queue position loses value as a property right.
Higher grid charges do create an incentive for data centers to go behind-the-meter, but the 'bit-watt spread' (revenue per gigawatt vs cost of power, perhaps 50x) is so large that a $5-10/MWh increase is small relative to speed to power.
causal🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: mediumdata centersbehind-the-meter gas
So what: Speed to power and social license, not tariffs, are the binding constraints on data center siting.
“Speed to power and social license are like the two rate limiting factors” ▶ 58:49
Data center developers going off-grid would prefer not to be their own utility and fuel procurer; grid lack of headroom is what drives the off-grid rush, so transmission buildout should pull them back to the grid in the medium-long run.
prediction🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: mediumdata centers
So what: Off-grid gas for data centers may be a transitional phenomenon.
“it's the fact that the grid is out of headroom and out of capacity that is driving the off-grid rush” ▶ 59:43
The bill adds a VPP piece to speed compliance with FERC Order 2222 on aggregated distributed resources in wholesale markets, plus a $20M/yr grant program for local interconnection of distributed resources such as rooftop solar, EV charging and home batteries.
data_point🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: mediumFERC Order 2222virtual power plantsrooftop solar
So what: Modest support for DER aggregators; under-discussed.
Geothermal as the sleeper winner 3
Palin argues geothermal is the one major new clean technology where, if the US does not lead, nobody will on a relevant time scale, given US fracking technology, workforce, companies and resources.
thesis🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: highgeothermalFervo
So what: Policy tailwinds concentrate on US geothermal developers.
“if the US does not do this nobody else is really going to on a relevant time scale” ▶ 45:33
The geothermal title (about nine provisions, from Reps. Ansari and Huffman) creates a geothermal 'gold book' permitting manual, new categorical exclusions for test wells mirroring oil and gas, more frequent lease sales, rationalized royalties on upgrades, relief for laterals from private into federal minerals, and continued processing during litigation.
data_point🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: highgeothermalDepartment of the InteriorYassamin AnsariJared Huffman
So what: Incremental individually but cumulatively lowers geothermal permitting cost and time.
The biggest geothermal benefits come from the NEPA/NHPA and transmission reforms, because NEPA burdens fall asymmetrically on western federal land, which is exactly where geothermal resources sit; companies like Fervo rank transmission first.
causal🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: highgeothermalFervoNEPANHPA
So what: Geothermal gains disproportionately from tech-neutral provisions.
Fossil, mining and water provisions: limited real impact 9
Section 1102 exempts upgrades to existing pipelines and transmission rights of way (including compressors, per Roberts) from NEPA; Roberts cites one model calling it possibly the most emissions-increasing part of the bill.
data_point🗣 David Roberts (host)conviction: mediumSection 1102NEPAnatural gas pipelines
So what: Eases capacity expansion on existing gas corridors serving data center regions.
Palin is skeptical Section 1102 is game-changing because pipeline buildout is concentrated in easy-permitting areas (Haynesville, Permian, Marcellus) and Clean Air Act permits, not NEPA, are the rate-limiting step.
contrarian🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: mediumHaynesvillePermianMarcellusClean Air Act
So what: Gas midstream upside from the bill is likely modest.
“These are areas where it tends to be relatively easy to get permits.” ▶ 51:12
A Stanford study of a decade of NEPA analyses found gas pipelines underwent fewer full EISs than solar, even though the period coincided with the shale boom when solar barely existed.
data_point🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: mediumStanfordNEPAsolargas pipelines
So what: Evidence NEPA burden falls more on clean projects.
“gas pipelines somewhat shockingly underwent fewer NEPA EIS's than solar over the same period” ▶ 51:38
With about 90% of US onshore oil and gas production off federal land, and only the New Mexico Permian as a significant federal shale play, the bill's drilling provisions are unlikely to move production much.
data_point🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: mediumPermianfederal lands
So what: Limited production upside for E&Ps from federal drilling streamlining.
“with 90ish% of US onshore production not occurring on federal land” ▶ 53:20
Palin argues supply-side restrictions on oil are a bad emissions strategy because oil is globally traded and inelastic, so added US output is largely offset elsewhere.
contrarian🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: mediumoil
So what: Frames the bill's fossil concessions as low climate cost.
The mining provision restores the pre-Rosemont status quo by letting miners use mill sites for waste rock and tailings, with a fee about twice the EPRA bill's level going to the hard rock abandoned mine fund.
data_point🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: mediumRosemont rulingNinth CircuitHard Rock Mining Restoration Fund
So what: Removes a legal barrier to new US critical-mineral mines such as copper and lithium.
The Clean Water Act Section 401 reforms are the title most asymmetrically favoring pipelines: states must show clear and convincing evidence to deny and limit review to direct discharges, aimed at states like New York denying pipelines on climate grounds.
thesis🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: highClean Water ActSection 401New York
So what: Mainly unlocks Northeast pipelines; main gas demand markets are not blocked by this today.
Palin predicts states seeking to block federally backstopped transmission, now barred from economic objections by the 2025 Third Circuit Transource ruling, may turn to Section 401 water certifications, so the 401 reform has real value for transmission too.
prediction🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: mediumTransource rulingThird CircuitClean Water Act
So what: 401 reform protects transmission from a likely next blocking tactic.
Palin views the ESA reforms, including delegation to states certified as having technical, financial and personnel capacity, as modest and mostly a sideshow, citing Tim Male's analysis.
contrarian🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: mediumEndangered Species ActTim Male
So what: Biodiversity impact of the bill likely smaller than feared.
NEPA judicial reform and permit certainty 8
The biggest NEPA change eliminates vacatur, injunctions and preliminary injunctions as remedies for NEPA violations, making NEPA much less usable to obstruct projects while preserving its coordination and public-disclosure role.
thesis🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: highNEPAAdministrative Procedure Act
So what: Removes the main litigation delay mechanism for federal infrastructure projects.
Substantive environmental laws (Clean Water Act, ESA, Clean Air Act) remain enforceable, and projects violating their substantive standards can still be stopped, per Palin's reading of savings clauses.
thesis🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: mediumClean Water ActEndangered Species ActClean Air Act
So what: Litigation shifts to substantive statutes rather than disappearing.
The statute of limitations on NEPA suits falls from six years to 150 days.
data_point🗣 David Roberts (host)conviction: highNEPA
So what: Faster certainty for project finance.
The 2025 Seven County Supreme Court ruling already required substantial deference to agencies on NEPA and suggested vacatur need not follow every error, and challengers are already shifting to Natural Gas Act balancing claims.
causal🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: mediumSupreme CourtSeven County Infrastructure CoalitionNatural Gas Act
So what: The bill partly codifies a direction courts were already taking.
In a developer survey, 82% said they had avoided federal lands due to anticipated NEPA or other approvals; federal lands are 21% of the lower 48 but hold only about 3-4% of wind, solar and geothermal.
data_point🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: highNEPAfederal lands
So what: Supports the thesis that NEPA has asymmetrically burdened clean energy; reform opens federal land.
≈ “82% yes, 18% no” paraphrase ▶ 1:18:22
Section 1401 bars agencies from revoking, suspending or altering issued permits except for court orders, fraud/illegality, or specific urgent proximate harm, shifting the burden to the government under a clear and convincing standard.
thesis🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: highSection 1401Trump administration
So what: Protects wind and solar from Trump-style revocations but also locks in fossil permits against a future Democratic administration.
Section 1402 lets developers (not AGs or trade groups) sue over disparate treatment compared to the prior five years, with damages of 50-100% of expected construction cost (1401 offers roughly 25-50%), which Roberts fears invites venue-shopping windfalls; Palin thinks 60-day-overdue and systematic showings limit abuse.
data_point🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: mediumSection 1402Section 1401
So what: Novel litigation and compensation risk for federal government; key technical fight before a manager's amendment.
Section 1403 creates a cause of action for unreasonable delay against new permit deadlines, and a reported side deal has Trump releasing a clutch of wind farms held up at the FAA and Department of War, roughly 20% of them.
data_point🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: mediumSection 1403FAADepartment of WarTrump administration
So what: Near-term relief for some stalled wind projects; others rely on ongoing litigation that is generally going well.
Politics, passage odds and emissions uncertainty 7
Palin argues waiting for a better Democratic deal is futile: any future bill needs 60 Senate votes, each successive permitting bill ratchets both sides' demands upward, 2027-28 is a presidential cycle, and key experienced members such as Whitehouse and Westerman are turning over.
thesis🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: highSheldon WhitehouseBruce WestermanJeff Merkley
So what: The lame duck is the realistic window for permitting reform.
Roberts argues green groups opposing the bill are misleading members by implying a better deal is available in the next Congress.
contrarian🗣 David Roberts (host)conviction: highclimate organizations
So what: Signals host support for passage despite progressive opposition.
“there's just not a better Democraton bill on the horizon” ▶ 1:59:05
Palin is confident the net emissions sign is negative (emissions fall), materially in the medium-long run, but says magnitude modeling is false precision given energy-sector shocks every 3-5 years like data center load growth; one bottom-up model found net emissions a coin toss over 10 years.
prediction🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: mediumdata centers
So what: Treat emissions models as directional; transmission and land access give optionality across scenarios.
“anybody who's telling you a number with two significant digits is definitely making up the second” ▶ 2:09:26
Utilities, which take hits on local transmission prudence, competitive interregional lines and data center pricing, are likely to lobby behind the scenes to weaken the bill and deserve more attention than green groups as blockers.
prediction🗣 David Roberts (host)conviction: mediumutilities
So what: Watch utility lobbying in the manager's amendment.
Process: nothing happens until after the November election; then a 60-vote Senate cloture, a manager's amendment for technical fixes (including an unresolved offshore wind language issue), and House passage via rule, suspension (two-thirds), or attachment to the December 11 funding bill or NDAA.
framework🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: highSenateHouseNDAA
So what: Lame-duck must-pass vehicles are key catalysts to watch.
Palin disputes Jared Huffman's claim that the bill must get much worse to win the Freedom Caucus, noting several House pathways do not require Freedom Caucus votes.
contrarian🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: mediumJared HuffmanFreedom Caucus
So what: Passage does not hinge on pivoting right.
Palin puts odds of passage at 50/50, up from a 10-30% range over the prior six months.
prediction🗣 Daniel Palin (Arnold Ventures)conviction: mediumBAAJA
So what: Highest probability yet for federal permitting reform; a real catalyst for transmission, geothermal and grid tech.
“I think at my highest I've been at 30. At my lowest, I've been at 10. So, I've been oscillating around 20. I am now at 50%.” ▶ 2:19:35
Checking back in with Greenlight America67 min37 insights2026-10-14
▶ Watch on YouTube
Greenlight America CEO Matt Traldy tells David Roberts (live at a Canary Media Climate Week NYC event) that safe-harbored wind/solar must clear local permitting by early-to-mid 2028 to capture credits, driving an explosion of county hearings where organized local supporters lift Greenlight's win rate to 79%. He expects only a modest 20-30% post-deadline dip, flags restrictive ordinances and blue-state storage bans, and warns that data center backlash is already pushing would-be supporters away from clean energy. Note: the transcript repeats the intro, figures differ between intro and interview (55.7 vs 67 GW; staff counts), and it cuts off mid-Q&A.

Tax-credit deadline sprint and post-2028 outlook 7
Under last year's reconciliation bill (OBBBA), wind and solar tax credits are being phased out while credits for many other clean technologies were left in place.
data_point🗣 Matt Traldy (CEO, Greenlight America)conviction: highOBBBAwindsolar
So what: Policy risk is technology-specific: wind/solar face a cliff while other clean tech keeps credit support.
“It did phase out tax credits for wind and solar specifically. It left in place tax credits for many other technologies” ▶ 9:44
Large developers Greenlight works with report having started construction (often via offsite equipment purchases, per IRS rules) on virtually all projects already in advanced planning, meeting the July start-of-construction deadline.
data_point🗣 Matt Traldy (CEO, Greenlight America)conviction: highIRSdevelopers
So what: A large safe-harbored pipeline exists on paper; the binding constraint has shifted to permitting and on-site build.
“we hear that they've started construction on virtually all of their projects that are already in advanced planning” ▶ 10:17
Because projects that began construction in 2025 must be placed in service within four years (by end-2029), and on-site build plus interconnection takes 12-18 months minimum, developers must clear local permitting and break ground by early-to-mid 2028.
causal🗣 Matt Traldy (CEO, Greenlight America)conviction: highIRSgrid interconnection
So what: Expect a surge of local permitting hearings through 2027-early 2028; local approvals become the critical path for credit capture.
“they'll have to be through the permitting phase and be able to start building on the ground by early to mid 2028” ▶ 21:31
Citing Wood Mackenzie (transcribed as Woods McKenzie), roughly 216-240 GW of solar and about 20-plus GW of wind have been safe harbored, versus a historical good year of about 50 GW of build.
data_point🗣 Matt Traldy (CEO, Greenlight America)conviction: mediumWoods McKenziesolarwind
So what: The safe-harbored backlog equals several years of prior US build pace, implying a multi-year deployment wave if permits clear.
“something like 216 to 240 gawatts of solar and another maybe 20ome gigawatts of wind has been safe harbored” ▶ 22:03
Industry contacts expect only a modest, temporary post-deadline dip in deployment, with the largest estimate heard being a 20-30% decline for a year or two, cushioned by rising power demand.
prediction🗣 Matt Traldy (CEO, Greenlight America)conviction: mediumpower demanddevelopers
So what: Downside to post-2028 solar/wind volumes may be smaller than feared; load growth supports continued demand.
“the largest number we've heard on that front for folks who've been willing to put a number on is maybe 20 to 30% of a decline” ▶ 23:35
Traldy says he is not worried about the US being overbuilt with clean energy after the sprint.
contrarian🗣 Matt Traldy (CEO, Greenlight America)conviction: mediumclean energy
So what: Counter to the pull-forward-then-bust narrative; the speaker sees structural demand outstripping supply.
“Uh I'm not worried about being overbuilt. Far from that.” ▶ 23:03
Greenlight once calculated that meeting emissions-reduction goals requires approving about 600 large-scale clean energy projects per year, almost two per day.
data_point🗣 Matt Traldy (CEO, Greenlight America)conviction: mediumGreenlight America
So what: Required throughput dwarfs current local approval capacity, so permitting remains a long-term bottleneck.
“it was 600 projects per year. So almost two per day of these large scale clean energy projects” ▶ 55:32
Local permitting as the binding constraint 7
The main obstacle to building clean power is neither technology nor cost but local permitting, where small, noisy opposition groups show up and supporters don't, so projects lose by forfeit.
thesis🗣 Matt Traldy (CEO, Greenlight America)conviction: highlocal permittingclean energy
So what: Permitting/siting risk, not tech cost, is the key execution risk for renewable developers.
“the big obstacle to doing that was not the technology which is well established or the cost. This was the cheapest and fastest energy to build. It was local permitting.” ▶ 3:28
Counties are the most common decision-making body for clean energy siting, and there are more than 3,100 US counties, with municipalities deciding in some states, so there is no central repository of upcoming decisions.
data_point🗣 Matt Traldy (CEO, Greenlight America)conviction: highcountiesmunicipalities
So what: Siting risk is extremely fragmented, rewarding whoever builds data infrastructure to track it.
“There are more than 3,100 counties in the US and county is generally I'll get to this in a moment but county is the most common decision-making body” ▶ 6:42
Most local ordinances Greenlight has seen advance are restrictive, ranging from outright bans to de facto bans via unreasonable setbacks.
data_point🗣 Matt Traldy (CEO, Greenlight America)conviction: highlocal ordinancessetbacks
So what: Ordinances are a bigger threat than single-project denials since they foreclose future pipeline in a jurisdiction.
“The majority of places that we've seen ordinances go forward, they are restrictive, right?” ▶ 39:20
Some of the toughest siting territory is in blue places; New York State has many local battery storage bans and Santa Fe County was a hard fight, even as New York approved the Propel transmission line.
contrarian🗣 Matt Traldy (CEO, Greenlight America)conviction: highNew YorkSanta Fe CountyPropel transmission linebattery storage
So what: Do not assume progressive jurisdictions are easy siting markets; storage bans in NY are a specific headwind.
“But there are a ton of places with bans on battery storage in New York State” ▶ 45:30
Proposals to meet clean energy needs only with sites like parking-lot solar are partly fantasy: projects use land that has other uses, so building enough requires accepting trade-offs.
thesis🗣 Matt Traldy (CEO, Greenlight America)conviction: highsolarland use
So what: Utility-scale land-intensive projects remain necessary; distributed-only strategies won't close the gap.
“These projects take up space. They use land. Most of the time, land is useful for something else to someone, right?” ▶ 46:22
On state permitting reform, Illinois's law works well because it keeps decisions local but sets clear rules of the road, and any uncertainty in process gets exploited by opponents to derail projects.
framework🗣 Matt Traldy (CEO, Greenlight America)conviction: mediumIllinoisstate permitting reform
So what: States with clear, certain siting criteria (e.g., Illinois) should carry lower permitting risk for developers.
“if there's not certainty about the way the process works or what's able to be considered, then opponents will absolutely use whatever uncertainty there is to derail stuff” ▶ 1:05:56
Project delays can cause cancellations or make equipment more expensive, rendering projects unviable, which is why speed should be a priority in permitting reform.
causal🗣 Matt Traldy (CEO, Greenlight America)conviction: mediumpermitting
So what: Permitting duration directly erodes project economics, not just timelines.
Greenlight's model, scale and win rates 8
Greenlight America runs an early warning system combining developer information-sharing (it is philanthropically, not developer, funded) with web scrapers and algorithmic review of thousands of local agenda and media alerts, plus a research team.
framework🗣 Matt Traldy (CEO, Greenlight America)conviction: highGreenlight Americaearly warning system
So what: Systematic tracking of local hearings is an emerging data asset that could matter for siting-risk assessment.
“we have web scrapers that gather this information we have algorithmic review to determine which of the alerts coming from these things” ▶ 6:59
Greenlight says it has helped advance 67 GW of clean power across the US, which it equates to more than 1 billion tons of lifetime emissions averted and 3.5 times what it would take to power New York City (the host's intro cites 20.9 GW in 2025 and about 55.7 GW since the prior conversation).
data_point🗣 Matt Traldy (CEO, Greenlight America)conviction: mediumGreenlight AmericaNew York City
So what: A meaningful share of the US utility-scale pipeline now passes through organized local-support campaigns.
“I'm pleased to share that we've now advanced 67 gawatts of clean power across the US.” ▶ 4:31
Greenlight's win rate rose from 60% in its pilot to 75% last year and 79% so far this year, versus an industry baseline Traldy guesses is roughly a coin flip.
data_point🗣 Matt Traldy (CEO, Greenlight America)conviction: highGreenlight America
So what: Organized supporter mobilization measurably raises approval odds for contested projects.
“Last year, our win rate was 75%. this year so far it's 79%.” ▶ 38:05
Using its scraping tools on historical decisions, Greenlight's early counterfactual analysis in the first several states found its win rate significantly above baseline.
data_point🗣 Matt Traldy (CEO, Greenlight America)conviction: lowGreenlight America
So what: Preliminary evidence that advocacy causally improves outcomes, though methodology is not detailed.
Greenlight went from four local wins in a quarter last year to eight local wins in a single day this month, reflecting the explosion of projects moving through permitting.
data_point🗣 Matt Traldy (CEO, Greenlight America)conviction: highGreenlight America
So what: Hearing volume is spiking now, consistent with the deadline-driven sprint.
“We had a day earlier this month where we had eight local winds in one day.” ▶ 21:47
Greenlight has nearly 90 staff (fully remote, largest team is campaigns), raised almost $20 million this year, aims to raise more next year, and has worked with almost 300 local partner organizations.
data_point🗣 Matt Traldy (CEO, Greenlight America)conviction: highGreenlight AmericaFaith in PlaceLand and Liberty
So what: Pro-build local advocacy is scaling quickly as a philanthropically funded field.
“we've raised almost $20 million this year. We're looking to raise even more next year.” ▶ 8:19
Neither Greenlight nor the field as a whole is close to covering 100% of local clean energy decisions, so there is substantial room and need to grow.
thesis🗣 Matt Traldy (CEO, Greenlight America)conviction: mediumGreenlight America
So what: Most local siting decisions still go uncontested by supporters, leaving outcomes to opponents.
Greenlight is technology neutral but follows mass deployment, so its work is mostly solar, storage and wind with growing transmission work; it has not yet worked on geothermal and does not work for or against data centers.
data_point🗣 Matt Traldy (CEO, Greenlight America)conviction: highsolarstoragewindtransmissiongeothermaldata centers
So what: Transmission siting is an expanding front for local mobilization.
What wins local hearings 10
Local decision makers are not deciding whether climate change is real but whether a project benefits the community, so effective messages center on economic benefits (big taxpayers funding public services) and property rights.
framework🗣 Matt Traldy (CEO, Greenlight America)conviction: highproperty rightstax revenue
So what: Local tax base and landowner-rights framing, not climate, drive approvals in conservative areas.
“They're deciding should we build this project here? And that's about whether it will benefit the community.” ▶ 29:25
A midsize utility-scale solar project roughly equals planting four million trees in emissions terms, and five local volunteers speaking at a county meeting can make the difference.
data_point🗣 Matt Traldy (CEO, Greenlight America)conviction: mediumutility-scale solar
So what: Tiny turnout can swing large-capacity decisions, an extremely high-leverage intervention.
“a midsize utility scale solar project is roughly the equivalent of planting four million trees in terms of emissions” ▶ 26:00
The feeling in the room drives what seems politically possible: if the first five speakers support a project it seems to sail through, if they oppose it, approval feels like political suicide, regardless of decision makers' priors.
causal🗣 Matt Traldy (CEO, Greenlight America)conviction: highcounty councils
So what: Hearing dynamics, not community-wide opinion polls, determine approvals.
“If the first five people speak up in favor of something, it feels like it's sailing through.” ▶ 36:46
Local permitting decisions are not 50%-plus-one popularity contests but hinge on who shows up, so Greenlight focuses on supporters rather than trying to convert opponents.
thesis🗣 Matt Traldy (CEO, Greenlight America)conviction: highGreenlight America
So what: Broad public polling on renewables is a poor predictor of local siting outcomes.
“They're not a 50% plus one vote in the community. They're not a popularity contest.” ▶ 51:19
Greenlight learned 'there is no bag': projects developers thought were safe lost when last-minute opposition appeared, so it now right-sizes by having supporters attend even when they may not need to speak.
anecdote🗣 Matt Traldy (CEO, Greenlight America)conviction: highGreenlight Americadevelopers
So what: Unopposed projects still carry late-stage siting risk; low-visibility strategies can backfire.
“There is no bag. There's no such thing as being in the bag. It's not in the bag until you've won the vote.” ▶ 40:13
Greenlight never shows up under its own name; it works through credible local partners across the spectrum, such as Faith in Place in Illinois, the conservative Land and Liberty, labor unions and Latinx groups.
framework🗣 Matt Traldy (CEO, Greenlight America)conviction: highFaith in PlaceLand and Libertylabor unions
So what: Local credibility, not national advocacy brands, moves conservative local boards.
“We're never showing up as Greenlight.” ▶ 30:57
Most siting decisions occur in conservative areas made by conservative decision makers, who typically judge on local benefit or neighbor sentiment rather than national partisan cues about windmills.
contrarian🗣 Matt Traldy (CEO, Greenlight America)conviction: mediumconservative communities
So what: Red-state siting risk is more local and negotiable than national rhetoric suggests.
Cited wins include a big solar project in Coles County, Illinois with Faith in Place; a Yakima County, Washington solar and agrivoltaics ordinance that became more permissive at the end after 10+ volunteers and 100+ petitions; and a rapid-response campaign for a 600 MW solar project in Morgan County, Colorado.
anecdote🗣 Matt Traldy (CEO, Greenlight America)conviction: mediumColes CountyYakima CountyMorgan CountyFaith in Placeagrivoltaics
So what: Concrete examples of contested utility-scale solar jurisdictions where organized support tipped outcomes.
Brokering talks between developers and groups with reasonable concerns can turn opposition into assets, e.g., introducing a reclaimed-mine-land solar developer to the local watershed association over watershed concerns.
anecdote🗣 Matt Traldy (CEO, Greenlight America)conviction: mediumreclaimed mine landwatershed association
So what: Early stakeholder engagement on site-specific issues de-risks approvals.
When countering disinformation, Traldy recommends George Lakoff's truth sandwich (start with a shared value, name that disinformation exists without repeating it, redirect) and says showing up in person beats social media.
framework🗣 Matt Traldy (CEO, Greenlight America)conviction: mediumGeorge Lakoffsocial media
So what: Repeating myths to debunk them spreads them; in-person constituent presence matters most to local officials.
“you don't want to like repeat and then mythbust the disinformation. That just spreads it further.” ▶ 1:03:08

Roberts worries that public fury at data centers (associated with AI, oligarchs and broad social ills) will spill over into opposition to electrification broadly, tainting solar, transmission and batteries.

BG2Pod · Brad Gerstner & Bill Gurley 1 episode(s) · 50 insights

Jensen Huang | Does AI Revenue Justify Capex, '27 AI Bottlenecks, Singularity, Open vs Closed & more100 min50 insights2026-10-15
▶ Watch on YouTube
Jensen Huang tells Brad Gerstner that AI compute demand far exceeds supply, with land, power, shell, substrates and photonics as the 2027 bottlenecks, and that Nvidia's share and content per gigawatt ($40B+) are rising. He argues capex payback does not hinge on two frontier labs, since roughly a third of Nvidia's business is classical computing and a third is AI startups, and he lays out an engineering-first safety framework plus new software-licensing and rev-share business models.
Compute demand: reasoning, agents and harnesses · 9Physical AI and the three-computer stack · 6AI safety framework and policy · 62027 bottlenecks: land, power, shell and supply chain · 8Does AI revenue justify capex · 9Nvidia economics, moat and capital return · 12
Compute demand: reasoning, agents and harnesses 9
Jensen Huang argues compute demand is set to rise roughly a billion-fold versus recent levels because reasoning (planning, rolling out options, pruning, reflecting) consumes far more inference than recalling memorized answers, and useful AI then gets applied to more jobs, compounding usage.
causal🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: highNvidia
So what: Inference demand compounds on two axes: tokens per task and number of tasks.
“it uses more compute to do its job, but it's doing a good job and therefore we're going to apply we're going to hire it to do more jobs” ▶ 10:28
Huang says the biggest unlock of the past year was not just better models but the 'harness' or exoskeleton around LLMs: working memory, offline knowledge-graph compaction, and tool use with existing professional software.
framework🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: highSPICEVerilogPhotoshopAutoCADSiemens
So what: Value accrues to incumbent tool vendors whose software agents learn to drive, and to agent infrastructure for memory.
“these two areas knowledge knowledge um and working memory and um tool use huge unlocks in the last year” ▶ 12:28
Huang predicts agents will use humans' existing tools (simulators, design compilers, CAD, EDA) rather than rebuild them from scratch, keeping those tools valuable.
thesis🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: mediumSPICEVerilogAutoCADPhotoshopSiemens
So what: Contrarian to the 'AI kills legacy software' view for engineering/design tool vendors.
“it's more likely you're going to use all the tools that I have in the house than to go off and build it from scratch” ▶ 11:57
Coding was the first on-ramp for agents; Huang says the next phase is design and creative work, citing OpenAI Astra turning a photo into editable CAD, which Nvidia uses internally.
prediction🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: mediumOpenAIAstraNvidia
So what: Agent adoption is moving from software engineering into design, CAD and marketing workflows.
“you take a picture and give it to Astra it literally recreates it into CAD” ▶ 13:53
Brad Gerstner notes this year's agent breakthroughs happened with OpenAI and Anthropic having only about 2-3 GW of compute each, and they may 3-5x compute next year, implying a double-exponential that linear economic models miss.
thesis🗣 Brad Gerstnerconviction: mediumOpenAIAnthropic
So what: Capability gains to date came on a small compute base; consensus models extrapolating linearly may underestimate next year.
“They're not very good at modeling acceleration. But here we have this double exponential.” ▶ 15:14
Huang frames OpenAI's Navier-Stokes result as building on decades of prior human work; he expects a rapid succession of breakthroughs in fields at a similar 'precipice' where exhaustive AI search-space exploration is the missing ingredient, which requires lots of compute.
prediction🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: mediumOpenAINavier-Stokes
So what: Scientific discovery is a new, compute-hungry demand vector.
“If it didn't require that, then somebody would have found it earlier” ▶ 17:03
Gerstner's estimate is that the Navier-Stokes solution used about 10,000 agents and roughly $4 million of compute, and that the cost of such an effort should fall 5-10x over the next few years.
data_point🗣 Brad Gerstnerconviction: mediumNavier-Stokes
So what: Falling cost per breakthrough expands the set of problems worth attacking with agent swarms.
“I think our math says probably cost about $4 million of compute to solve” ▶ 18:55
Huang expects the cost of intelligence to fall quickly, like Moore's law, citing GPT-1 going from roughly $10-20 million to train to about $100 in ten years, while usage rose about a million times and model sizes grew to 3-30 trillion parameters: a Jevons-style insatiable demand.
causal🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: highGPT-1Moore's law
So what: Price declines in tokens are bullish for total compute spend, not bearish.
“we want intelligence cost to decrease by a factor of two or 20 every single year” ▶ 18:02
Huang rejects the idea that AI will give people more leisure; he argues intelligence unlocks more opportunities and ambitious people will pursue them, echoing Gerstner's point that Keynes was right on 8x growth but wrong on a 15-hour work week.
contrarian🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: mediumKeynes
So what: Demand for AI-augmented work expands rather than substitutes labor hours.
“people say we're going to have more time. I I completely disagree.” ▶ 20:45
Physical AI and the three-computer stack 6
Huang says Nvidia did $10 billion in physical AI revenue in the first six months of this year, after $10 billion last year (its first $10B year) and $5 billion the year before, suggesting roughly $20 billion this year and that his $100 billion market call was understated.
data_point🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: highNvidia
So what: Physical AI is roughly doubling annually and is a material second growth leg.
“we're already the first six months of this year we did $10 billion in physical AI” ▶ 24:49
Huang defines physical AI broadly across the electromagnetic spectrum: photon-counting CT, ultrasound, imaging radar, next-gen 5G sensing networks, geospatial satellites, visual inspection, robotics, self-driving and robotic surgical rooms.
framework🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: mediumNvidia
So what: The addressable market spans healthcare, telecom, logistics and manufacturing, not just humanoids.
Huang predicts every car, logistics, retail and telecom company becomes a technology/AI company, naming FedEx, Walmart, AT&T and Verizon, with telecom shifting back from a services industry to a technology industry.
prediction🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: mediumFedExWalmartAT&TVerizonTesla
So what: Incumbent industrials that deploy AI are Nvidia's next customer base.
“Every shipping and rece, logistics company is going to be a technology company.” ▶ 27:48
Nvidia's physical AI framework requires three computers and three complete software stacks: a training system to create the brain, a simulation digital twin (Omniverse) where the robot learns, and the edge computer inside the robot, car or instrument; Huang claims Nvidia is the only company building all three.
framework🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: highNvidiaOmniverse
So what: Full-stack position lets Nvidia sell into each vertical regardless of which layer a customer builds.
“it takes three robots three computers &gt;&gt; and three complete stacks” ▶ 30:13
Huang expects AI labs to build digital twins of the internet so agents can learn to use it without being deployed onto the live internet, mirroring Omniverse for the physical world.
prediction🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: exploratoryOmniverse
So what: Simulation environments for agent training could become a new compute and software category.
“they need to build a digital twin &gt;&gt; of the internet” ▶ 29:43
Huang says people may stop categorizing Nvidia as a chip company because it runs deep stacks in automotive, healthcare, telecom, logistics, life sciences and entertainment.
thesis🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: mediumNvidia
So what: Valuation framing may shift from cyclical semis to platform/infrastructure.
“one of these days you know people might not categorize us as a chip company anymore” ▶ 29:12
AI safety framework and policy 6
Huang argues perfect alignment is a forever problem, so safety must rely on engineering: zero-trust containment with minimal rights expanded as confidence grows, plus out-of-band monitoring that can escalate or shut a system down.
framework🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: highNvidia
So what: Agent security/containment tooling is a required layer for enterprise deployment.
“If we depend on perfect alignment in order to ensure safety, then we're never going to get there.” ▶ 33:38
Huang says monitoring must be out of band because an intelligent autonomous system behaves differently when it knows it is being watched; Nvidia announced Open Shell for this, with over 100 companies joining.
data_point🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: mediumNvidiaOpen Shell
So what: Nvidia is extending into agent security software, adding to content per gigawatt.
“we we announced it recently called Open Shelf. We have, you know, over 100 companies now joining us” ▶ 35:41
Huang rejects the framing that capability and safety are orthogonal, comparing safety tech to a car's brakes and airbags; accelerating AI means accelerating safety and security technology too.
contrarian🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: high
So what: Safety spending is part of the AI capex/software stack rather than a drag on it.
“You can't separate capability, functionality from safety and security.” ▶ 37:46
Huang says AI only reached genuine usefulness about six months ago, so the industry's urgent imperative has now shifted to making broad deployment safe and secure, because without that the industry itself would not exist.
thesis🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: high
So what: Enterprise deployment, not model capability, is the next gating factor.
“there will be no AI technology industry if we don't build it safely and securely okay this is an existential issue for us” ▶ 41:50
Gerstner describes the U.S. super-intelligence security framework as a three-legged stool: CAISI/Commerce review of every frontier model, the White House Accord on Super Intelligence (internal/external audits, board oversight, liability), and an SI force led by Jay Clayton; he notes the U.S. pulled the Mythos model off the shelf under export-control authority.
data_point🗣 Brad Gerstnerconviction: mediumCAISIDepartment of CommerceWhite House Accord on Super IntelligenceJay ClaytonMythos
So what: Regulatory regime is already binding; frontier model releases face government review risk.
“we're clearly the only country on the planet that's actually sanctioned a model and pulled it off the shelf with Mythos earlier this year” ▶ 43:12
Huang favors fast, actionable regulation over arguing for perfection, and says the three-legged framework has real teeth; he urges critics in the Senate to learn the problem and existing solutions first.
thesis🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: medium
So what: Lowers odds of disruptive new legislation if the existing framework is seen as sufficient.
“if we if we uh argue about what is the what's perfect uh versus what's first” ▶ 44:31
2027 bottlenecks: land, power, shell and supply chain 8
Gerstner cites SemiAnalysis (Dylan) estimating 43 GW of new AI compute coming online next year, more than all AI compute existing today, and another ~40 GW the year after; Huang says demand is there 'and more'.
data_point🗣 Brad Gerstnerconviction: highSemiAnalysisNvidia
So what: Demand is not the constraint for 2027; physical buildout is.
“the demand is definitely there and more” ▶ 48:00
Huang expects land, power and shell (plus permitting, moratoriums, and scarce specialized construction trades) to be among the most important obstacles to next year's buildout.
prediction🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: highNvidia
So what: Power, land and data center construction owners hold pricing power in 2027.
“I do think that land powered shell is going to be a challenging part next year” ▶ 51:40
A co-host relays street estimates of about 50 GW of announced chips versus 35-40 GW of land/power/shell coming online, leaving uncertainty about what compute fits and when.
data_point🗣 co-host (Clark)conviction: medium
So what: A chip-to-shell gap could shift timing of deliveries and favor the most power-efficient vendor.
Huang argues power scarcity shifts buyers toward Nvidia because it generates the most tokens per watt, and customers must maximize revenue from limited power; as a result Nvidia's already high market share is increasing.
causal🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: highNvidia
So what: Energy constraints are a share tailwind for the efficiency leader rather than a headwind.
“not only are we a large company and our market share is high, our market share is actually increasing for these reasons” ▶ 53:26
Huang says Nvidia's resilience to local buildout problems comes from serving every CSP, NCP, OEM (Dell, HP, Lenovo) and region, so it can reroute supply when a state or community stalls, including to Europe, Africa, Central Asia, Southeast Asia, Latin America and the Middle East.
thesis🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: mediumDellHPLenovoNvidia
So what: U.S. permitting friction hurts U.S. buildout more than Nvidia revenue.
“our demand is so much greater than our supply at the moment we'll just adjust our supply chain accordingly” ▶ 50:21
Huang identifies substrates (larger packages causing warpage and thermal-coefficient issues) and silicon photonics as supply constraints, and says the gap between supply and a growing order book will be hard to close for a few years.
data_point🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: highNvidia
So what: Substrate and photonics suppliers are chokepoints with multi-year tightness.
“it's going to be hard to close that gap for a few years” ▶ 1:17:32
Nvidia recently announced 2 GW in Australia, which Huang says equates to about $80 billion for Nvidia, and he hopes to announce more land/power/shell partnerships in Europe.
data_point🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: mediumNvidiaAustralia
So what: Implies roughly $40B of Nvidia revenue per GW of deployment.
“2 gawatts to us is like $80 billion” ▶ 1:15:30
Huang says he published the forward revenue outlook because Nvidia was allocating far less than customers wanted from a 1-2 year forecast order book, and since then new orders arrive almost weekly and daily.
anecdote🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: highNvidia
So what: Guidance reflects supply allocation, not demand ceiling; upside skew.
“what I was allocating to all of our customers is a lot less than &gt;&gt; they wanted” ▶ 1:14:38
Does AI revenue justify capex 9
Gerstner frames the payback math: frontier and inference lab revenue is about $200 billion this year and needs to double to roughly $400 billion next year and $800 billion the year after to approach payback on trillions of capex; Claude Code drove a 10x increase in Anthropic's revenue this year.
data_point🗣 Brad Gerstnerconviction: mediumAnthropicClaude CodeOpenAI
So what: Lab revenue growth is the market's key watched variable for capex sustainability.
“we had this massive unlock this year with with Claude Code that led to a 10x increase in anthropics revenues” ▶ 1:01:24
Huang breaks Nvidia's business into roughly three segments: about a third classical accelerated computing (data processing, simulation, XGBoost fraud detection, recommenders) growing 30-40% a year, about a quarter to a third frontier labs (five to six, not two), and about a third AI-native startups renting compute via clouds.
framework🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: highNvidiaMetaGeminiAnthropicGrok
So what: Nvidia demand is far less concentrated on OpenAI/Anthropic than the market assumes.
“in the absence of any AI labs, Nvidia is not zero” ▶ 1:03:20
Huang says the market puts too much pressure on two labs; they will be hugely successful but need not reach anywhere near a trillion dollars of revenue to justify the buildout.
contrarian🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: highOpenAIAnthropic
So what: Selloffs in AI infrastructure on lab-revenue headlines (e.g. OpenAI $50B vs $70B chatter) may be overdone.
“they don't have to be anywhere near a trillion dollars in revenues to be successful” ▶ 1:12:28
Huang says about 80% of AI startups use open models, mostly to unlock new verticals rather than compete with frontier labs, often customizing Nemotron into domain super-intelligence with proprietary data, while closed labs pursue AGI.
data_point🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: mediumNemotronNvidia
So what: Open-model vertical AI is a large, diversified compute renter base.
“if you look at all these startup companies, 80% of them use open models” ▶ 1:06:14
Huang reiterates infrastructure buildout will reach $3-4 trillion per year by end of decade (closer to four), with roughly $1.5 trillion of that compute, up from about $0.5 trillion before AI.
prediction🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: highNvidia
So what: Sets the long-run TAM anchor for AI capex.
“infrastructure buildout will probably be somewhere between 3 to four trillion a year” ▶ 1:09:03
Huang argues that because Moore's law is over, doubling compute now means paying twice as much, so even without AI the semiconductor industry was on a path to become the world's largest industry.
causal🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: highMoore's law
So what: Structural reason compute spend scales with demand rather than deflating.
“if you want twice as many transistors now, doesn't matter how long you wait, you have to pay twice as much” ▶ 1:09:54
Huang notes general-purpose compute cost about $5 billion per gigawatt versus about $50 billion for Vera Rubin today.
data_point🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: mediumVera RubinNvidia
So what: Capex intensity per GW is 10x legacy, raising dollar TAM per unit of power.
“general purpose compute, $5 billion per gigawatt” ▶ 1:11:00
Huang says he would not be surprised if annual infrastructure building plus refresh eventually runs around $10 trillion, versus about $1 trillion per year globally in the retrieval-computing era.
prediction🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: exploratoryNvidia
So what: Bull-case framing that this is a permanent re-architecture of global compute.
“runs around 10 trillion dollars. I'm not even a little bit surprised” ▶ 1:33:29
Gerstner's supporting math: a 20% productivity gain over five years on about $50 trillion of knowledge-work GDP yields about $10 trillion of incremental activity by 2029-2030, of which spending half on intelligence capex seems reasonable.
framework🗣 Brad Gerstnerconviction: exploratory
So what: Top-down GDP framing used to sanity-check $4-5T annual AI spend.
Nvidia economics, moat and capital return 12
Huang says Nvidia's content per gigawatt has risen from about $18 billion to the $20s and now $40-plus billion as it added networking, CPUs, storage and security (Open Shell, Sentry), and expects it to keep rising.
data_point🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: highNvidiaOpen ShellSentry
So what: Revenue can grow faster than gigawatts deployed.
“uh I guess about you know 18 billion or so per gigawatt to 20 somewhat billion per gigawatt to now 40 plus per gigawatt” ▶ 54:05
Huang says Nvidia's share of world capex was single digits six years ago, is about 40% at the CSPs and about 80% outside CSPs (on-prem, neoclouds), each half of the business, both doubling annually, so its share of global capex is still growing.
data_point🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: highNvidia
So what: Wallet share expansion compounds with market growth.
“our percentage of capex is probably what 80%” ▶ 55:00
Huang says Nvidia is becoming one of the largest CPU companies and surely the fastest-growing one, after previously becoming the largest and fastest-growing networking company.
prediction🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: mediumNvidia
So what: CPU share gains are a threat to incumbent x86 vendors.
“We're now going to be one of the largest CPU companies, surely the fastest growing one.” ▶ 53:42
Huang's moat argument: at $50-100 billion order sizes systems cannot be benchmarked, so customers must order sight unseen (e.g. Vera Rubin Ultra orders soon) from a trusted vendor, analogous to why Nvidia gives 100% of its business to TSMC.
framework🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: highVera Rubin UltraTSMCNvidia
So what: Scale and trust create a widening moat versus custom-chip challengers.
“who can you place a $50 billion order on? &gt;&gt; Yeah. &gt;&gt; We're literally the only one.” ▶ 1:20:40
Huang argues specialization is the enemy of fungibility and durability; general-purpose CUDA hardware that runs any model, workload or customer anywhere translates into rentability, financeability and lowest TCO, the core case against custom ASICs.
framework🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: highCUDANvidia
So what: Key rebuttal to the custom silicon threat; supports residual value of Nvidia GPUs.
“So specialization is the enemy of fungibility.” ▶ 1:25:29
Huang says Nvidia still updates software for GPUs bought eight years ago via CUDA, making the hardware durable, which he cites as why it is the ideal payload for SpaceX's space data centers.
data_point🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: mediumCUDASpaceXNvidia
So what: Supports longer depreciation lives for Nvidia hardware.
“you pay us eight years ago and we're still updating your software” ▶ 1:00:14
Gerstner shows consensus for Nvidia shifted up 100-200% in a year (from flat/shrinking 2027 to 74% growth), yet the stock is only up about 20% and trades at 13-14x earnings; Huang attributes underappreciation of durability and growing share at scale.
data_point🗣 Brad Gerstnerconviction: highNvidiaAltimeter
So what: Estimate revisions have far outpaced multiple; implies market doubts durability.
“You're now trading at 13 to 14 times earnings depending upon who you're looking at.” ▶ 1:18:59
Huang outlines two new business models tied to the installed base: enterprise software licensing (roughly a couple thousand dollars per GPU per year, with ~20 million GPUs shipped a year and half going to enterprise) and revenue share with neoclouds Nvidia supports with offtake, software and financing.
thesis🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: highNvidia
So what: Recurring software and rev-share revenue could re-rate Nvidia toward an installed-base model.
“notice I just described two new growth vectors &gt;&gt; independent of the hardware sale of our company” ▶ 1:29:22
Nvidia spent over 50% of free cash flow on buybacks (60-some percent last quarter); Gerstner estimates it could buy back upwards of 10% of the company within two years, likening it to Apple's capital-return transition.
data_point🗣 Brad Gerstnerconviction: mediumNvidiaApple
So what: Capital return supports EPS and the 'growth value' framing.
“I think this last quarter we did 60 some odd.” ▶ 1:30:28
Huang says strategic investments as a share of cash generation will decline rapidly because OpenAI and Anthropic-type opportunities will not come around again and cash flow is growing fast.
prediction🗣 Jensen Huangconviction: mediumOpenAIAnthropicNvidia
So what: More free cash flow likely available for buybacks.
“open AI and anthropic isn't going to come around again that opportunity is not going to come around again” ▶ 1:31:31
Elon Musk's xAI/SpaceX moved from Nvidia's enterprise segment to CSPs; Gerstner says Musk is raising another $40 billion for perhaps over a million Vera Rubin and targets 10 GW by end of 2027, and Huang says Nvidia and SpaceX engineers are co-designing servers for data centers in space.
data_point🗣 Brad Gerstnerconviction: mediumElon MuskSpaceXVera RubinGrokNvidia
So what: Musk is now a hyperscale-class Nvidia customer and space compute is an emerging option.

What $450M Portfolio Manager Learned From Paul Tudor Jones · Andrew Perry78 min46 insights2026-10-14

Ex-Tudor PM Andrew Perry (Macro Pillars) explains his five-pillar macro framework centered on policy and non-traditional liquidity, especially Treasury refunding duration (e.g., Nov 1, 2023) and key policy dates as price levels, plus why the MOVE index matters more than the VIX. He details recent trades (short KOSPI/Taiwan, long corn/wheat/soy on the Middle East crisis), bearishness on crypto lagging key dates, and Tudor-style risk discipline (55-57% win rates, hard stops, drawdown limits).

Macro Pillars: liquidity-driven framework 9
Andrew Perry's Macro Pillars framework has five parts: (1) government policy/geopolitics, (2) non-traditional liquidity (Treasury quarterly refunding duration, fiscal policy), (3) traditional monetary policy, (4-5) key dates/technicals and price response, with execution as the final step.
framework🗣 Andrew Perryconviction: highMacro Pillars
So what: A checklist for layering policy, liquidity, dates and price before sizing a macro trade.
“pillar two is what I call non-traditional liquidity and that's the quant that's the quantitative refunding they do every quarter” ▶ 35:19
Perry built the pillars after being run over short on March 20, 2020, when the Fed bought corporate credit and global central banks cut to zero, turning a COVID lockdown into a market 'casino' he had failed to anticipate.
anecdote🗣 Andrew Perryconviction: highFederal ReserveCOVID
So what: Short theses based on economic damage fail when policy floods the system with liquidity; watch what policymakers do, not say.
“Now I was still trading short and got completely run over.” ▶ 34:14
Perry argues that during COVID, deficits globally of 15-20%, zero rates and Fed corporate credit buying made markets rip despite lockdowns, because liquidity overwhelmed fundamentals.
causal🗣 Andrew Perryconviction: highFederal ReserveCOVID
So what: Liquidity regimes can trump the economic narrative; model policy flows explicitly.
“It turned into a casino because they because they deployed so much liquidity.” ▶ 34:41
When the US Treasury borrows shorter (more bills) in its quarterly refunding it adds liquidity, and when it borrows longer (more coupons) it drains liquidity, making refunding announcements pivotal market dates.
causal🗣 Andrew Perryconviction: highUS TreasuryQuarterly Refunding Announcement
So what: Track the QRA bill/coupon mix as a liquidity signal for risk assets.
“If they borrow shorter, there's more liquidity. They borrow longer, there's less liquidity.” ▶ 0:51
On November 1, 2023, Treasury Secretary Janet Yellen shifted issuance from the usual ~80% coupons/20% bills mix toward more short-end borrowing; markets bottomed that day, yields fell and stocks ripped, and Perry says no asset went lower afterward.
data_point🗣 Andrew Perryconviction: highJanet YellenUS Treasury10-year Treasury
So what: The Nov 2023 QRA is a template for how duration shifts can mark a cross-asset bottom.
“I defy any of your listeners to look at November 1, 2023 and see if you can find any asset that went lower. It didn't.” ▶ 36:11
Perry says markets tend to be weak heading into quarterly refunding announcements, an observation drawn from price evidence rather than guesswork.
data_point🗣 Andrew Perryconviction: mediumQuarterly Refunding Announcement
So what: Consider reducing risk or waiting before QRA dates, then trading the confirmation.
“we've found leading into QAS the market tends to be weak” ▶ 1:01:18
The most recent refunding announcement (around August 5, under Treasury Secretary Bessent) kept duration short and coupon issuance unchanged, which Perry reads as liquidity-supportive, though the market has not yet clearly confirmed it.
data_point🗣 Andrew Perryconviction: mediumScott BessentUS Treasury
So what: Short-duration issuance supports risk, but needs price confirmation before acting.
“he kept duration short and when he keeps duration short there's more liquidity” ▶ 57:48

“Not often do not often do the pillars align.” ▶ 42:23

Perry explains that QE adds bank reserves which allow banks to lend more, whereas fiscal policy hands money directly to people, and the 2020 fiscal policies created the inflation.
causal🗣 Andrew Perryconviction: mediumQE

thesis🗣 Andrew Perryconviction: highKevin WarshFederal Reserve

Risk management and sizing 9
Perry says top traders win only about 55-57% of the time; the edge comes from cutting losses quickly and letting winners run larger, and anyone claiming 80-90% win rates is lying.
data_point🗣 Andrew Perryconviction: highPaul Tudor JonesRoger Federer
So what: Optimize payoff asymmetry, not hit rate.
“the good ones run around 55 [clears throat] to 57” ▶ 13:09
Perry uses a 1-5 risk-unit scale: 3-5 units when he is 'seeing it' and 1 unit when he isn't; uniform small sizing regardless of conviction won't make money.
framework🗣 Andrew Perryconviction: high
So what: Vary size with conviction to make the 57% win rate pay.
“When you're seeing it, you got to you got to three to five units.” ▶ 27:25
Perry insists on always trading with hard stop losses, calling inability to take losses the biggest mistake of retail and learning traders.
framework🗣 Andrew Perryconviction: high
So what: Non-negotiable: every position has a stop.

Perry says risk/reward must match the time frame: buying the S&P at 7,000 targeting 10,000 with a 10-point stop is nonsense; long-term targets need long-term thinking and wider risk.
framework🗣 Andrew Perryconviction: highS&P 500
So what: Align stops, targets and holding period.
“There's no point looking for longer long longerterm moves with shorter term thinking.” ▶ 25:50
At Tudor, new PMs got about $50 million with a 10% drawdown limit and a two-year contract; hitting the drawdown meant automatic termination, and failing to deploy capital could also cost your job. One colleague grew from $50 million to over $1.5 billion in ten years.
data_point🗣 Andrew Perryconviction: highTudor Investment Corporation
So what: Institutional benchmark: hard drawdown limits plus a mandate to use risk.

When hiring, Tudor examined a candidate's daily return data, specifically the worst day and how they performed after it, rejecting those who 'go into their shell' in drawdown.
framework🗣 Andrew Perryconviction: mediumTudor Investment Corporation

Perry warns that more capital is often allocated right after a good run, which is the worst timing, and that managing $500M-$1B+ is far harder because liquidity becomes a constraint.
causal🗣 Andrew Perryconviction: mediumTudor Investment Corporation
So what: Beware scaling up after hot streaks; size capacity shrinks with liquidity.
“you get allocated more capital just after you've had a good run” ▶ 28:07
Perry, who has watched four major crises (Nasdaq bust, GFC, 2012 Europe, 2020), says leverage without risk management is what got people 'body bagged' each time.

framework🗣 Andrew Perryconviction: mediumKOSPI
