Latent Space 1 episode(s) · 44 insights

From Wet Lab to RL Env: 10,000 Drug-Discovery Campaigns in a Weekend — Ian Quigley, Leash Bio110 min44 insights2026-10-14
▶ Watch on YouTube
Ian Quigley (CEO, Leash Bio) explains how ~10-person Leash uses DNA-encoded library screens (about 2,000 protein constructs, ~5,000 screens, 6.5M+ molecules each, plus engineered pocket mutants) to build physically grounded small-molecule binding data, after showing public ChEMBL data mostly encodes chemist 'sociology' and that no Kaggle team beat chance on novel chemistry. Leash now turns its database into an instant-feedback RL environment for language-model agents (10,000+ SAR campaigns in a weekend) and is pursuing spinouts from selective hits on ~700 targets.
Clever Hans: public chem data learns sociology · 6Kaggle BELKA and out-of-distribution failure · 6DEL engine: scale, cost, and data strategy · 10Engineered activity cliffs and mutant screens · 8Language-model RL environment for med-chem · 6Pipeline, business model and bottlenecks · 8
Clever Hans: public chem data learns sociology 6
A classifier trained on ChEMBL molecules from 1,815 prolific chemists (30+ papers, 600+ molecules) identified the creating chemist from molecular structure alone with 60% top-5 accuracy.
data_point🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: highLeash BioChEMBL
So what: Public med-chem datasets carry strong author-style fingerprints that models can exploit instead of learning chemistry.
“It's 1,815 chemists. And then we trained a classifier to predict a chemist from looking at the molecule alone. And we got a top five accuracy of [music] 60%.” ▶ 0:42
Representing molecules only as a ~1,800-element vector of 'probability of creator' predicts ChEMBL activity as well as using chemical structure, and combining the two adds little, implying activity models on public data mostly learn sociology rather than physics.
contrarian🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: highChEMBLLeash Bio
So what: Models trained on public binding data (a field standard for PhDs and startups) may have little real chemical understanding; diligence any AI-chem company relying on it.
“We really think that it's learning a lot of sociology. It's not learning ground truth.” ▶ 1:12
Chemist fingerprints arise because medicinal chemists make many unrecorded choices about what not to make (cost, synthesizability, superstition about favorite building blocks), and those omissions imprint on what does get made and tested.
causal🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: highmedicinal chemists
So what: Absence of counterfactual/negative data in pharma records is a structural flaw, not just noise.
“not making that choice is a fingerprint that that is going to land on what does get made” ▶ 50:27
Even companies generating their own internal data are likely exposed to the authorship bias, because their multiple chemists have distinctive styles and apply them to different projects.
thesis🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: mediummedicinal chemists
So what: Proprietary pharma data moats may be less valuable for ML than assumed unless experimentally designed to remove selection bias.
“if you are generating your own data, then you are probably victim to this because your organization probably has multiple chemists who have their own distinctive chemical styles” ▶ 49:16
In public datasets, molecular weight alone is among the strongest predictors of binding (e.g., tying for first in CASP16), because chemists keep adding groups to molecules that already work, an artifact of the data rather than chemistry.
data_point🗣 Co-host (Brandon Anderson)conviction: mediumCASP16
So what: Benchmarks on public data can be gamed by trivial baselines; claimed AI wins need artifact-controlled evals.
“all you have to do is sort by molecular weight. That's like one of the single [laughter] single biggest predictors you can possibly have.” ▶ 52:20
For its Kaggle competition Leash released about 4 billion physical small-molecule/protein interactions, measured by one person in Quigley's basement, versus just under 3 million in BindingDB, then the largest aggregated public binding database.
data_point🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: highBindingDBKaggleLeash Bio
So what: Pooled DEL screening can generate data three orders of magnitude beyond public corpora at tiny cost.
“we released a data set that was about four billion physical interactions and it was all measured by a single person in the basement of my house.” ▶ 27:51
Kaggle BELKA and out-of-distribution failure 6
In Leash's Kaggle competition (~2,000 teams, 3 well-studied protein targets, a 130M-molecule triazine-core library from AlphaMa sponsor), no team beat chance on the hardest held-out split using a totally different Leash-designed library with no shared hub or building blocks.
data_point🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: highKaggleLeash BioVictor Schlapov
So what: Current ML small-molecule binding prediction does not generalize to novel chemistry; a large white space remains.
“And then when you go to the most challenging eval, no one did better than chance.” ▶ 31:42
Three of the top five Kaggle finishers were variants of the noisy tutorial notebook, and the winner (Victor Schlapov) also performed no better than chance on the hardest split.
data_point🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: highKaggleVictor Schlapov
So what: Sophisticated modeling gave little edge; data, not architecture, is the bottleneck.
“three of the top five uh were the noisy tutorial notebook.” ▶ 32:01
Leash gave roughly a 5% chance that co-folding approaches plus its 1000x-larger dataset would solve the problem and obviate the company; it did not happen, which the team read as validation to scale data collection.
anecdote🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: mediumLeash Bio
So what: Founders treated a public contest as a cheap existential test of their moat before committing a decade.
“we gave it about a 5% chance that recent advancements and kind of like coalfolding approaches would mean that it would obiate our company entirely.” ▶ 32:51
Simple SMILES strings beat fancier molecular representations (3D graphs, spherical convolutional nets) in the Kaggle competition, though not by a resounding margin.
data_point🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: mediumSMILESKaggle
So what: Supports a bitter-lesson, data-first strategy over bespoke chemistry-informed architectures.
“the thing that we were doing which are just basic smiles kind of beat everything else.” ▶ 33:53
Leash describes itself as 'extremely bitter lesson pilled': if a scalable measurement regime yields good signal, the right move is to collect massive data, and the contest's difficulty was a signal to 'pour gas' on data generation.
framework🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: highLeash Bio
So what: Strategy is a data-scaling bet; value accrues to whoever owns the scalable, clean measurement engine.
“like we we are extremely bitter lesson pill over here.” ▶ 34:56
All Kaggle competition data, including evals, was released openly on Polaris (a nonprofit run by Recursion) to challenge skeptics who argued only weak teams competed; no one has yet performed well on it.
data_point🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: mediumPolarisRecursionLeash Bio
So what: Open benchmark remains unsolved; a clear yardstick for any claimed generalizable binding model.
“it is all on Polaris which is like a nonprofit group that was run by recursion.” ▶ 39:15
DEL engine: scale, cost, and data strategy 10
Arrayed one-compound-per-well libraries max out in the low single-digit millions, while the largest DNA-encoded library ever built contains 40 trillion molecules.
data_point🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: highDNA-encoded libraries
So what: DELs offer orders-of-magnitude more measurement throughput than traditional HTS for small-molecule binding.
“With Dell's, the largest one that has been produced um is 40 trillion molecules.” ▶ 4:06
Leash's 'party line' is that there are only two scalable readouts in life-science ML: taking pictures and DNA sequencing (itself a form of pictures); Quigley argues proteomics is not yet at scale.
contrarian🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: highRecursionDNA sequencing
So what: Bio-ML platforms built on imaging or sequencing readouts have structural scalability advantages over proteomics-based ones.
“There's really only really only one scalable technology in town and that technology is taking pictures. Sequencing DNA is another way of taking pictures.” ▶ 5:52
Sequencing cost declines plateaued for a while but new entrants are driving prices down again, and Leash explicitly plans its data-collection scale around that trajectory.
causal🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: mediumDNA sequencing
So what: Cheaper sequencing directly lowers Leash-style DEL screening costs, a tailwind for sequencing-readout biotechs.
“now a bunch of new companies have started up and they're driving it down again.” ▶ 6:54
Leash has developed and screened about 2,000 protein constructs across roughly 5,000 screens, each against at least 6.5 million molecules, likely the most mutant screening of anyone; a one-off CRO DEL screen would cost about $100K each.
data_point🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: highLeash BioCRO
So what: Implies Leash produced data worth hundreds of millions at CRO prices on modest capital, a big cost advantage.
“we have uh developed and screened about 2,000 different protein constructs. um a total of probably about 5,000 screens in the history of the company across at least 6 and a half million molecules each.” ▶ 1:39:55
Leash has only about 10 employees, has not raised much money, and screens up to 100 protein constructs per week on big weeks.
data_point🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: highLeash Bio
So what: Extreme capital efficiency; small team running a high-throughput platform.
“if you're going to be out screening a 100 protein constructs a week, which is what we do on big weeks” ▶ 1:05:53
Leash chose to better characterize smaller libraries across many proteins rather than use ever-larger libraries against few targets, since a 100M-compound library helped less than deeper characterization under a fixed budget.
framework🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: highLeash Bio
So what: Data diversity across the protein axis matters more than raw library size for generalizable models.
“this 100 million compound library that didn't help as much to us as better characterizing a smaller one.” ▶ 36:33
Traditional DEL users are traumatized because they screen one hard target after months of protein prep, push for giant libraries, and never characterize library quirks; screening each library against hundreds of targets reveals manufacturing artifacts and even protein production problems.
causal🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: highDNA-encoded librariesCRO
So what: Leash's edge comes from usage pattern (many targets per library), not just DEL chemistry itself.
“every one of our chemical libraries gets screened against many hundreds of targets.” ▶ 17:35
Leash uses 32 or 48 distinct DNA barcodes per molecule to detect proteins that bind DNA rather than the small molecule, and has discovered proteins not previously described as DNA binders.
data_point🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: mediumLeash Bio
So what: Built-in controls make DEL data more trustworthy as ML training data.
“we have 32 or 48 different barcodes all encoding this one small molecule.” ▶ 10:42
When off-DNA resynthesized, about 90% of easy-target DEL hits and about 50% of hard-target hits still work in functional assays, and replicate screens months apart return the same hits in roughly the same order.
data_point🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: highLeash Bio
So what: Quantifies DEL hit-translation attrition and reproducibility.
“for things that are easy in our hands about 90% of them work for things that are hard is about 50” ▶ 13:01
Arrayed compound library labels are typically wrong 20 to 30% of the time when audited, a mislabeling problem Quigley says is comparable to or worse than in DELs.
contrarian🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: mediumDNA-encoded libraries
So what: Undercuts the common assumption that arrayed HTS data is cleaner than DEL data.
“they're typically off by 20 to 30%. It's an enormous mislabeling problem.” ▶ 27:14
Engineered activity cliffs and mutant screens 8
Hermes, Leash's simple model (ESM protein embedding plus a SMILES next-token chemistry embedding joined with cross-attention, predicting binary binding), held steady across increasingly strict building-block-cluster splits while XGBoost degraded steadily.
data_point🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: highHermesESMXGBoostLeash Bio
So what: Rigorous splits separate genuine generalization from memorization; XGBoost is a mandatory baseline.
“we compared that to the Hermes performance, which was pretty steady across all these different regimes.” ▶ 45:52
Trained exclusively on internal Leash data, Hermes was benchmarked on ~13 million public tests across ~500 protein targets and performed roughly on par with Boltz on the binding task.
data_point🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: mediumHermesBoltzLeash Bio
So what: Internal-only DEL data generalizes to external chemical space at roughly SOTA level, supporting the data moat claim.
“I think it's like 13 million or something across like many it's like 500 protein target.” ▶ 57:10
On BRD4, Boltz was perfect on a molecule that also appeared in PDB crystal structures it had almost certainly trained on, while Hermes did poorly, which Leash reads as evidence of memorization in structural models.
causal🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: mediumBoltzBRD4PDB
So what: Co-folding benchmark results may be inflated by train/test leakage.
“Boltz was perfect at it. And it's because we suspect that Boltz had seen this protein and this individual molecule in a crystal structure already” ▶ 58:54
State-of-the-art co-folding models, when a protein pocket is digitally broken (filled in or removed via sequence changes), still place the small molecule where the pocket used to be, suggesting they memorize rather than understand physics.
contrarian🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: mediumco-folding models
So what: Structure-based AI drug design has a physics-understanding gap that experimental negative data could fill.
“it will shove the small molecule in where the pocket used to be.” ▶ 1:02:24
Hermes attention maps showed the model focusing on short, non-conserved amino-acid stretches away from binding pockets, effectively memorizing a 'nickname' for each protein; adding engineered mutant screens made attention spread across residues evenly.
causal🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: highHermes
So what: Interpretability can diagnose memorization, and targeted data (mutants) can fix it.
“Hermes was probably coming up with a nickname for the protein.” ▶ 1:13:06
Leash physically mutates binding-pocket residues and rescreens, combined with competition assays using known ligands, to generate true negatives and map which molecular groups interact with which residues; the combinatorial library yields multiple independent chemical series showing the same shift.
framework🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: highLeash BioDNA-encoded libraries
So what: Creates causal, physically grounded negative data that public structural datasets lack.
“we thought one way to go get around this is to experimentally engineer mutations in binding regions of these proteins and then rescreening.” ▶ 1:07:49
Leash aspires to screen every human protein and then non-human orthologs, partly to predict species-specific behavior such as whether to run a mouse versus rat model.
prediction🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: exploratoryLeash Bio
So what: Broad proteome coverage could extend into off-target toxicity and preclinical species selection.
“It is it is not at all an exaggeration to say that it is our aspiration to have screened every protein” ▶ 25:12
Quigley's real moat against a better-funded lab out-scaling Leash is process culture: years of catching cheating modes, batch effects and metadata tracking from Recursion and Leash, e.g., using lumacaftor-like library compounds as a flag for misfolded protein batches.
thesis🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: mediumLeash BioRecursionlumacaftor
So what: Moat is tacit operational know-how, not capital; replication takes time rather than money.
“if we get a bunch of lumac heads that protein probably didn't fall fold right.” ▶ 1:46:04
Language-model RL environment for med-chem 6
Leash is training open-source language models (Qwen, GLM) to describe molecules in plain English and run hypothesis-driven SAR campaigns against its existing screening database as an instant-feedback RL environment, much like a coding sandbox.
framework🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: highQwenGLMLeash Bio
So what: Turns wet-lab data into a scalable, physically grounded RL environment, a rare asset for reasoning-model training.
“It will get back the answer instantly like it's a coding sandbox and then it will update its hypothesis about the binding.” ▶ 1:18:35
A medicinal chemist sees maybe a couple dozen to 50 SAR campaigns in a career; Leash generated over 10,000 such campaigns in one weekend without trying hard.
data_point🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: highLeash Bio
So what: Massive scaling of med-chem experience data for training reasoning agents.
“you might see a couple dozen of these, maybe 50 of these in your career. >> And so right now, you know, last weekend we pulled out >> over 10,000 of them.”
Given 10 binders and 10 non-binders and a budget of 100 molecule queries, Leash's agents find the best binders out of 6.5 million molecules.
data_point🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: mediumLeash Bio
So what: Early evidence of agentic molecular optimization efficiency in silico.
“they'll get the best binders out of six and a half million in that budget.” ▶ 1:22:18
Natural language acts as a regularizer that resists memorization better than scaling Hermes-style models, which would eventually memorize the mutants too; Leash knows of no other physically grounded RL loop this scalable and fast.
contrarian🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: mediumHermesLeash Bio
So what: Positions DEL data as differentiated RL infrastructure versus robotic lab-in-the-loop efforts that are slower and resource limited.
“we are unaware of other problems where you have a reinforcement learning loop that is physically grounded that is this scalable this fast.” ▶ 1:20:11
Because Leash measures all molecules across all proteins, its data includes counterfactual choices a human chemist would not have made, which agents need to learn without inheriting chemist sociology.
causal🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: mediumLeash Bio
So what: Unbiased exhaustive measurement is the key differentiator over pharma historical data.
“It's got to have the counterfactuals. It's got to make mistakes” ▶ 1:23:06
If models master chemical reasoning, Leash expects to enter agriculture, veterinary science and materials science without screening in those domains, and eventually no longer need screening at all.
prediction🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: exploratoryLeash Bio
So what: Option value beyond pharma; answer to the 'why do you need ML if you have data' business-model critique.
“we can run off and go work in agriculture like immediately right you could go work in veterinary science you go work off material science” ▶ 1:38:36
Pipeline, business model and bottlenecks 8
Leash went from DEL hits to good PK/PD in mice in 6 months with few med-chem cycles on an inflammation compound, with cytokine biomarkers in mouse blood moving as expected; libraries were designed with drug-likeness and ADMET scores in the optimization function.
data_point🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: mediumLeash Bio
So what: Early in vivo validation that DEL-derived, ML-designed chemistry can be drug-like.
“we've gone directly from uh from chemical library hits to good PKPD in a mouse in 6 months” ▶ 1:28:47
Leash has selective-looking starting chemical matter for around 700 targets and is talking with partners to spin up new entities to advance some assets.
data_point🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: mediumLeash Bio
So what: Business model includes spinouts/partnerships monetizing a large proprietary hit portfolio.
“right now we have starting material that looks pretty selective to us for uh I think like 700 targets” ▶ 1:32:30
A key commercial target is oral small molecules replacing injectable antibodies for cytokine-driven inflammatory indications, avoiding infusion and cold-chain logistics; Leash says it has selective material for many cytokines.
thesis🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: mediumLeash Bio
So what: Oral alternatives to biologics in inflammation are a large value-capture opportunity and a threat to antibody incumbents.
“going after a small molecule therapeutic that you could take as a pill instead of an infusion, one that doesn't require uh, like cold chain” ▶ 1:34:00
Quigley argues protein-drug ML (antibody companies such as Isomorphic, Chai, Nabla) works because protein data is ample, but comparable capabilities do not yet exist for small molecules, which is Leash's founding thesis.
thesis🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: highIsomorphicChaiLeash Bio
So what: Small-molecule AI is the less-crowded, data-starved frontier relative to biologics AI.
“Um our thesis is that uh those capabilities are really not here for small molecule approaches.” ▶ 23:13
Early discovery is a 'lamppost problem': it is where scalable measurement is possible today (no million-mouse or million-phase-1 experiments), so ML will move up layers of biological abstraction over time.
framework🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: mediumLeash Bio
So what: Explains why AI-bio value is concentrated in early discovery despite it being a small share of drug costs.
“It is a little bit of a lampost problem where the things where we can make enough scalable measurements in the life sciences are these early problems.” ▶ 1:35:13
Newer DNA-compatible chemistries over the past ~four years give carbon-carbon and carbon-nitrogen bonds beyond digestible amide links, so Quigley worries more about limited commercial building-block diversity and custom synthesis than about DEL linkage stability.
causal🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: mediumDNA-encoded libraries
So what: Building-block supply and custom synthesis are the scaling constraint for DEL chemical space.
“I'm more worried about building block diversity uh than there sources for that.” ▶ 1:28:30
Leash can reliably rank relative potency from DEL reads but cannot yet translate reads into Kd values, and is exploring SPR-based potency ladders.
data_point🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: mediumLeash BioSPR

“I think translating it into KDS is something that we're actively working on” ▶ 1:42:49
Quigley's by-fiat bottleneck wish is massively parallel, high-quality small-molecule purification (beyond HPLC), enabling clean orthogonal validation of DEL measurements at scale.
thesis🗣 Ian Quigley (Leash Bio CEO)conviction: mediumHPLC

“being able to like clean up stuff at a massive scale would be really really nice” ▶ 1:48:38

Can Open Models Keep Up With OpenAI and Anthropic? | Nathan Lambert59 min43 insights2026-10-14
▶ Watch on YouTube
Nathan Lambert (launching the nonprofit open lab Trillium) argues open-weight models can stay near the frontier: Chinese labs equal the US on talent but lack compute, distillation is a smaller lever than assumed in today's RL-heavy post-training, and data/environment diffusion makes fast-following easy. He expects tiered markets (closed models with top margins, near-frontier open models), doubts fast takeoff given exponential compute costs, and worries more about societal unpreparedness like cybersecurity.
Capital, org charts and frontier lab cultures · 6Chinese open labs: compute-starved commercialization · 5Distillation: smaller lever than assumed · 7Open vs closed economics and tiered markets · 9Data and RL environment industry · 6Trillium, open RSI and pretraining accessibility · 7Takeoff skepticism and real risks · 3
Capital, org charts and frontier lab cultures 6
Nathan Lambert frames model progress as a function of three forms of capital (compute, data, talent), with the binding constraint being a lab's ability to organize them via its org chart.
framework🗣 Nathan Lambertconviction: highOpenAIAnthropicGoogle
So what: Evaluate labs on organizational execution, not just capital raised.
“When I think about model progress, it comes down to like different forms of capital that you put into them. And that is like compute, data, and talent.” ▶ 2:06
Lambert says Meta did a complete bottom-up reset of its AI org and is now on a positive trajectory again.
thesis🗣 Nathan Lambertconviction: mediumMeta
So what: Meta's model efforts may be re-rating upward after reorganization.
“Like company like Meta is like totally reset and they're like on a positive trajectory again.” ▶ 2:37
Lambert's default advice for working at the AI frontier is Anthropic or OpenAI; Meta, Google and SpaceX AI may be there but are harder to judge from outside.
thesis🗣 Nathan Lambertconviction: highAnthropicOpenAIMetaGoogleSpaceX AI
So what: Talent flows and perceived frontier status still concentrate in two labs.
“if somebody wants to work at the frontier of AI, I mostly tell them to work at Anthropic and OpenAI, which is the easiest bet, I think.” ▶ 2:59
OpenAI's culture excels at paradigm-shifting breakthroughs like the reasoning-model/inference-scaling plot, while Anthropic's long focus on what models can do in coding was rewarded by the Claude Code explosion.
framework🗣 Nathan Lambertconviction: highOpenAIAnthropicClaude Code
So what: Distinct lab cultures produce distinct product wins; coding was Anthropic's payoff.

data_point🗣 Nathan Lambertconviction: mediumOpenAIAnthropic

Distillation: smaller lever than assumed 7
Distillation effectively converts money into compute-equivalent: knowing the outputs you want from a strong model is equivalent to having the compute to generate them.
framework🗣 Nathan Lambertconviction: high
So what: Distillation is a compute-substitute for compute-starved labs.
“outputs from a language model are a function of compute and if you know the outputs you want it's the same as having compute to generate with it with the model.” ▶ 9:47
Lambert argues distillation mattered more in the SFT/RLHF era but maps poorly onto today's RL-heavy, environment-based post-training, so he believes it is not a huge effect in current post-training, albeit with big error bars.
contrarian🗣 Nathan Lambertconviction: medium
So what: Banning distillation would not erase Chinese progress.
“previous post-training was much more reliant on the supervised fine-tuning” ▶ 0:26
Evidence cited: fully open post-training researchers remain stuck using an outdated Qwen model because they cannot get distillation teachers right, and Lambert doubts Claude reasoning traces would have much improved Olmo.
data_point🗣 Nathan Lambertconviction: mediumQwenOlmoClaude
So what: Reasoning-trace distillation is technically harder than the narrative suggests.
“all the open model builders are stuck using like a really outdated Qwen model because you can't figure out how to get the teacher right.” ▶ 11:04

data_point🗣 Nathan Lambertconviction: mediumClaudeKimi

“even cases where Kimi was serving Claude in their products to users.” ▶ 12:25
As long as frontier labs expose reasoning tokens via APIs, distillation cannot be stopped; restricting models to products and enterprise agreements would make it much harder, but Lambert opposes KYC mandates and government action over ToS violations.
thesis🗣 Nathan Lambertconviction: high
So what: Expect labs to self-secure via product gating rather than policy.
“so long as they offer these APIs there's going to be no way to stop that type of distillation.” ▶ 14:05

thesis🗣 Nathan Lambertconviction: mediumCongress

Lambert predicts tiers: best closed models command the best margins, while near-frontier models one step behind will be open, serving less profitable markets such as private enterprise data.
prediction🗣 Nathan Lambertconviction: high

causal🗣 Nathan Lambertconviction: highOpenAIAnthropic

framework🗣 Nathan Lambertconviction: high

The Great Simplification · Nate Hagens 1 episode(s) · 43 insights

We're Investing in a Future That Won't Exist with Indy Johar88 min43 insights2026-10-14
▶ Watch on YouTube
Indy Johar (Dark Matter Labs) tells Nate Hagens that AI-driven disruption of humans as labor and cognition will couple with climate, food and geopolitical risks into a 2028-29 crisis apex, and that historical theories of alpha built on Holocene-era predictability are already obsolete. He urges sovereigns and family offices, as self-interest rather than charity, to hedge the 'optionality collapse' of capital (he cites ~80% odds of a collapse in money's claim capacity over 30 years) by funding continuity and system-level interventions like city cooling, aquifer stabilization and bioregional restoration.
2028-29 labor/cognition shock and conflict path · 5AI capital as a civilizational stack · 9End of predictability and the death of alpha · 12Optionality collapse and how capital should hedge · 9System value, continuity and quasi-infrastructure · 5Barriers and theory of change · 3
2028-29 labor/cognition shock and conflict path 5
Indy Johar expects AI to disrupt humans both as units of labor and as units of cognition, with these crises reaching an apex around 2028-2029, earlier than he previously expected climate breakdown to bite.
prediction🗣 Indy Joharconviction: highAI
So what: Positions a near-term (2028-29) macro/social discontinuity window that investors should stress-test portfolios against.
≈ “I think we're looking at a 2028 2029 disruption where these crisis come into apex” paraphrase
Johar argues that as AI pushes people outside the economy, nations will start removing people to preserve national economics, creating a pathway first to internal conflict and then to war.
causal🗣 Indy Joharconviction: high
So what: Labor displacement is framed as a geopolitical and civil-unrest risk, not just a productivity story.
≈ “as nations start to remove people from themselves to preserve the kind of economics of the nation and in that process set up the pathway to war” paraphrase
Johar believes a significant war would be non-retrievable because mass trauma combined with a collapsing unit cost of destruction (e.g., access to CRISPR technology) enables harm at unprecedented scale.
thesis🗣 Indy Joharconviction: mediumCRISPR
So what: Tail risk of conflict is treated as potentially irreversible, which argues for paying to prevent it rather than hedging after the fact.
Johar contends that a labor-cognition collapse would destroy the distribution of income through the economy, causing demand to collapse quickly and simultaneously, so commodity demand forecasts built on linear consumer growth may be wrong.
contrarian🗣 Indy Joharconviction: mediumcopper
So what: Challenges consensus electrification-driven commodity bull cases that assume consumer demand stays intact.
≈ “if we have a major labor cognition collapse, we don't necessarily have the value of the labor distribution in the economy. So the demand collapses simultaneously very quickly” paraphrase
Challenging a private equity copper-demand thesis, Johar suggested war could become the source of copper demand rather than traditional consumer supply chains.
contrarian🗣 Indy Joharconviction: exploratorycopperprivate equity
So what: Even if commodity demand materializes, its drivers and beneficiaries could differ sharply from base-case models.
≈ “The war may create the copper demand, but it won't be the traditional consumer supply.” paraphrase
AI capital as a civilizational stack 9
Johar argues that the capital flowing into AI is justified publicly by labor disruption but is actually hunting recursive intelligence/AGI, which he evocatively calls 'touching the mirror of God'.
thesis🗣 Indy Joharconviction: mediumAIAGI
So what: AI capex may be priced on a non-commodity theory of value, making traditional valuation frameworks a poor guide to how much capital keeps flowing.
Johar compares current AI capital allocation to medieval capital allocated to alchemy, which ended up giving birth to chemistry, implying money is moving on a completely different theory of value than the commodity theory.
framework🗣 Indy Joharconviction: mediumAI
So what: Speculative AI spending could yield real but unexpected breakthroughs, so judging it purely on near-term returns misreads it.
≈ “the allocation of capital that went into alchemy gave birth to chemistry” paraphrase
Johar notes his understanding that multiple large language models are converging on the same higher-dimensional geometric manifold, which he interprets as a kind of higher-dimensional cognition space.
data_point🗣 Indy Joharconviction: exploratorylarge language modelstransformers
So what: Suggests convergence across frontier models, implying limited differentiation among them at the representational level.
Reverse-engineering Eric Schmidt's 'solve AGI then climate' claim, Johar lays out a stack: whoever gets recursive intelligence gains strategic freedom, values it on labor disruption, and drives an energy demand super cycle.
framework🗣 Indy Joharconviction: mediumEric SchmidtAGI
So what: Gives investors a map of the dominant 'civilizational stack' thesis that is driving big-tech and energy capital allocation.
≈ “whoever gets recursive intelligence will have the pathway of holding freedom” paraphrase
Johar says AI compute demand reverses a period of flat demographics and falling energy demand, triggering an energy super cycle that brings investment into energy innovation that has been missing for perhaps 20 years.
causal🗣 Indy Joharconviction: highAI
So what: AI-driven energy capex is a structural, multi-year theme for energy innovation.
≈ “What this does is drive a super cycle of energy demand which brings unprecedented investment into energy innovation that has been missing for perhaps 20 years.” paraphrase
In the stack logic Johar describes, near-zero energy costs would unlock desalination, carbon sequestration and vertical farming because each is roughly a direct function of energy, letting the stack-holder profit from global volatility while creating local hyperstability.
framework🗣 Indy Joharconviction: exploratorydesalinationcarbon sequestrationvertical farming
So what: Cheap energy is the linchpin variable: if it materializes, water, carbon removal and controlled-environment food become investable at scale.
≈ “carbon sequestation is a onetoone function of energy as is desalination” paraphrase
Johar does not endorse the AGI stack thesis, arguing that LLM intelligence is a partial mirror filtered through language abstraction and is less advanced than actual human consciousness, so a civilization built solely on it becomes problematic.
contrarian🗣 Indy Joharconviction: mediumlarge language modelsAI
So what: Signals limits to AI-maximalist narratives and a case for capital in counter-positions.
Johar warns that in rooms where capital is allocated there is little comprehension of the scale of the civilizational play, and that no single actor is designing it; it emerges from entangled actors.
thesis🗣 Indy Joharconviction: medium
So what: Investors are underweighting how coordinated-in-effect the AI/energy power build-out is and lack counter-strategies.
Johar proposes that 'Frontier Labs' on the future of being human and on consciousness are needed as counterweights to AI labs and as a north star for the transition.
thesis🗣 Indy Joharconviction: exploratoryFrontier Labs
So what: Points to a nascent, underfunded research category adjacent to AI.
End of predictability and the death of alpha 12
Johar argues that civilization, institutions, prices and most accrued financial wealth were built on and exploited the unusual climatic, energy and demographic predictability of the last 12,000 years, which is now ending.
thesis🗣 Indy Joharconviction: highHolocene
So what: Return expectations calibrated on stable history may be structurally overstated.
≈ “I would argue that most of our financial acral of wealth has been built on that predictability and the exploitation of that predictability.” paraphrase
Johar claims conventional theories of alpha have already ceased to exist and markets simply have not realized it yet.
contrarian🗣 Indy Joharconviction: high
So what: Strategies relying on historical relationships may fail abruptly.
≈ “all of our theories of alpha, they don't exist. You know, I think they've already gone. We just don't know it.” paraphrase
Johar says the projections he sees are linear and treat systems separately, whereas risks like nutrition, labor/cognition collapse, geopolitical resource conflict and climate volatility are now coupling and feeding back on each other.
framework🗣 Indy Joharconviction: high
So what: Correlation-based risk models will underestimate joint tail events; institutions should model coupled risks.
≈ “The volatility is not only coupling but it's also feedbacking on itself.” paraphrase
Johar says a capital allocator who does not see these coupled systemic risks may not be fulfilling fiduciary duty.
thesis🗣 Indy Joharconviction: high
So what: Frames systemic-risk modeling as a fiduciary obligation, a potential governance and litigation angle.
≈ “if you're not seeing this I don't know how fidiciary duty is being fulfilled” paraphrase
Johar cites the view (attributed to Sir David King) that enough carbon is already emitted that the Arctic must be stabilized or the world faces about 4.5 m of sea level rise; London alone has 3.8 million homes averaging GBP 668,000, roughly GBP 2.52 trillion of residential value at stake.
data_point🗣 Indy Joharconviction: mediumSir David KingLondonArctic
So what: Coastal real-estate exposure is a trillion-scale impairment risk, not a minor adjustment.
≈ “there are 3.8 million residential apartments and homes. average price is 668,000 pounds. That's 2.52 trillion just in London alone.” paraphrase
In a bioregion in Spain where Dark Matter Labs works, monoculture charcoal forests dried soils and killed mycelium, hunting removed fauna, clouds now pass over, and depopulation removed stewards, producing a self-drying regime shift toward desertification and abandonment.
anecdote🗣 Indy Joharconviction: highSpainDark Matter Labs
So what: Illustrates how regressive system coupling can drive land assets to collapse in value.
Johar reports that land in that Spanish bioregion is worth about 1,500 per hectare while total fire and other liabilities are about 20,000 per hectare, meaning the land economics are fundamentally collapsing.
data_point🗣 Indy Joharconviction: highSpain
So what: Liabilities can exceed asset value by over 10x in climate-stressed regions, a warning for land and insurance exposure.
≈ “land value is now 1,500 per hectare in that environment the total liabilities of fire and other things are 20,000” paraphrase
Johar predicts a major food-system event likely between end of 2026 and 2027, driven by a super El Nino effect colliding with disruption around the straits (transcribed as 'straits of emmer', likely the Strait of Hormuz), creating nutrition risk.
prediction🗣 Indy Joharconviction: mediumEl NinoStrait of Hormuz
So what: Near-term food and fertilizer price spike risk worth positioning for.
≈ “we're about to see a major event now probably between end of 2026 and 27 uh driven by super alino effect and the straits of emmer” paraphrase
Johar argues food price whiplash will be amplified by financial systems, drive inequality up sharply, make the social contract fragile and weaponizable, and accelerate incentives for war in a resource-constrained environment.
causal🗣 Indy Joharconviction: high
So what: Price shocks propagate into political instability, a second-order risk for sovereign and EM exposure.
Nate Hagens frames roughly 100 trillion in global GDP against upwards of 400 trillion in total debt and claims, arguing there is not enough energy, materials and ecological health for all of it to be spent as holders expect.
thesis🗣 Nate Hagensconviction: high
So what: Implies widespread real-value impairment of financial claims.
≈ “So, we're 100 trillion odd in GDP. There's uh upwards of 400 trillion in total debt and claims.” paraphrase
Hagens says he has a strong feeling of some famine and caloric shortfall by 2028, putting a 30-40% chance on a major disruption in the not-too-distant future while allowing it may come after 2040.
prediction🗣 Nate Hagensconviction: mediumUkraineRussiaIranIsrael
So what: A host-level probability estimate for food-system disruption that can be used as a scenario input.
≈ “I think there's a 30 or 40% chance.” paraphrase
Johar argues market fundamentals across energy, demographics, M2 supply versus GDP, AI infrastructure and climate breakdown effects are all off, so relying on existing market norms to make claims on the future is 'crazy'.
contrarian🗣 Indy Joharconviction: highM2
So what: Encourages LPs to demand an explicit worldview from managers rather than relying on market norms.
≈ “all the energy demographics, M2 supply versus GDP, AI infrastructures, climate breakdown effects, you put any of them down, the fundamentals of those norms are off.” paraphrase
Optionality collapse and how capital should hedge 9
Johar says his models indicate roughly an 80% chance of a collapse in the claim capacity of money over the next 30 years, driven largely by biophysical realities and volatility.
prediction🗣 Indy Joharconviction: medium
So what: If even partly right, long-duration financial claims are materially overvalued.
≈ “there will be a collapse of the claim capacity of money over the next 30 years.” paraphrase
Johar's pitch to wealth holders: check the models; if they look realistic, allocate some share (even 0.1%, 1%, 2% or 10%) to managing the existential collapse of optionality to capital itself, framed like fire insurance.
framework🗣 Indy Joharconviction: high
So what: A concrete, non-moral allocation framing that institutions can test within existing mandates.
≈ “even if it's 0.1%, 1% 2% 10%. It is the management of these existential collapse of optionality to capital itself.” paraphrase
Johar stresses this is self-interest, not charity: holders of wealth must understand the optionality collapse their claims on the future face.
thesis🗣 Indy Joharconviction: high
So what: Reframes climate/systemic allocation from ESG ethics to capital preservation.
≈ “I'm not inviting you to be kind. I'm just inviting you to be smart right now.” paraphrase
Johar argues sovereigns and intergenerational family offices have a strategic advantage because their capital is unencumbered, whereas 'coded capital' in pre-structured funds locked 5-20 years in advance cannot react to real risks.
thesis🗣 Indy Joharconviction: highfamily officessovereign wealth funds
So what: Long-horizon unconstrained capital is best placed to front-run systemic repricing; closed-end funds are structurally slow.
Johar proposes two linked buckets: 'existential capital' that hedges the existential risk to capital itself, and 'participatory capital' that takes options in the futures that hedge creates, together forming a conduit to recycle the ~400 trillion of claims.
framework🗣 Indy Joharconviction: medium
So what: Suggests a structure for a fund that pairs hedging with venture-style optionality.
Hagens coins a 'civilizational Sharpe ratio': if future returns average 3% IRR with swings like down 50% and up 60%, that is a terrible outcome, so reducing systemic volatility raises the risk-adjusted value of all other investments; Johar agrees.
framework🗣 Nate Hagensconviction: medium
So what: Systemic-stability investments can be justified as raising portfolio-wide risk-adjusted returns.
Hagens notes the game-theory problem that investing in systemic stability benefits everyone in a competitive, dog-eat-dog market, and Johar responds that derivative and layered capital products could price underpriced continuity value and change those dynamics.
framework🗣 Nate Hagensconviction: exploratoryderivatives
So what: Potential product-innovation space: derivatives that monetize continuity of utilities and supply.
Johar says Dark Matter Labs is rooting capital aware of the optionality collapse into roughly 17 on-the-ground options, including cooling cities, stabilizing aquifers, atmospheric river governance and zero-material-growth cities, and is working in seven bioregions.
data_point🗣 Indy Joharconviction: highDark Matter Labs
So what: Identifies specific investable intervention categories being structured.
Johar cautions investors to keep his approach to a small part of their portfolio and discount it, adding that pooling capital is a whole-life commitment he would only make when certain.
anecdote🗣 Indy Joharconviction: medium
So what: Honest sizing guidance: treat as a hedge/satellite, not core.
≈ “make sure we're only a small part of your portfolio discount us” paraphrase
System value, continuity and quasi-infrastructure 5
Johar argues capital markets should allocate to quasi-certain necessities such as stabilizing the Arctic or cooling cities like Madrid by 7.5-8.5 degrees, where the economic asset is the population-scale effect from multiple interventions and beneficiaries rather than individual products.
thesis🗣 Indy Joharconviction: mediumMadridArctic
So what: Points to a new asset class of outcome-based 'quasi-infrastructure' financed on the demand side.
≈ “the economic asset is not individual economic products and services but is the effect of cooling Madrid by 7.5°” paraphrase
Johar says the value of continuity, of water, energy, nutrition, critical minerals and even labor, is massively underpriced, and will become more valuable than capital efficiency as fewer places can stabilize supply.
contrarian🗣 Indy Joharconviction: high
So what: Favors assets and strategies that guarantee resilience and reliability over lean efficiency.
≈ “The value of stability I think is massively underpriced in this in the world.” paraphrase
Johar argues that as volatility rises, the only way to hold assets well will be a systems worldview, investing in a city's vitality rather than extracting land value, since a Manhattan building moved to Nova Scotia would be worth nearly nothing.
framework🗣 Indy Joharconviction: mediumManhattanNova Scotia
So what: Real-asset returns increasingly depend on investing in surrounding system health.
Johar argues the economics of resilience sit in total asset theory: losing a city's nutrition supply would cause a total loss in residential asset value, so asset owners have a reason to finance supply continuity.
causal🗣 Indy Joharconviction: medium
So what: Real-estate owners and lenders have a direct economic case to fund food/water resilience.
Johar says every LP should ask any fund for its worldview and why its logic is pertinent in an age of volatility, and that this interrogation alone could transform capital structures.
thesis🗣 Indy Joharconviction: medium
So what: Actionable due-diligence question for allocators.
≈ “any LP should be asking any fund is give me your world worldview.” paraphrase
Barriers and theory of change 3
Johar observes that capital acts in the 'space of the possible' (incremental fixes) rather than the 'space of the necessary' (first-principles redesign, e.g., doubling a city's housing without large material or energy footprint increases).
framework🗣 Indy Joharconviction: medium
So what: Distinguishes incremental ESG from transformational investment theses.
Johar finds the main barriers among wealth holders are a lack of first-principles thinking, residual belief in a 'billion-human future'/bunker strategy, and 'good capital' that is too apologetic to exercise agency.
thesis🗣 Indy Joharconviction: medium
So what: Explains why systemic-risk capital remains scarce despite awareness.
Johar expects crises to be the Overton window shifts, and says the key question is whether actors can move fast enough with enough diffusibility in those moments to prevent large-scale violence.
prediction🗣 Indy Joharconviction: medium
So what: Prepared capital and playbooks gain outsized leverage in crisis windows.
≈ “moments of crisis will be our overton window shift landscape moment” paraphrase

Adam Taggart | Thoughtful Money 1 episode(s) · 43 insights

When A.I. Spending Starts Slowing, Start Worrying About Recession | Wolf Richter67 min43 insights2026-10-14
▶ Watch on YouTube
Wolf Richter (wolfstreet.com) argues the US economy is running hot (Q2 nominal GDP +8.5%, ~6.4% inflation) as a deliberate White House/Fed strategy to erode the ~$40T federal debt, sees no near-term recession, and names an AI capex slowdown plus a major AI-stock sell-off as the likely future trigger. He rejects the K-shaped consumer narrative with credit data, locates affordability pain in housing and health insurance, and has exited stocks entirely in favor of TIPS and Treasuries while avoiding corporate/AI bonds and gold.
Run-it-hot economy and debt strategy · 8AI capex as the recession trigger · 11AI revenue model and deflationary pricing · 5Consumer health vs. K-shaped narrative · 9Housing and structural affordability · 4Positioning: out of stocks, into TIPS and Treasuries · 6
Run-it-hot economy and debt strategy 8
Wolf Richter says the US economy is running hot: Q2 nominal GDP grew at an 8.5% annualized rate with roughly 6.4% GDP-price inflation.
data_point🗣 Wolf Richterconviction: highUS GDPinflation
So what: Nominal growth and inflation, not weakness, are the dominant macro regime per Richter.
“nominal GDP without inflation, so that's the economic growth in current dollars was 8.5%” ▶ 5:04
Richter argues the White House has chosen, and the Fed is going along with, a policy of high nominal growth and 3-5% inflation as the practical way to manage the ~$40 trillion federal debt, since no Congress will raise taxes or cut spending enough.
thesis🗣 Wolf Richterconviction: highWhite HouseFederal Reservefederal debt
So what: Expect persistently above-target inflation to be tolerated by policymakers.
“the way to do this is with higher nominal economic growth and uh much higher uh inflation” ▶ 7:56
Richter says the Fed still talks about a 2% inflation target but is effectively on the bandwagon of letting the economy run hot.
contrarian🗣 Wolf Richterconviction: mediumFederal Reserve
So what: Fed rhetoric about 2% should be discounted versus actual tolerance of higher inflation.
“the Fed's still talking about 2% inflation target, but they're really on the bandwagon, too” ▶ 5:38
Richter predicts inflation will stay elevated in roughly the current range, making inflation (not growth) the main economic problem.
prediction🗣 Wolf Richterconviction: highinflation
So what: Position for sustained inflation rather than disinflation.
“We're going to have uh inflation. Uh we already have inflation. It's quite a bit of inflation. It's going to stay up there in that range.” ▶ 5:50
Because federal debt grew about 8% while nominal GDP grew 8.5% in Q2, the debt-to-GDP ratio falls, and since tax revenues track nominal GDP absent policy changes, interest expense becomes easier to cover.
causal🗣 Wolf Richterconviction: highfederal debttax revenuesUS GDP
So what: Nominal growth outpacing debt growth is the key metric for fiscal sustainability.
“when the economy outgrows the debt in current dollars, it it it yeah, it lowers the debt to GDP ratio, but it also GDP triggers taxes” ▶ 9:46
Richter warns the run-hot strategy requires a fine balance: inflation of 20-30% would be catastrophic and would make investors unwilling to lend to the government.
framework🗣 Wolf Richterconviction: mediumTreasuriesinflation
So what: Watch for inflation breaking out of the 3-5% band as the failure mode for bondholders.
“if you have 20 or 25 or 30% inflation, that is a that is a catastrophic economic event” ▶ 10:59
Richter says a recession would be magnified in this regime because falling nominal growth would compound normal recession problems with the debt problem, so policymakers are trying to avoid recession at all costs.
causal🗣 Wolf Richterconviction: highfederal debtrecession
So what: Expect aggressive policy response to any slowdown, with higher fiscal stakes.
“if you have a recession and that thing goes down, you know, then we have a huge problem. It's magnified.” ▶ 11:50
Richter lists the drivers of the hot economy: roughly $1 trillion of AI corporate and investor spending (much raised overseas), ~$2 trillion annual government deficits, full employment, rising wages, and wealthy stock-invested retirees spending.
data_point🗣 Wolf Richterconviction: highAIgovernment deficitsconsumers
So what: Multiple simultaneous spending engines support nominal growth near term.
“we've got the AI spending boom that's throwing maybe a trillion dollars in corporate money and investor money at the economy” ▶ 4:28
AI capex as the recession trigger 11
Richter says a decline in AI investment spending, combined with a major sell-off in AI stocks, is the leading candidate trigger for the next US recession.
thesis🗣 Wolf Richterconviction: highAIAI stocksrecession
So what: Track AI capex trends as the primary recession leading indicator.
“When that starts declining uh that's when I start seeing a recession on the horizon.” ▶ 0:05
Richter is not calling for a recession in the next couple of quarters; he sees no collapse in stocks or bonds and consumers remain employed.
prediction🗣 Wolf Richterconviction: highrecessionstock marketbond market
So what: Near-term macro base case is continued expansion.
“I don't see I don't see any recession on the horizon.”
Using the dot-com bust as a template, Richter notes the recession began about a year after the March 2000 sell-off started, because it took a large decline to destroy corporate confidence and trigger layoffs, cuts and project cancellations.
framework🗣 Wolf Richterconviction: highdot-com bustrecession
So what: After an AI-stock peak, expect roughly a one-year lag before recession.
“it took 1 year during the dot-com bust from the beginning of the sell-off in March 2000, a year later the recession began” ▶ 13:51
Richter says a 20% stock decline is not enough to cause a recession; it takes a much larger crash to eviscerate corporate willingness to spend.
framework🗣 Wolf Richterconviction: mediumstock marketdot-com bust
So what: Ordinary corrections should not be read as recession signals.
“it's not a 20% decline, it takes a lot more and it takes uh a big stock market decline to to eviscerate corporate confidence in spending money” ▶ 13:25
Richter expects a slowdown in AI spending would likely trigger the correction in AI stocks, with individual names potentially falling 80-90%.
prediction🗣 Wolf Richterconviction: mediumAI stocks
So what: Spending data may lead stock prices; concentrated AI holdings carry crash risk.
“you can have these stocks go down by 80 or 90% and when that happens in your portfolio, you think, "Oh my god."” ▶ 14:49
Richter says he has been wrong for a long time about how far the AI boom would go, explaining it by a constant global flow of hundreds of billions in debt, equity and IPO money into AI centers.
anecdote🗣 Wolf Richterconviction: mediumAIIPOsSan Francisco
So what: Capital inflows, not fundamentals, are sustaining the boom; watch the flow.
“Well, I've been wrong about this stuff long enough. Uh I didn't think it would go this far. It keeps on going.” ▶ 16:21
Richter judges an AI bust has a good chance of being worse than the dot-com bust because the current boom is shoving trillions of real money into the economy.
prediction🗣 Wolf Richterconviction: mediumAIdot-com bust
So what: Downside scenario severity may exceed 2000-2002.
“I think this is kind of a a good chance of being worse than the dot-com bust.” ▶ 28:37
Richter expects an AI bust to hit concentrated tech hubs hard, with negative regional GDP heavy enough to drag national GDP and employment growth negative, while places like Tulsa may barely notice outside portfolios.
prediction🗣 Wolf Richterconviction: mediumTulsaAIGDP
So what: Regional exposure matters: tech-hub real estate and employment most vulnerable.
“we may have negative GDP for for other places that is so negative and so heavily negative that it will drag down the national averages” ▶ 27:51
Richter notes the data-center build-out spreads impact broadly: durable goods orders for power equipment such as gas turbines and on-site generators have skyrocketed, along with construction and power-line work that would stop when the boom ends.
data_point🗣 Wolf Richterconviction: highdata centersgas turbinesdurable goods orders
So what: Industrial and power-equipment suppliers are second-order AI-bust casualties.
“We have seen uh durable goods orders for that kind of equipment skyrocket.” ▶ 31:33
Adam Taggart notes AI-related companies are close to half of S&P 500 market cap and cites a Wall Street Journal chart showing AI investment as a share of GDP eclipsing prior tech revolutions including railroads.
data_point🗣 Adam Taggart (host)conviction: mediumS&P 500Wall Street Journalrailroads
So what: Index concentration means an AI bust would not be contained like dot-com.
“AI is is these companies are like almost half the S&P market value cap”
Richter compares the AI build-out to the dot-com era's telecom/fiber-optic infrastructure wave (e.g., Lucent Technologies, broadband), which also involved massive global physical spending.

AI revenue model and deflationary pricing 5
Richter says the biggest problem of the AI bubble is an uncertain revenue model: consumers spend a fixed ~96-97% of income and cannot suddenly spend trillions more on AI.
thesis🗣 Wolf Richterconviction: highAIconsumers
So what: AI capex returns depend on revenue that may not materialize.
“one of the the biggest problems this this bubble has is that the revenue model for AI is completely uncertain” ▶ 21:31
Richter says companies are already curtailing AI spending and shifting to free models, producing super-deflationary, race-to-the-bottom pricing.
contrarian🗣 Wolf Richterconviction: mediumAIfree models
So what: AI model pricing power is weak; bearish for AI monetization assumptions.
“it seems like the price the the the price models that are emerging are super deflationary” ▶ 22:45
Richter's own consumer AI spend is limited to a few extra dollars per month from Google Workspace bundling Gemini, illustrating thin consumer monetization.
anecdote🗣 Wolf Richterconviction: lowGoogle WorkspaceGemini
So what: Bundling, not standalone subscriptions, is how consumers pay for AI.
“I've got a uh uh an increase from from uh Google Workspace because now they include uh Gemini in it” ▶ 22:21
Richter concludes there likely isn't enough revenue out there to pay for all the AI investments, a risk that is widely known yet ignored.
thesis🗣 Wolf Richterconviction: highAI
So what: Known-but-ignored risk underpins eventual bust thesis.
“there just isn't enough revenue out there in all likelihood to pay for all these AI investments” ▶ 25:02
Richter says AI does raise productivity (he now makes his site's donation-ad graphics himself instead of hiring a designer; architects use it to cut workload), creating some labor substitution alongside AI-related hiring.
anecdote🗣 Wolf Richterconviction: mediumAIarchitectsgraphic designers
So what: Labor impact is mixed: substitution in creative/services, hiring elsewhere.
“I would have had to hire a graphic designer to do that. And that's where maybe where the the jobs impact comes in.” ▶ 23:48
Consumer health vs. K-shaped narrative 9
Richter rejects the K-shaped economy framing: the lower ~70% of households is not declining but rising more slowly than the top, and he told the New York Fed its own charts don't show a K.
contrarian🗣 Wolf Richterconviction: highK-shaped economyNew York Federal Reserve
So what: Bearish consumer-collapse narratives may keep being wrong.
“Your own charts show that one going up faster than the other but both going up.” ▶ 51:09
Richter cites median married-couple household income of $128,000 as a cleaner measure than overall median household income, which is depressed by singles, students and asset-rich retirees on Social Security.
data_point🗣 Wolf Richterconviction: highCensus Bureauhousehold income
So what: Headline median income understates the spending power of core households.
“The typical that the median married couple household income is $128,000” ▶ 36:04
Richter says corporate layoff announcements are largely a tactic to suppress wages and retention demands while companies such as Google and Amazon keep hiring.
contrarian🗣 Wolf Richterconviction: mediumGoogleAmazonlayoffs
So what: Don't read layoff headlines as labor-market weakness.
“this is a way for corporate America to bash down wages” ▶ 39:08
Richter says roughly $7 trillion a year flows through US credit cards, with ~$1.3 trillion in statement balances but only about $500 billion of interest-bearing debt, growing perhaps $20 billion a year.
data_point🗣 Wolf Richterconviction: highcredit cardsFederal Reserve
So what: Credit card debt is a payment flow, not evidence of consumer stress.
“It's half a trillion dollars. It's 500 billion, roughly.” ▶ 44:09
Prime credit card delinquency is about 0.8%, near historic lows; overall card delinquency is about 2.8%, versus 6.5% in the financial crisis and over 4% in the early 2000s.
data_point🗣 Wolf Richterconviction: highcredit cardsdelinquency rates
So what: Credit metrics are normalizing from pandemic lows, not signaling crisis.
“now we're at just below we're like 2.8%” ▶ 45:37
Richter argues rising delinquencies are driven by higher-income borrowers (e.g., an overextended young dentist), since low-income people often can't get credit cards; subprime is a revolving status people fall into and climb out of.
framework🗣 Wolf Richterconviction: mediumsubprimecredit cards
So what: Delinquency upticks reflect cyclical overextension, not low-income distress.
“the people that have the most debt are the high-income people, and they're the ones that move the needle” ▶ 48:00
Richter says subprime auto delinquencies spiked when lenders like Tricolor blew up (with some fraud), then came down; subprime is only ~15% of financed used-vehicle deals, and about half of used-vehicle deals are cash.

Thrive Capital healthcare partner Dr. Nworah Ayogu argues that if you are AGI-pilled you should be long healthcare: AI drug discovery (Isomorphic) will shift bottlenecks to trials and manufacturing, hardware-bio companies (Neuralink, Forell) unlock new treatments, and clinical AI (OpenEvidence, Cadence, ChatGPT) is spreading at unprecedented speed. AI drug discovery stack and bottleneck shifts 10
AI's impact on biotech will flow through the entire value chain (manufacturing, clinical development, commercialization), analogous to how AI lab demand flowed through compute, memory and energy supply chains.
thesis🗣 Nworah Ayogu (Thrive Capital)conviction: highThrive Capital
So what: Look for second-order biotech picks-and-shovels plays beyond discovery models (CDMOs, trial infrastructure, commercialization).
“We're going to see the same thing with, you know, biotech where it's not just drug discovery, it's going to be manufacturing” ▶ 2:50
AI drug discovery progresses through three prediction problems: designing a molecule that binds the target, predicting on-target biological effects, and predicting off-target side effects; binding prediction is largely improving while effect prediction is the next frontier.
framework🗣 Nworah Ayogu (Thrive Capital)conviction: highIsomorphic LabsChai
So what: Value in AI biotech is migrating from structure/binding models toward biology/effect prediction and the data that trains it.
“the first step of what molecule binds well is the thing we've actually gotten, you know, better and better and better at doing” ▶ 6:42
Unlike code or math, biology outputs are not cheaply verifiable; confirming effects requires cell, animal and human testing, so proprietary data generation for training effect-prediction models is a key bottleneck.
causal🗣 Nworah Ayogu (Thrive Capital)conviction: high
So what: Companies generating proprietary wet-lab/clinical data for model training hold a structural moat.
“with coding, with math, you know, those things are very verifiable. So I can I know whether my code compiles or not” ▶ 7:00
Isomorphic Labs' stated aspiration (per Max at the Biohub event) is to move drug development from 90%+ failure rates toward ~88-90% success rates, and from a 10-15 year process toward roughly four years.
prediction🗣 Nworah Ayogu (Thrive Capital)conviction: exploratoryIsomorphic LabsBiohub
So what: If even partially realized, pipeline economics and pharma R&D ROI would be re-rated dramatically.
“he said for like 88 to 90% success rates for a process right now that has 90 plus% failure rates. takes 10 to 15 years and costs billions of dollars” ▶ 8:46
Raising clinical success from ~5% to ~60% would radically change which programs drug developers are willing to pursue across the whole process.
causal🗣 Nworah Ayogu (Thrive Capital)conviction: medium
