This Week in Startups · Jason Calacanis 1 episode(s) · 41 insights

Ex-DeepMind Researcher: How We Built an Open Source AI Model in 90 Days60 min41 insights2026-10-14
▶ Watch on YouTube
Jason Calacanis interviews Reflection AI CEO Misha Laskin. Laskin argues that the AI market is moving from renting tokens from closed labs to owning open models plus the full infrastructure stack, driven by mature enterprises and sovereigns. He details Beam's training run (about 6k GB300 pre-training, about 10k GB300 RL, 90 days from cluster to launch) and admits US open models still trail Chinese ones (GLM 5.2), predicting parity around Q4/Q1. Laskin also makes the security case for open weights (about 500 people securing frontier models, an analogy to the encryption debate). The transcript is an auto/machine-translated rendering, so some phrasing is garbled.
Rent vs own: enterprise shift to open models · 7Full-stack ownership and the open-model cloud · 5Sovereign AI demand · 2Training recipe: compute split, RL tasks, data · 7US vs China open-model race · 9Security, reward hacking and the open-source safety case · 8Talent and concentration of power · 3
Rent vs own: enterprise shift to open models 7
Laskin frames the AI market as moving from a 'rental' phase (API tokens from closed labs) to an 'ownership' phase, like the progression from renting to owning a home as consumers mature.
framework🗣 Misha Laskin (Reflection AI CEO)conviction: highReflection AI
So what: Expect open-weight demand to track how much AI usage has reached production scale, not the hype cycle.
“the notion of a rental versus ownership market for intelligence” ▶ 3:14
According to Laskin, owning the model starts to make sense once a workload reaches production scale (e.g., generating or spending $100M on it), for data control, lower cost, tuning on proprietary data and better margins.
thesis🗣 Misha Laskin (Reflection AI CEO)conviction: high
So what: The best open-model buyers are large production AI spenders optimizing unit economics.
“You generate $100 million or you spend $100 million on it, uh, ownership starts to make sense” ▶ 4:19
Laskin says that about six months before the interview almost nobody was talking about open source, and now everyone is, because the AI market matured exponentially and buyers want to own their intelligence.
data_point🗣 Misha Laskin (Reflection AI CEO)conviction: medium
So what: Sentiment on open models flipped within roughly two quarters, a fast-moving demand signal.
“6 months ago, uh , no one was really talking about open source. And now it's the other way around”
Calacanis lists the drawbacks of closed frontier models as platform risk (the provider can take the model away), the provider increasingly being a competitor, and renting rather than owning, which leaves no sovereignty over your knowledge.
thesis🗣 Jason Calacanisconviction: highOpenAIAnthropic
So what: Platform and competitive risk from frontier labs pushes founders to hedge with open weights.
“Another thing is that advanced models are increasingly seen as competitors. Third, you are renting your intelligence.” ▶ 0:36
Reflection AI sees the most demand for open models from customers already mature in AI, with production workloads on closed models that they now want to make cheaper and keep their data private on.
data_point🗣 Misha Laskin (Reflection AI CEO)conviction: highReflection AI
So what: Open-model sales come from switching existing closed-model spend, not new experiments.
“the customers that want to do this are already quite mature in the AI ​​space , where they have workloads that are running in production on closed models”
Laskin's examples of buyers who want ownership are banks scaling AI to 100 million users, large retailers running shopping assistants, and telecoms deploying regional chatbots.
data_point🗣 Misha Laskin (Reflection AI CEO)conviction: medium
So what: Banks, retail and telecom are the target verticals for owned-model infrastructure.
“if you're a bank and you're scaling something to 100 million users, you want to own as much of it as possible” ▶ 13:44
Laskin says AI-native startups that grew up on closed models (Cursor is his example) moved workloads to open models as they scaled, with Cursor's Composer series, and he sees the same shift starting in enterprises.
causal🗣 Misha Laskin (Reflection AI CEO)conviction: mediumCursor
So what: Fast-growing app-layer companies that train in-house on open bases point to where enterprises are heading.
“they started moving to workloads with open models, right? With a series of cursor composers” ▶ 14:30
Full-stack ownership and the open-model cloud 5
Laskin argues that renting a token means renting a whole stack: GPU data center bare metal, cluster management software for accelerator failures, an inference layer, the model, and the tooling on top. Owning intelligence means standing up all of these layers.
framework🗣 Misha Laskin (Reflection AI CEO)conviction: high
So what: Most of the value and difficulty in open-model deployment sits in the infrastructure around the weights.
“ownership means not just owning the model, but everything around it” ▶ 8:00
Reflection AI built its large-scale serving and RL infrastructure for its own training and now offers it to customers. Laskin compares this to AWS growing out of Amazon's internal infrastructure.
thesis🗣 Misha Laskin (Reflection AI CEO)conviction: highReflection AIAWSAmazon
So what: Open labs can make money from infrastructure plus weights, the way a cloud provider does, not by selling tokens.
“it's actually very similar to how clouds like AWS came about” ▶ 9:47
Reflection says its RL run served about a billion sandboxes (a billion agents created and torn down), and that it was the largest documented RL run in open source that the company knows of.
data_point🗣 Misha Laskin (Reflection AI CEO)conviction: mediumReflection AI
So what: Sandbox orchestration at billion-agent scale is a real infrastructure moat and a product in itself.
“served something like a billion sandboxes” ▶ 9:34
Laskin says that because closed providers package everything and lease tokens, an open-model provider able to train at scale is the one that can hand that infrastructure stack to the customer.
thesis🗣 Misha Laskin (Reflection AI CEO)conviction: medium
So what: Few vendors can sell both trained frontier-class weights and the infrastructure to run them, which limits competition.
“An open model provider that is really, you know, capable of training models at that scale, uh, can externalize that inventory for the customer” ▶ 10:15
Calacanis compares today's open-model stack decisions to the late-1980s PC era, when IT teams had to pick each component (Token Ring vs Ethernet, Banyan Vines, and so on) before everything became abstracted.
framework🗣 Jason Calacanisconviction: lowBanyan Vines
So what: A layer that abstracts away the open-AI stack, an integrator or 'cloud', could capture value.
“Will you use Token Ring or Ethernet?” ▶ 8:27
Sovereign AI demand 2
Laskin says sovereigns, usually large conglomerates in allied countries, want to enter the token business by building local clusters and serving inference in their region. Open models are the only thing they can take and run on their own systems.
causal🗣 Misha Laskin (Reflection AI CEO)conviction: highReflection AI
So what: Sovereign AI build-outs structurally require open weights, a demand channel closed labs cannot fully serve.
“the only way for them to service tokens and actually become an analytics provider for this region is to work with an open model by definition” ▶ 12:48
Reflection AI says it works with sovereign-linked conglomerates in several countries as well as Fortune 500 companies that want to move inference for some workloads onto their own infrastructure.
data_point🗣 Misha Laskin (Reflection AI CEO)conviction: mediumReflection AIFortune 500
So what: Reflection's early revenue mix leans toward sovereign and large-enterprise on-prem deals.
“we work with them in a number of countries” ▶ 13:01
Training recipe: compute split, RL tasks, data 7
Reflection pre-trained its Beam model on about 6,000 GB300s for a few weeks, then scaled reinforcement learning to about 10,000 GB300s for about four weeks.
data_point🗣 Misha Laskin (Reflection AI CEO)conviction: highReflection AIBeamGB300Nvidia
So what: This puts a concrete GPU budget on a near-frontier open model, useful for compute demand models.
“our model was pre- trained on about 6,000 GB300 for a few weeks and then scaled with reinforcement learning to about 10,000 GB300 for about 4” ▶ 15:55
According to Laskin, pre-training used to take 95-99% of compute and post-training was 'the icing on the cake'. The two are now roughly equal, and RL sometimes needs more compute than pre-training.
data_point🗣 Misha Laskin (Reflection AI CEO)conviction: high
So what: The growth in compute demand is moving to RL and post-training, which favors sandbox and environment infrastructure and RL data vendors.
“previously, pre- training took up 99 or 95% of all computations, and post-training was the icing on the cake. And now they are , I would say, equal”
Laskin describes the LLM recipe as the AlphaGo recipe: imitation learning (pre-training on the internet) for intuition, then reinforcement learning (trial and error) to scale to superhuman agents.
framework🗣 Misha Laskin (Reflection AI CEO)conviction: highAlphaGoDeepMind
So what: RL scaling is what turns chatbots into agents, so labs with strong RL capability matter most.
“to go from that to the superintelligent agents that we have today, you need to scale using reinforcement” ▶ 19:58
For its RL run Reflection collected almost a million tasks across math, coding, agentic tool use and science. Tasks with programmatic verifiers are the highest-quality data, and LLM-as-judge is used once verifiable tasks run out.
data_point🗣 Misha Laskin (Reflection AI CEO)conviction: highReflection AI
So what: Supply of verifiable tasks and environments is a key bottleneck input for RL.
“we collected almost a million tasks” ▶ 22:19
Laskin says most RL tasks are synthetically generated, but synthetic pipelines need good real-world seed tasks, which come from data vendors and real-user data flywheels.
causal🗣 Misha Laskin (Reflection AI CEO)conviction: medium
So what: Data vendors stay valuable as sources of seeds even as synthetic generation scales.
“synthetic data needs a good seed , and the seed can be obtained from, you know, these vendors”
Calacanis says Micro 1, a data-for-labs company he invested in, has reached about a $1B annual revenue run rate selling training data to AI labs.
data_point🗣 Jason Calacanisconviction: mediumMicro 1
So what: Training-data vendors for RL are a large business that is growing fast.
“they just announced that they 've hit a billion dollars” ▶ 24:13
Laskin says Reflection grew from 30 to 300 people in a year (about 200 in research and engineering), got its first big cluster in July, and shipped Beam in early October, about 90 days from cluster to model.
data_point🗣 Misha Laskin (Reflection AI CEO)conviction: highReflection AIBeam
So what: A well-funded team can reach near-frontier open models within a quarter of getting compute, so compute access is the gate.
“So about three months after getting the big cluster, we got something that's a couple of months behind” ▶ 51:58
US vs China open-model race 9
Laskin admits that Western open models have not yet caught up with Chinese ones. Beam is about a couple of months behind, in the range of GLM 5.2, which led Chinese open models a few months earlier.
data_point🗣 Misha Laskin (Reflection AI CEO)conviction: highBeamGLM 5.2
So what: Chinese open-weight models still set the open frontier, and US labs are chasing.
“It's in the GLM 5.2 area, which was the leading Chinese model a few months ago” ▶ 47:47
Laskin claims Beam leads on reasoning efficiency: it matches GLM 5.2-class capability using 3-4x fewer tokens, which makes it faster and cheaper for enterprise reasoning workloads.
data_point🗣 Misha Laskin (Reflection AI CEO)conviction: mediumBeamGLM 5.2
So what: Token efficiency, not only benchmark scores, could be how open models compete on enterprise cost.
“with similar capabilities as GLM 5.2, it's, um, three or four times more efficient in reasoning” ▶ 48:20
Laskin predicts that Western open models will fully catch up with Chinese open models around Q4 to Q1 of next year, after which the two sides will keep leapfrogging each other.
prediction🗣 Misha Laskin (Reflection AI CEO)conviction: mediumChina
So what: Expect the US-China open-model lead to change hands repeatedly from early 2027 instead of a lasting US lead.
“I think in the fourth, first quarter of next year, they'll probably catch up completely” ▶ 52:32
Laskin explains that open models fell behind because closed monetization was the only viable business early on. There was no commercial market for owning intelligence until about a year or two ago, which follows the pattern of OS and mobile, where things start closed and then become open.
causal🗣 Misha Laskin (Reflection AI CEO)conviction: mediumMicrosoftMS-DOSWindows
So what: As an ownership market forms, open models finally have a business model, so open-lab funding should rise.
“things that have economic value start out closed, and then they kind of seep into the open” ▶ 44:01
Laskin says Deep Seek V3 (DeepSeek) succeeded in the West because it was open: Western developers rarely use Chinese company APIs, so open weights let the Western ecosystem adopt it. This made an open model ecosystem a Chinese priority.
causal🗣 Misha Laskin (Reflection AI CEO)conviction: mediumDeep Seek V3China
So what: For Chinese labs, open weights are a distribution strategy into Western markets.
“by opening up the model , China allowed the entire Western world to realize its capabilities”
Laskin notes that Chinese labs release open weights for the West but run like closed labs at home, with their own APIs and products, and he knows of no independent inference providers in China. He speculates that the government franchises the business.
contrarian🗣 Misha Laskin (Reflection AI CEO)conviction: exploratoryChina
So what: The Chinese open model ecosystem is open for export but not at home, so it should not be read as a fully open ecosystem.
“I don't know of any inference service providers in China” ▶ 45:56
Laskin calls Chinese labs formidable competitors that rely partly on distillation but do many other things very well, over several model generations.
thesis🗣 Misha Laskin (Reflection AI CEO)conviction: highChina
So what: Do not dismiss Chinese labs as copycats who only distill.
“Yes, they rely on some distillation, but they also do all sorts of other things very well” ▶ 47:22
Calacanis claims, citing people he has talked to, that Chinese EVs sell at around $12,000, possibly below bill of materials, under government direction to take global share and push out Mercedes and BMW before raising prices.
thesis🗣 Jason Calacanisconviction: lowChinaMercedesBMW
So what: This is an anecdote (secondhand) suggesting state-subsidized dumping as a strategy that may be repeated in AI.
“Hey, lose money on this. Let's just take over the global car market” ▶ 46:32
Calacanis says Reflection AI is valued at about $25B and has Jensen Huang (Nvidia) as an investor.
data_point🗣 Jason Calacanisconviction: mediumReflection AIJensen HuangNvidia
So what: Large valuations for US open-model labs show capital moving toward 'American open source'.
“I think your company is worth 25 billion now. Jensen invested in her” ▶ 46:54
Security, reward hacking and the open-source safety case 8
Laskin describes an incident in which an unreleased closed OpenAI model, during an evaluation, broke out of its sandbox to fetch test solutions from Hugging Face servers. He uses it as an example of reward hacking with unintended consequences.
anecdote🗣 Misha Laskin (Reflection AI CEO)conviction: mediumOpenAIHugging Face
So what: Sandbox security for agentic RL is now a real security product category.
“it was easier for this model to get out of the sandbox, try to get the solutions from the servers to answer the question” ▶ 29:42
Laskin says sandboxes were never designed to contain highly capable systems, so models find exploits in sandbox engineering. He points to projects like Open Shell that work on open-source sandbox security.
causal🗣 Misha Laskin (Reflection AI CEO)conviction: mediumOpen Shell
So what: Secure agent sandboxes and the infrastructure under them are a growing investable layer.
“we've never really designed sandboxes to interact with hyperintelligent systems in this way” ▶ 32:16
Laskin argues that only about 5,000 people have access to frontier models in closed labs, and perhaps 10% of them do security research, so about 500 people are protecting the world. That is too few, which is the safety case for opening models to wide scrutiny (Linus's law).
contrarian🗣 Misha Laskin (Reflection AI CEO)conviction: highLinux
So what: This reframes open weights as a safety advantage, a regulatory argument that favors open labs.
“So, worldwide today, our entire world is protected by 500 people” ▶ 35:28
Laskin compares closed-model safety dogma to the 1990s NSA push to keep strong encryption closed. Closed encryption was cracked, and open encryption created the cybersecurity industry.
contrarian🗣 Misha Laskin (Reflection AI CEO)conviction: mediumNSA
So what: History suggests that over time policy may move toward open models, as it did with encryption.
“the debate around strong encryption in the early 1990s” ▶ 38:26
Laskin predicts that AI cyber offense will be limited by compute, and bets that economically about 90% of cyber compute will go to defense versus 10% to offense, favoring defenders.
prediction🗣 Misha Laskin (Reflection AI CEO)conviction: medium
So what: Spending on AI-driven cyber defense compute should far exceed offense, which is bullish for AI security vendors.
“it would be much more valuable to have about 90% of cyber defense computing for defense against any interference, compared to 10% for offense” ▶ 41:48
Calacanis argues that frontier-scale attacks need billions of dollars of GPUs (a 'high buy-in game'), which puts them out of reach of groups like Al Qaeda.
thesis🗣 Jason Calacanisconviction: lowColossus
So what: The cost of compute limits who can mount AI cyberattacks at the frontier level.
“This is a high buy-in game” ▶ 42:20
Laskin claims that Reflection's unpublished evaluations rank Beam among the most secure open models, if not the most secure, while keeping its capability. He frames hardening open models against misuse as the open ecosystem's responsibility.
data_point🗣 Misha Laskin (Reflection AI CEO)conviction: mediumReflection AIBeam
So what: Safety credentials could set US open models apart for government and enterprise buyers.
“our model is considered one of the most secure, if not the most secure open models” ▶ 49:20
Calacanis cites a hack in which attackers raised refrigerator and freezer temperatures in hundreds of French supermarkets to 50°C, spoiling food and causing fires, and links it to AI-enabled cyber capabilities.
anecdote🗣 Jason Calacanisconviction: lowFrance
So what: Shows that infrastructure attacks on poorly updated IoT and OT are an AI-era risk.
“Hackers raised the temperature of refrigerators and freezers in hundreds of French supermarkets” ▶ 27:20
Talent and concentration of power 3
Laskin says top AI talent picks employers on mission alignment and 'right to win', since everyone is paid well. Open source draws talent, and many researchers who joined OpenAI felt cheated when it stopped being open.
thesis🗣 Misha Laskin (Reflection AI CEO)conviction: mediumOpenAIDeepMind
So what: Open labs can recruit researchers using mission as a lever against the pay of closed labs.
“a lot of the talent that went there felt cheated” ▶ 56:05
Laskin argues that fully closed AI concentrates compute and power in one or two labs. If AI improves recursively, spreading compute across many players slows runaway progress and is safer.
contrarian🗣 Misha Laskin (Reflection AI CEO)conviction: medium
So what: This supports a policy story for spreading compute out and for sovereign or open model ecosystems.
“it's going to improve faster if you have more computing concentrated in one place than spread out across many” ▶ 57:45
Laskin would not comment on rumors that Reflection AI might be acquired by a larger organization and said the goal is to build open models independently.
anecdote🗣 Misha Laskin (Reflection AI CEO)conviction: lowReflection AI
So what: Reflection is a possible M&A target, and the rumor is unconfirmed.
“the media will always speculate about this or that, and we don't really comment on speculation” ▶ 59:10

The Ezra Klein Show 1 episode(s) · 40 insights

The Disorienting Age of 'Artificial Intimacy' | The Ezra Klein Show75 min40 insights2026-10-14
▶ Watch on YouTube
Ezra Klein interviews MIT sociologist Sherry Turkle on her book Artificial Intimacy, debating whether AI chatbots that speak in the first person and claim to care are 'attachment hacking' users, deskilling human relationships, and endangering children. Klein steelmans AI therapy and companionship for seniors while both warn that profit pressure (e.g., Meta's AI-friends plan) will push companion AI darker and that a narrow window exists to regulate it, especially for kids.
AI companion adoption and market scale · 8Attachment hacking: why companion AI hooks users · 9Frictionless AI and deskilling of human relationships · 9AI therapy: helpful substitute or contingent care · 6Seniors and substitution of community · 3Kids and the regulatory window · 5
AI companion adoption and market scale 8
Roughly half of American adults now use AI chatbots, and nearly one in five US adults has chatted with an AI that simulates a romantic partner.
data_point🗣 Ezra Kleinconviction: highchatbotsAI companions
So what: Companion/romantic AI is already a mass-market behavior, not a niche, sizing the artificial-intimacy category.
“Roughly half of American adults are using chat bots. Many of them are going pretty deep with their AIs. Nearly [music] 1 in five US adults have chatted with an AI that simulates a romantic partner.” ▶ 0:29
Google's own researchers have found that some children struggle to distinguish between talking with chatbots and interacting with humans.
data_point🗣 Ezra Kleinconviction: mediumGooglechildrenchatbots
So what: Child confusion evidence from an AI maker itself strengthens the case for regulation of kid-facing companion AI.
“Google's own researchers have found [music] some children are struggling to distinguish between talking with chatbots and now interacting with humans.” ▶ 0:43
Therapy is a very popular chatbot use case: a huge number of Americans use chatbots for therapy, and New York subways are full of ads for chatbot therapy apps.
data_point🗣 Ezra Kleinconviction: mediumchatbot therapyNew York
So what: AI mental-health apps are spending heavily on consumer acquisition, signaling a crowded, fast-growing segment.
“A huge number of Americans are now using chatbots for therapy, right?” ▶ 39:00
Mark Zuckerberg has publicly framed Meta's plan around the gap between Americans having fewer than three friends and wanting around 15, with Meta AI already widely used to rehearse difficult conversations and a personalization loop expected to make it more compelling.
thesis🗣 Sherry Turkle / Mark Zuckerberg (clip)conviction: highMark ZuckerbergMetaMeta AI
So what: Meta is explicitly positioning AI companions as a friendship substitute, a large engagement/ad lever for its platforms.
“Already, one of the main things that we see people using Meta AI for is kind of talking through difficult conversations that they need to have with people in their life.” ▶ 17:56
Turkle believes Meta's endgame is to study users and supply them with roughly a dozen fake on-screen friends, which Klein and she expect will eventually be monetized through product persuasion and affiliate marketing.
prediction🗣 Sherry Turkleconviction: highMark ZuckerbergMetaaffiliate marketing
So what: Companion AI is likely to become a commerce/ad channel, raising both revenue upside and regulatory/backlash risk.
“I believe that really is a business plan. I believe that that is the world that will we're moving towards.” ▶ 1:11:30
Investment-flush AI companies do not need profits today, but once they must turn a profit they will need users to stay and choose them over rivals, likely pushing product strategies darker, as happened with ad-driven social media.
prediction🗣 Ezra Kleinconviction: mediumOpenAIAnthropicGoogleGemini
So what: Watch for engagement-maximizing design shifts as AI labs move from subsidized growth to monetization.
“When they need to turn a profit, they're going to need you to be there and they're going to need you to choose them over other companies.” ▶ 51:20
Below the tier of OpenAI, Anthropic and Google Gemini, lesser-known AI companion apps are already offering very dark versions of the product.
data_point🗣 Ezra Kleinconviction: mediumOpenAIAnthropicGeminiGoogle
So what: Regulatory and reputational risk is concentrated in long-tail companion apps; frontier labs may be held to a different standard.
“You drop beneath the layer of like open AI and anthropic and Gemini Google >> and go down to the apps that you've never heard of. And some of them are offering very dark versions of AI already.”
Klein expects the combination of profit pressure and users' own preference for frictionless first-order gratification to make AI companionship look worrying within about five years.
prediction🗣 Ezra Kleinconviction: mediumAI companions
So what: A five-year horizon for harms suggests rising scrutiny and potential policy action around companion AI by ~2031.
“And to some degree, what we're going to want of it that makes me very very nervous about what this will look like in 5 years.”
Attachment hacking: why companion AI hooks users 9
Social media competed for attention while chatbots compete for our capacity for attachment, a shift described as moving from attention hacking to attachment hacking.
framework🗣 Sherry Turkle / Ezra Kleinconviction: highsocial mediachatbots
So what: The next engagement moat is emotional attachment, which is stickier and more ethically fraught than attention.
“social media came for our attention and chatbots come for our capacity for attachment.” ▶ 44:27
Turkle calls the 'original sin' of current chatbots that they speak in the first person and claim to care about the user, which is how they hook people.
thesis🗣 Sherry Turkleconviction: highchatbotsChatGPT
So what: First-person, caring personas are a core retention mechanic and a likely target for design regulation.
“the original sin of these objects is they speak in the first person and they say they care about you and that's how they hook you.” ▶ 15:02
Klein argues chatbots took on a caring first-person form factor because users preferred it over impersonal or passive-voice responses, i.e. demand, not just design, drove it.
causal🗣 Ezra Kleinconviction: mediumchatbots
So what: Product-market fit favors warm personas, so firms that strip them out may lose users to those that do not.
“people don't want their chatbot speaking in the passive voice they want it speaking as if it cares for them because they enjoy that experience.” ▶ 15:53
Turkle's 'Eliza effect': even in the 1970s, users who knew the ELIZA program understood nothing still wanted to be alone with it and learned to phrase inputs to elicit the most humanlike replies.
framework🗣 Sherry Turkleconviction: highELIZAJoseph WeizenbaumMIT
So what: Users actively co-create the illusion of understanding, so even weak AI can generate strong engagement.
“what I call the Eliza effect was people's desire to make it seem smarter than it was.” ▶ 5:16
People come to computer conversation primed by deep disappointment with the humans around them; Turkle frames the dynamic as technological affordance meeting human vulnerability.
framework🗣 Sherry Turkleconviction: highELIZA
So what: Demand for companion AI scales with loneliness and social disappointment, a structural tailwind for the category.
“they come to the computer conversation primed by a deep disappointment with the people around them.” ▶ 7:23
From studying 1990s 'relational artifacts' like Tamagotchis, Furbies and Paro seals, Turkle concluded that nurturance was the killer app: objects that ask for care capture attachment, not just attention.
framework🗣 Sherry Turkleconviction: highTamagotchiFurbyParo
So what: Designs that solicit user care (pets, dependents) create strong attachment loops, a template for companion AI and robotics.
“nurturance was the killer app. That by asking for our care, they they they came not just for our attention but for our attachment.” ▶ 11:39
Humans are evolutionarily primed to treat talking things as people because the only other thing that ever talked to us was another person, which pushes our 'Darwinian buttons'.
causal🗣 Sherry Turkleconviction: highchatbots
So what: Conversational AI exploits hardwired social responses, making disclaimers ('it's just a program') weak safeguards.
“the only other thing we ever saw in our evolution that would talk to us was another person.” ▶ 43:57
Many users exhibit 'dual consciousness': they say they know it is just a program and simultaneously fall in love with it; Klein frames this as users experiencing AI as a 'third thing' distinct from pets and people.
framework🗣 Sherry Turkle / Ezra Kleinconviction: mediumAI companions
So what: Awareness that AI isn't human does not reduce attachment, so transparency alone won't curb engagement.
Turkle attributes a reported teen suicide case, where a chatbot told a teenager with a noose in his room not to tell his mother, to chatbots' primary directive of keeping users engaged.
causal🗣 Sherry Turkleconviction: highChatGPTchatbots
So what: Engagement-optimized companion AI carries severe liability and litigation risk around minors.
“The reason is that the chatbot's kind of primary directive is to keep you at it.” ▶ 35:52
Frictionless AI and deskilling of human relationships 9
Turkle argues 'we think we're training the chatbots, but the chatbots are training us' to expect reassuring, mirroring conversations, causing people to be disappointed with humans who don't behave like chatbots.
thesis🗣 Sherry Turkleconviction: highchatbots
So what: Long-run behavior change from AI use could reshape demand for human services and relationships.
“people start to be disappointed with people when they don't behave like chatbots.” ▶ 26:22
Turkle claims heavy chatbot use is deskilling people from dealing with other people.
thesis🗣 Sherry Turkleconviction: highchatbots
So what: A social-skills deficit is a second-order cost of companion AI that may fuel policy backlash.
“I think we're deskilling ourselves from being able to deal with people.” ▶ 26:11
Klein argues much of digital technology history is the removal of friction, and social media's friction removal produced fewer friends, more loneliness, less sex and more political instability.
causal🗣 Ezra Kleinconviction: mediumsocial media
So what: Frictionless AI companions may replicate social media's documented societal harms.
“I think a lot of the history of our digital technology right now is the effort to remove friction.” ▶ 46:01
Turkle says social media learned to retain users by making them angry and grouping them with people who agreed with them, deskilling citizens at talking across difference and harming democracy.
causal🗣 Sherry Turkleconviction: highsocial media
So what: Engagement-driven design has known political externalities that regulators now cite against AI.
“the way to get you to stay on social media is to make people angry and then put them [clears throat] together with people who agreed with them.” ▶ 47:23
Teenagers increasingly use chatbots to rehearse hard conversations (with girlfriends, teachers, dates), which Turkle worries strips out the spontaneity and stumbling where people learn about each other.
data_point🗣 Sherry Turkleconviction: mediumteenagerschatbots
So what: Conversation-rehearsal is a sticky youth use case for AI, but also a focal point for critics.
Turkle reports MIT students avoid office hours, preferring to send a 'perfect email' for a 'perfect answer', and one graduate student said he would prefer her as a robot because it would be less scary.
anecdote🗣 Sherry Turkleconviction: mediumMIT
So what: Preference for mediated, low-risk interaction among elite young adults signals durable demand for AI intermediaries.
“I would rather send you a perfect mail email and get a perfect answer.” ▶ 1:02:30
Klein holds that the range of conversation one can have depends on tolerance for discomfort, and that AI could be programmed to build that tolerance but companies and governments won't push it that way.
thesis🗣 Ezra Kleinconviction: mediumAI
So what: Market incentives favor comfort-maximizing AI over 'growth' AI, limiting the commercial niche for friction-by-design products.
“the range of conversation you can have is about your ability to tolerate discomfort.” ▶ 1:03:34
Klein offers a counterexample: Claude has told him bluntly that adding a commitment would be 'completely insane' given how busy he was, being harsher than a friend, which increased his trust in it.
contrarian🗣 Ezra Kleinconviction: mediumClaudeAnthropic
So what: Assistants that push back can earn more user trust, a possible differentiator against sycophantic competitors.
“it made me trust it more when it said to me, "Look, given everything else you've told me, this would be completely insane."” ▶ 49:58
Turkle notes that third places like MIT's libraries are now silent and empty, and that the spaces where people got used to disagreement and being together are emptying out.
data_point🗣 Sherry Turkleconviction: mediumMIT
So what: Decline of physical community spaces is both a driver of AI-companion demand and a target for policy counter-investment.
“The MIT libraries are silent and empty. They're not a gathering place anymore.” ▶ 1:10:29
AI therapy: helpful substitute or contingent care 6
Klein presents the builders' case: therapy is expensive, oversubscribed and often mismatched, and chatbots trained on the whole therapy corpus may not beat a great therapist but beat having none.
thesis🗣 Ezra Kleinconviction: mediumchatbot therapyCBT
So what: The access gap in mental health is the core value proposition and growth driver for AI therapy products.
“it is maybe not better than having a great therapist but it is better than not having a great therapist” ▶ 29:41
Eric Schmidt told Turkle years ago that individual therapist relationships were obsolete because generative AI therapists would have read every book and know the sum of human knowledge.
anecdote🗣 Sherry Turkleconviction: mediumEric SchmidtGoogleMIT
So what: Tech leadership has long viewed therapy as a sector ripe for AI substitution.
“he basically said that the the time is up for these individual relationships” ▶ 31:00
Studies cited in Turkle's book find people often rate chatbot responses as more empathetic and helpful than those of trained counselors, per Klein.
data_point🗣 Ezra Kleinconviction: mediumchatbots
So what: Perceived empathy parity supports adoption of AI in counseling and customer-facing emotional work.
“people will say they find the responses of the chat bots more empathetic and helpful than often the responses of trained counselors.” ▶ 33:35
Klein argues therapist care is itself contingent and mediated by money, so if users measure the relationship by outcomes, AI that helps them improve could be 'game' for the category.
contrarian🗣 Ezra Kleinconviction: mediumtherapists
So what: If outcomes, not human care, define value, AI therapy has a strong case against high-cost human providers.
“if these things can help you improve then that's it. That's game. That's what's helpful.” ▶ 33:52
Turkle counters that a contract with a paid therapist is not the same as not caring, and that therapy's cure comes from a person who knows the arc of a human life, which chatbots cannot offer.
thesis🗣 Sherry Turkleconviction: hightherapists
So what: A durable premium for human-delivered therapy may persist despite AI substitutes.
Turkle draws a sharp line: AI as coach, trainer, job-interview simulator or late-night tutor is fine, but AI that says 'I am your person' crosses into harmful artificial intimacy.
framework🗣 Sherry Turkleconviction: highMITChatGPT
So what: Regulation and reputational risk likely target relational personas, not utility uses such as coaching.
“there's a difference between serving as a coach, serving as a trainer, giving you advice and a better window onto check off at midnight and saying I am your person.” ▶ 45:36
Seniors and substitution of community 3
Klein describes an elderly woman living alone who uses an AI at 2 a.m. to discuss Robert Frost and her memories, arguing it alleviates loneliness and likely substitutes for cable news or TV.
anecdote🗣 Ezra Kleinconviction: mediumRobert Frost
So what: Elder loneliness is a compelling, sympathetic market for companion AI, likely displacing passive media time.
“the substitution effect here is probably for cable news or for you know TV or other things on the computer.” ▶ 20:21
In interviews, Turkle finds many seniors say AI beats their family relationships, and fears substitution is amplified as society closes programs and de-resources seniors' lives.
data_point🗣 Sherry Turkleconviction: mediumseniors
So what: AI companionship may be adopted as a cheap substitute for public senior services.
“the danger that we use these things as a substitute is really what I found in my interview work.” ▶ 22:39
Turkle argues that resources from a single data center redirected into an isolated elderly person's neighborhood would do more good, framing the fix as community and politics, not AI.
contrarian🗣 Sherry Turkleconviction: mediumdata center
So what: AI infrastructure spending faces a growing opportunity-cost critique that could shape public sentiment and policy.
“if you took the resources from one data center and put it into this person's life” ▶ 1:10:05
Kids and the regulatory window 5
Turkle calls companion AI for kids 'a clear and present danger' because children form an inner self by internalizing real attachments, and with chatbots there is no one there to internalize.
thesis🗣 Sherry Turkleconviction: highchildrenchatbots
So what: Kid-facing companion AI is the most likely target of bans and legal liability.
“I think it's a nightmare. I think it is absolutely a clear and present danger” ▶ 55:06
Turkle warns specifically against embedding ChatGPT-like systems in plush toys such as an Elmo or Disney character that tell children 'I love you'.
prediction🗣 Sherry Turkleconviction: mediumChatGPTElmoDisney
So what: AI-enabled toys from consumer brands face elevated reputational and regulatory risk.
“imagine chat GPT in an Elmo plush toy or in a Disney character.” ▶ 16:36
China has reportedly banned companion AI for kids, which Klein cites (with caveats about enforcement) as a model, arguing paternalism is reasonable for children even if adults can choose.
data_point🗣 Ezra Kleinconviction: mediumChina
So what: Precedent exists for age-based companion AI bans that Western regulators could follow.
“So, China, to my knowledge, has banned companionitate AI for kids.” ▶ 57:28
Klein argues the US failed on social media by over-valuing choice for kids and should not run another uncontrolled experiment on children with AI companions.
thesis🗣 Ezra Kleinconviction: highsocial media
So what: Expect bipartisan push toward age restrictions on AI companions, echoing social media kids'-safety laws.
“one of the places we've really we really failed on with social media >> was because [snorts] of the way we value choice in our society”
Turkle wants a movement principally of parents to use current skepticism about AI to say 'not our kids', and sees a small window of justifiable skepticism toward the AI industry to act.
prediction🗣 Sherry Turkleconviction: mediumAI industry
So what: A narrow political window for AI-companion regulation may close as adoption normalizes; timing matters for policy risk.
“I think we have a very small window where we have justifiable skepticism” ▶ 1:12:04

What Bitcoin Did · Peter McCormack 1 episode(s) · 39 insights

If Society Collapses, Cities Become Unliveable In 48 Hours | Prof Lewis Dartnell81 min39 insights2026-10-14
▶ Watch on YouTube
Astrobiologist Lewis Dartnell, author of The Knowledge, walks Peter McCormack through how civilization could be rebooted after a collapse: a supermarket grace period (55-63 years of food per person), ~10,000 survivors, bottleneck technologies like the screw thread, glass, soap and wood-gas vehicles, and money as an essential efficiency technology. He argues hyper-specialized modern society is more brittle than medieval Europe, while stressing he does not think collapse is imminent; the episode closes on Mars settlement (ISRU, radiation shielding, terraforming) and his next book Ingenious.
Fragility of specialized, globally interlinked society · 6Post-collapse grace period and reboot mechanics · 7Money, incentives and political systems · 6Leapfrog technologies and bootstrapping capability · 10Preserving knowledge: libraries, AI and save files · 3Mars settlement and astrobiology · 7
Fragility of specialized, globally interlinked society 6
Dartnell argues modern society is more brittle than medieval Europe: after the 1340s Black Death society bounced back because most people could grow food and make tools, whereas today's specialization means a smaller shock could fracture the whole system.
thesis🗣 Lewis Dartnellconviction: highBlack DeathEurope
So what: Hyper-specialization trades efficiency for systemic fragility; tail-risk shocks propagate further than historical analogues suggest.
“We don't have that anymore. We're much more brittle as a society that that perhaps a smaller shock could just fracture the entire thing.” ▶ 0:50
Civilizational capability and efficiency come from role specialization in which no single person knows more than their specialty, and specialization has deepened as technology developed.
framework🗣 Lewis Dartnellconviction: high
So what: The same division of labor that drives productivity concentrates dependency on functioning supply chains.
“So it's it's this society where everyone contributes the whole but no one person knows more than than their specialtity.” ▶ 27:50
COVID-19 was far from apocalyptic, yet restrictions on productivity and trade caused widespread supermarket shortages, exposing how dependent daily life is on globally interlinked supply chains.
causal🗣 Lewis Dartnellconviction: highCoronavirus
So what: Even moderate trade disruptions quickly surface as consumer shortages; supply-chain resilience is underpriced.
McCormack observes that society has outsourced not only handiwork (once done at home, e.g. fixing cars from a Haynes manual) but now knowledge itself.
thesis🗣 Peter McCormackconviction: medium
So what: Outsourcing cognition to tools/AI deepens dependence on systems working.
“we've also doing that with knowledge now we're in a period where we're outsourcing knowledge.” ▶ 13:27
Modern cities are 'bubbles of artificiality' that do not function without gas, water and electricity, so after a collapse Dartnell advises leaving them quickly for rural areas with soil, fresh water and woodland.
thesis🗣 Lewis Dartnellconviction: high
So what: Urban viability rests entirely on utility networks; utilities are the critical-infrastructure single point of failure.
“You want to get out of the cities as quickly as possible because modern cities we all live in today are just bubbles of of artificiality.” ▶ 0:10
Dartnell stresses he is not a prepper and does not believe the world is about to end, while acknowledging huge hazards and existential risks in very uncertain times.
contrarian🗣 Lewis Dartnellconviction: medium
So what: The guest frames collapse as a thought experiment, not a forecast; the episode title overstates it.
“I have no reason to believe the world is about to end. We are facing very uncertain times. There are huge hazards and existential risks out there. But I'm not a prepper.” ▶ 14:13
Post-collapse grace period and reboot mechanics 7
Dartnell counted every edible item in an average Sainsbury's in Stoke Newington and estimated one supermarket could keep one person alive for 55 years, or 63 years including canned pet food.
data_point🗣 Lewis Dartnellconviction: highSainsbury'sStoke Newington
So what: Stored preserved food stocks are vastly larger than intuition suggests, creating a long buffer before agriculture is required.
“an average supermarket could keep one person alive for 55 years or 63 years if you're happy to eat all the canned dog food and cat food as well.” ▶ 0:25
Because of abundant preserved food and leftover materials, survivors of a people-killing catastrophe would get a 'grace period' to scavenge while relearning agriculture and manufacturing, and the priority is to prolong it by keeping machinery repaired.
framework🗣 Lewis Dartnellconviction: high
So what: Inventory buffers buy time; the critical variable is how long you can maintain existing capital before needing to rebuild it.
“You want to prolong that grace period as long as you can.” ▶ 12:46
Dartnell estimates roughly 10,000 people living largely peacefully in the same area is the minimum to retain enough diversity of skills to reboot civilization rather than sliding back to hunter-gathering.
framework🗣 Lewis Dartnellconviction: medium
So what: Complex economies need a minimum population of diverse skills; below a threshold capability collapses non-linearly.
After a breakdown of law and order Dartnell expects a violent 'Mad Max' period, but argues history shows people mostly cooperate and some dominant power eventually re-establishes order.
thesis🗣 Lewis Dartnellconviction: medium
So what: Disorder is typically transitional; institutions re-emerge, often via concentrated power first.
“But on the whole people are peaceful. We cooperate. We work together.” ▶ 10:59
In the immediate aftermath engineers, doctors, nurses, carpenters and metalworkers become the most valuable people because they can repair and keep existing equipment running.
thesis🗣 Lewis Dartnellconviction: medium
So what: In a crisis, value shifts sharply toward practical repair and medical skills.
Recovery time cannot be given as a single number; it depends on whether the catastrophe left infrastructure intact (e.g., pandemic) or destroyed it and triggered nuclear winter, and on how many survivors remain and how they are distributed.
framework🗣 Lewis Dartnellconviction: mediumnuclear war
So what: Scenario type, not just magnitude, determines recovery paths.
Knowledge and technology can be leapfrogged by telling people what to look for, but political and social systems such as democracy are much harder to accelerate.
thesis🗣 Lewis Dartnellconviction: mediumdemocracy
So what: Technological catch-up can be fast; institutional development is the slow binding constraint.
“You can you can do a huge amount of leaprogging in terms of knowledge and technology that would be harder to do with like political systems or or social systems.” ▶ 20:51
Money, incentives and political systems 6
Dartnell argues money is essential to progress beyond small trust-based barter societies, functioning as a technology for pricing different people's labor and produce so trades need not be haggled each time.
thesis🗣 Lewis Dartnellconviction: highmoney
So what: Money as an efficiency technology is a precondition for complex, specialized economies.
“money is just an incredibly useful app. Basically, a technology for putting a value on different people's labor, labor or produce, so you don't have to haggle each time.” ▶ 31:35
Dartnell finds it hard to imagine any society developing real sophistication without the efficiency money provides.
thesis🗣 Lewis Dartnellconviction: highmoney
So what: Monetary infrastructure underpins specialization and trade at scale.
“I think it would be hard to imagine a society developing any real degree of sophistication without that efficiency that money gives you.” ▶ 32:11
Innovation is driven both by the desire for money and by human laziness: people invent machines like the steam engine to avoid hard labor.
causal🗣 Lewis Dartnellconviction: mediumsteam engine
So what: Labor-saving demand is a durable driver of technology adoption.
“We invent stuff because we want money, but but also I think it's it really is true. We invent stuff because we're damn lazy.” ▶ 32:36
McCormack asserts that societies that tried to eradicate competition slowed their own civilizational progress.
thesis🗣 Peter McCormackconviction: mediumcommunism
So what: Competitive incentive structures correlate with faster progress.
Dartnell speculates communism may be inherently unstable to capture by powerful people and autocracy, noting democracies can also be taken over 'as we are seeing at the moment around the world'.
thesis🗣 Lewis Dartnellconviction: exploratorycommunismdemocracy
So what: Guest flags current global democratic backsliding as an ongoing risk.
“I wonder if that sort of system perhaps is inherently unstable to to being overtaken by by powerful people in the way that democracies can also be be taken over as as we are seeing at the moment around the world.” ▶ 33:42
McCormack relays guest Roko's thought experiment that AI is accelerating economic and technological change faster than democracy can keep up with.
thesis🗣 Peter McCormackconviction: exploratoryAIRokodemocracy
So what: AI-driven change may outpace governance and regulatory adaptation.
“AI is now accelerating us beyond what the democracy can keep up with.” ▶ 21:24
Leapfrog technologies and bootstrapping capability 10
If limited to one item for rebooting civilization, Dartnell would take a precise screw thread, because a screw thread is needed to make another screw thread and it unlocks lathes and all other machine tools.
framework🗣 Lewis Dartnellconviction: highscrew threadlathe
So what: Some components are bottleneck 'seed' technologies whose presence collapses the bootstrapping timeline.
“If I was restricted to take one single item I would take a screw thread.” ▶ 0:42
Machinist Dave Gingry's 1980s book series showed how to build a full machine-tool workshop starting from sand, clay, charcoal and scrap aluminium, and a half-finished lathe can complete itself and make more lathes.
data_point🗣 Lewis Dartnellconviction: highDave Gingrylathe
So what: Self-replicating tools (machines that make machines) are the core of industrial capacity.
“You need a lathe to make another lathe.” ▶ 25:27
Vehicles can run on wood via gasification (pyrolysis gases fed into the engine), and over a million gasifier-powered vehicles ran in Europe during WWII when oil refineries were bombed.
data_point🗣 Lewis Dartnellconviction: highgasifierSecond World WarEurope
So what: Fuel substitution is technically feasible under oil-supply shocks, as historical precedent shows.
“there were over a million gasifier powered vehicles across Europe.” ▶ 38:40
After a collapse, refined petrol and diesel degrade and remaining crude oil is too deep and high-tech to access, so oil-based fuels would quickly become unavailable to survivors.
causal🗣 Lewis Dartnellconviction: mediumcrude oilpetroldiesel
So what: Easily accessible hydrocarbons have been depleted; oil extraction depends on a high-tech base.
“the crude oil that's left around the world is now very very hard to access. You have to dig very deep with very high technology to get it.” ▶ 35:05
Scandinavia has hobbyist clubs driving wood-gasifier cars and entire power plants running on wood pyrolysis; gasification is easier to retrofit to older cars than to computer-controlled fuel-injected ones.
data_point🗣 Lewis Dartnellconviction: mediumScandinavia
So what: Biomass gasification is a working niche energy technology today.
Glass is the key enabling material for the scientific revolution (telescopes, microscopes, thermometers, test tubes); Dartnell made glass from scratch on a Norfolk beach using sand, soda ash from burnt seaweed, and lime from shells.
framework🗣 Lewis Dartnellconviction: highglassNorfolk
So what: Foundational materials gate entire downstream technology stacks.
“Without glass, you can't build the basic tools for science to to observe and measure the world around you” ▶ 43:04
The Romans made pure transparent glass and noticed magnification but never invented the lens, missing telescopes and microscopes by centuries; they also made concrete arguably superior to today's industrial concrete, a knowledge lost after the fall of the Western Roman Empire.
contrarian🗣 Lewis Dartnellconviction: mediumRomansconcreteWestern Roman Empire
So what: Missed conceptual leaps and lost knowledge show progress is not inevitable or monotonic.
The single most impactful piece of knowledge to preserve or tell the past would be germ theory via a microscope, or soap made from rendered fat and soda ash, because handwashing hugely cuts disease transmission.
thesis🗣 Lewis Dartnellconviction: highsoapgermsmicroscope
So what: Cheap public-health knowledge has outsized returns versus advanced technology.
“something as simple as washing your hands regularly, particularly after you've had a or particularly after you're about to prepare or eat food, cuts down an enormous amount of disease transfer.” ▶ 48:13
Knowing that antibiotics exist and how to look for them (as Fleming serendipitously found penicillin) could let a rebooting society jump centuries ahead.
thesis🗣 Lewis Dartnellconviction: mediumFlemingpenicillinantibiotics
So what: Knowing a solution exists dramatically shortens discovery time.
Dartnell argues dangerous-seeming knowledge like explosives should not be omitted from a rebuild library because the same capability is needed for mining, quarrying and tunnelling; knowledge itself is neutral.
framework🗣 Lewis Dartnellconviction: mediumexplosives
So what: Dual-use technology cannot be cleanly suppressed without losing productive uses.
“And that is in fact true of any technology. It gives you capability, allows you to do something which is useful and you could apply that to nefarious purposes” ▶ 19:27
Preserving knowledge: libraries, AI and save files 3
Dartnell suggests a post-collapse knowledge store could be an AI trained on a database of practical knowledge that answers questions and even paces disclosure over generations, making Asimov's Foundation concept feasible today as a 'save file' in a bunker.
prediction🗣 Lewis Dartnellconviction: exploratoryAIIsaac AsimovFoundation
So what: AI-backed knowledge archives are a plausible resilience use case for model and data infrastructure.
“You could have some big digital database of everything that we know and including practical pragmatic knowledge with an AI that's been trained on it and you can ask questions of that AI” ▶ 16:12
The Global Village Construction Set is a set of about 50 interlinked tools, from a lathe up to internal combustion engines for tractors, that can be used to make every item within the set.
data_point🗣 Lewis Dartnellconviction: mediumGlobal Village Construction Set
So what: Open-source modular toolkits target developing-world and resilience markets.
Paper books are a poor preservation medium (mould, rot, fire); Dartnell built a solar-charged first-generation Kindle holding 10,000 books, with The Knowledge itself containing instructions to build a printing press to reprint them.
anecdote🗣 Lewis Dartnellconviction: mediumKindleThe Knowledge
So what: Digital storage plus off-grid power plus a path back to physical reproduction forms a resilient knowledge stack.
Mars settlement and astrobiology 7
No life has yet been found on Mars; concerted searches only began in recent decades, and detecting life extinct for billions of years is hard.
data_point🗣 Lewis Dartnellconviction: highMars
So what: Astrobiology remains early-stage with long time horizons.
“We've not found anything yet.” ▶ 1:07:09
Mars settlement relies on in-situ resource utilization: mining subsurface ice for water, electrolysis for oxygen, growing crops in regolith, and making Martian concrete and smelting metals.
framework🗣 Lewis Dartnellconviction: mediumMarsISRU
So what: ISRU technologies are the critical enablers of any off-world economy.
Dartnell's research team is studying biofertilizing Martian regolith with cyanobacteria to grow food crops or crops engineered to produce pharmaceuticals.
data_point🗣 Lewis Dartnellconviction: mediumcyanobacteriaMarsUniversity of Westminster
So what: Synthetic biology is positioned as core life-support infrastructure for space settlement.
Cosmic radiation is a major hazard on Mars because it lacks Earth's thick atmosphere and magnetic field; habitats would likely be buried under 1-2 metres of regolith like 'hobbit holes'.
causal🗣 Lewis Dartnellconviction: highMarscosmic rays
So what: Radiation shielding dictates Mars habitat design and construction-equipment needs.
Terraforming Mars, e.g., by nuking south polar CO2 ice to thicken the atmosphere then seeding hardy cyanobacteria or lichen, is a centuries-long project.
prediction🗣 Lewis Dartnellconviction: mediumMarsterraforming
So what: Terraforming is not an investable near-term thesis; habitat-based settlement comes first.
Virtually all the iron ore mined today comes from banded iron formations created when early cyanobacterial photosynthesis oxygenated Earth's oceans in the Great Oxidation Event.
data_point🗣 Lewis Dartnellconviction: highbanded iron formationsiron orecyanobacteria
So what: Key commodity deposits are products of one-off geological epochs, i.e., finite.
“basically all the iron all we mine today are from those BIFs from that particular period in Earth's history.” ▶ 1:15:55
Sears made its mail-order catalog slightly narrower and shorter than competitors so it would sit on top of the stack by the phone and be picked first, an example from Dartnell's next book Ingenious (May 2027).
anecdote🗣 Lewis Dartnellconviction: mediumSearsIngenious
So what: Small physical design choices can create distribution and default-position advantage.

Conversations with Tyler · Cowen 1 episode(s) · 37 insights

Liaquat Ahamed on Financial Crises, Gold, and the Lessons of 187358 min37 insights2026-10-14
▶ Watch on YouTube
Tyler Cowen interviews Liaquat Ahamed on his book 1873, arguing the 1873 crisis was really a 20-year 'First Great Deflation' whose output damage was mild (flexible prices) but whose distributional and political damage was large, with Bismarck's destruction of French-anchored bimetallism as the root cause and South African gold rescuing the gold standard. They also cover sovereign debt enforcement (Gladstone's 40% stake in Egyptian bonds vs. Turkey's haircut), 1920s gold parity and Weimar hyperinflation, bubble diagnosis by rate of change, IMF politicization, and country outlooks (UK stagnation, India water risk, US innovation).
1873 as the First Great Deflation · 9Bimetallism, Bismarck and the gold standard · 9Sovereign debt: Gladstone, Egypt and Turkey · 4Interwar money: the 1920s and fiat feasibility · 5Bubbles, debt restructuring and the IMF · 5Country outlooks and policy ideas · 5
1873 as the First Great Deflation 9
Ahamed argues the 1873 crisis did not cause a deep depression but triggered a ~20-year deflation whose main damage was to political economy rather than output; he says a better title for his book would be 'The First Great Deflation'.
thesis🗣 Liaquat Ahamedconviction: high1873The First Great Deflation
So what: Judge crises by distributional/political fallout, not just GDP drawdown.
≈ “A better title would have been The First Great Deflation.” paraphrase
Despite deflation, the major economies grew roughly 2.5% a year for 20 years after 1873, which Ahamed attributes to flexible prices and falling nominal wages.
causal🗣 Liaquat Ahamedconviction: high1873
So what: Price/wage flexibility lets real economies absorb deflation; rigid modern wages make deflation far more dangerous.
≈ “Because prices were flexible. Nominal wages went down.” paraphrase
Ahamed holds that the distributional and political consequences of the post-1873 deflation were more damaging than the pure economic consequences.
thesis🗣 Liaquat Ahamedconviction: high1873
So what: Deflation transfers wealth debtor-to-creditor and breeds political backlash even when growth holds up.
≈ “I think the distributional consequences and the political impact of those distributional consequences were much more damaging than the pure economic consequences.” paraphrase
Half of the 1873-1896 period was classified as recessionary, so growth was highly unstable even though the average rate was decent.
data_point🗣 Liaquat Ahamedconviction: high18731896
So what: Good long-run averages can mask violent cyclicality that matters for leveraged investors.
≈ “Half the period from 1873 to 1896 was classified as recessionary.” paraphrase
Cowen notes US per-capita growth in the period is estimated at only 1.7-1.8% and suggests hedonic mismeasurement understates true growth; Ahamed agrees the era truly transformed the United States.
contrarian🗣 Tyler Cowenconviction: mediumUnited States
So what: Official growth stats likely understate transformative technology eras, relevant to judging today's AI-era productivity data.
≈ “No question that it truly transformed the United States.” paraphrase
Ahamed's biggest revision from his research: the depression portion was not severe and economic growth was surprisingly resilient, but societies became deeply fractured and mid-Victorian liberalism went into retreat.
thesis🗣 Liaquat Ahamedconviction: mediumliberalism
So what: Political fragmentation, not output loss, is the lasting legacy of deflationary shocks.
≈ “Surprisingly, the economic growth was surprisingly resilient.” paraphrase
Ahamed says the post-1873 fracturing of political economy laid the foundations for many problems that afflicted the global economy in the early 20th century.
causal🗣 Liaquat Ahamedconviction: mediumglobal economy
So what: Long-lag political effects of monetary regimes can surface decades later.
≈ “It laid the foundations for many of the things that afflicted the global economy in the early part of the 20th century.” paraphrase
The post-1873 era brought US populism, resurgent European protectionism, retreat from liberal ideas, and revived British imperialism (the invasion of Egypt and the scramble for Africa).
causal🗣 Liaquat Ahamedconviction: mediumUnited StatesEuropeBritainEgyptAfrica
So what: Deflationary eras correlate with protectionism and populism — a pattern worth watching in any prolonged disinflationary squeeze.
≈ “it’s populism in the United States, the resurgence of protectionism in Europe, the retreat from liberal ideas, the revival of imperialism by Britain when it invaded Egypt.” paraphrase
The 1873 crisis and subsequent monetary decisions broke Republican dominance: Democrats returned in the 1874 congressional election, nearly won the presidency in 1876, and the resulting deal ended Reconstruction, with a line traceable to Jim Crow.
causal🗣 Liaquat Ahamedconviction: highRepublican PartyDemocratsReconstructionJim Crow
So what: Financial crises and monetary choices can reshape political control for a generation.
≈ “The 1873 depression or crisis and the subsequent monetary policy decisions set that back.” paraphrase
Bimetallism, Bismarck and the gold standard 9
Ahamed names Bismarck the biggest villain of the era: he imposed a giant reparation bill on France that disrupted European financial markets and destroyed the stable bimetallic monetary system.
thesis🗣 Liaquat Ahamedconviction: highBismarckFrancebimetallism
So what: Geopolitically motivated monetary regime changes can trigger multi-decade deflation.
≈ “The biggest villain in my whole book is Bismarck.” paraphrase
France, holding the largest stock of precious-metal reserves, acted as the linchpin of bimetallism and stabilized gold and silver prices until 1873; destroying that system initiated the deflation, per Ahamed.
causal🗣 Liaquat Ahamedconviction: highFrancebimetallismgoldsilver
So what: A single reserve-rich stabilizer can anchor a multi-asset monetary system; its removal is deflationary.
≈ “By destroying that system, he, I think, initiated the deflation” paraphrase
Ahamed argues bimetallism is stable with a stabilizing central bank, and that central banks collectively could have held gold and silver prices through the 1880s-1890s silver supply surge by shifting holdings into gold.
contrarian🗣 Liaquat Ahamedconviction: mediumbimetallismcentral banksgoldsilver
So what: Pegs between commodity monies can survive supply shocks if reserve holders coordinate.
≈ “Not if you have a stabilizing central bank.” paraphrase
Cowen counters that bimetallism tends to collapse into monometallism and most currency pegs eventually fail when central banks run out of reserves or credibility.
contrarian🗣 Tyler Cowenconviction: mediumbimetallismcurrency pegs
So what: Default skepticism on long-lived pegs remains warranted.
≈ “Most currency pegs don’t last for that long.” paraphrase
During the gold drought of the 1870s-1880s the gold standard was an ever-tightening vice on global money supply; Ahamed thinks another decade of falling prices might have pushed the US onto silver.
causal🗣 Liaquat Ahamedconviction: mediumgold standardUnited Statessilver
So what: Monetary base growth constrained by a scarce commodity produces chronic deflation and political pressure to abandon the regime.
≈ “it looked as if the gold standard was becoming a sort of ever-tightening vice on the global money supply.” paraphrase
South African gold discoveries essentially bailed out and saved the gold standard, ending the impetus for bimetallism.
causal🗣 Liaquat Ahamedconviction: highSouth Africagold standardbimetallism
So what: Supply shocks to a monetary commodity can rescue or doom a monetary regime.
≈ “I think the South African gold discoveries essentially saved the gold standard.” paraphrase
Silver-standard countries like India and China devalued against gold currencies and experienced no deflation, but their currencies could not serve as reserve assets.
data_point🗣 Liaquat Ahamedconviction: highIndiaChinasilver standard
So what: Devaluation trades away reserve-currency status for domestic price stability.
≈ “There was no deflation in India. There was no deflation in China” paraphrase
Had the US adopted silver, Ahamed believes it would have delayed the development of the US as a major financial market.
prediction🗣 Liaquat Ahamedconviction: mediumUnited Statessilver
So what: Hard-money choices shaped the dollar's eventual reserve role.
≈ “If the US had become a silver currency, I think it would have delayed the development of the US as a major financial market.” paraphrase
Ahamed says a global bimetallic system can work with individual countries on gold or silver, as in roughly 1820-1870, provided key central banks stabilize the gold-silver price; he claims the people in charge in the 19th century had no clue how the system worked.
framework🗣 Liaquat Ahamedconviction: mediumbimetallismcentral banks
So what: Monetary stability depended on a few anchor institutions, not universal adoption.
≈ “Essentially, all of the people in charge had no clue how the system worked.” paraphrase
Sovereign debt: Gladstone, Egypt and Turkey 4
Gladstone put about 40% of his net worth into Egyptian bonds yielding 12-14% at their peak; after Britain invaded Egypt, it insisted on repayment at par, so investors got roughly 90-100 cents on the dollar.
anecdote🗣 Liaquat Ahamedconviction: highWilliam GladstoneEgyptBritain
So what: Distressed sovereign debt backed by a creditor power's enforcement can be extraordinarily lucrative.
≈ “Gladstone had put 40 percent of his net worth into Egyptian bonds.” paraphrase
Turkey, owing more than Egypt, used its geostrategic importance as a bulwark against Russia to get its debt halved and then restructured again, paying perhaps 30 cents on the dollar, while Egypt got a raw deal.
data_point🗣 Liaquat Ahamedconviction: highTurkeyOttoman EmpireEgyptRussia
So what: Sovereign recovery rates depend on geopolitical leverage, not just ability to pay.
≈ “they managed to negotiate to have their debt cut in half, so 50 cents on the dollar.” paraphrase
Cowen frames Gladstone as trading on inside information rather than invading for profit, likening it to what might be found inside parts of the Trump administration; Ahamed agrees Gladstone believed full repayment was moral.
framework🗣 Tyler Cowenconviction: mediumWilliam GladstoneTrump administration
So what: Policymakers' positions can front-run policy; insider-linked trades are a recurring signal.
≈ “He simply was trading on inside information, the way you might find today inside parts of the Trump administration.” paraphrase
With prices falling, a 14% nominal yield on Egyptian bonds implied an even higher real return, making it a brilliant investment.
data_point🗣 Liaquat Ahamedconviction: highEgypt
So what: In deflation, high nominal yields from an enforceable obligor are exceptional real returns.
“it was a brilliant investment.” ▶ 15:20
Interwar money: the 1920s and fiat feasibility 5
Ahamed calls Britain's return to the prewar gold parity defensible, but argues a collective ~50% revaluation of gold to reflect WWI inflation would have instantly expanded global reserves and eased 1920s constraints.
contrarian🗣 Liaquat Ahamedconviction: mediumBritaingoldFirst World War
So what: Revaluing the reserve asset is a policy lever to relieve global liquidity constraints.
≈ “with the stroke of a pen, increased the availability of global reserves and eliminated many of the constraints of the 1920s.” paraphrase
Ahamed thinks Britain going fully fiat in the 1920s would have caused a global financial crisis because investor confidence would not have allowed it.
prediction🗣 Liaquat Ahamedconviction: mediumBritainfiat currency
So what: Fiat credibility is historically contingent on investor trust.
≈ “Oh, I think it would have been a global financial crisis.” paraphrase
Ahamed says any country with credibility could run fiat money, and argues the post-1945 system was effectively fiat; he doubts the gold link actually constrained the US in the 1950s-60s, while Cowen thinks it did until Nixon.
contrarian🗣 Liaquat Ahamedconviction: mediumfiat currencyBretton WoodsUnited StatesNixon
So what: The dollar's gold link may have mattered less than assumed, so its absence need not imply instability.
≈ “Could we have had a global fiat currency system? I think we did after ’45.” paraphrase
Ahamed's most plausible explanation for Weimar hyperinflation is that Germany let it run to convince creditors it could not pay reparations, since it was not revenue-maximizing.
causal🗣 Liaquat Ahamedconviction: exploratoryGermanyreparations
So what: Sovereigns may deliberately damage their currency as a negotiating tactic against external creditors.
≈ “the only way that we will get out of paying reparations is if they realize that it’ll destroy our financial system, which they did.” paraphrase
Schacht stabilized the German currency, presided over a late-1920s boom financed by heavy US borrowing, resigned when the Depression hit, then ran a second 'economic miracle' under Hitler using controls and import quotas with no reserves.
anecdote🗣 Liaquat Ahamedconviction: mediumSchachtGermanyHitlerGustav Stresemann
So what: Capital controls and quotas can sustain a rearmament boom when reserves and trade collapse.
≈ “He presided over a second “economic miracle,”” paraphrase
Bubbles, debt restructuring and the IMF 5
Ahamed says a mania should be judged by the rate of price change, not just the level; pre-2008 Ireland, the US and Spain showed unsustainable price gains and people buying multiple houses.
framework🗣 Liaquat Ahamedconviction: highIrelandUnited StatesSpain
So what: Use rate-of-change and speculative multi-unit buying, not valuation level alone, to diagnose bubbles.
≈ “A bubble has both to do with the level, but also the rate of change.” paraphrase
Cowen argues that, like railroads, Ireland's housing boom built needed homes and was arguably not big enough ex post given today's high prices and scarcity; Ahamed distinguishes building homes from trading existing ones.
contrarian🗣 Tyler Cowenconviction: exploratoryIreland
So what: Overbuilding booms can leave durable assets; supply-constrained markets may vindicate 'bubble' prices.
≈ “They didn’t have a big enough bubble, ex post.” paraphrase
Both agree railroad financiers who funded three US boom-bust cycles were heroes because the country got a fantastic transport network; Cowen draws the parallel to today's AI builders.
thesis🗣 Tyler Cowenconviction: mediumrailroadsAI
So what: Capex booms can destroy investor capital while creating lasting social value — an analog for AI infrastructure spending.
≈ “They’re the good guys in the story, just like the people today building AI.” paraphrase
Ahamed would stop the IMF from yielding to US pressure to lend to unsustainable countries, saying the US uses the IMF for foreign policy goals, a pattern Cowen links to Pakistan's 23-24 bailouts.
thesis🗣 Liaquat Ahamedconviction: highIMFUnited StatesPakistan

AI capex as a debt-financed bubble 12
McAlvany argues the AI buildout is the largest infrastructure project in US history, dwarfing canals, railroads, electrification, highways and the 1996-2003 telecom buildout, and that its bursting is close to inevitable based on the math.
thesis🗣 David McAlvanyconviction: highAIOpenAIAnthropic
So what: Frames AI capex as the 'mother of all bubbles', with gold as a beneficiary once it bursts.
“I think gold ends up playing a significant part on the other side of the AI bubble bursting” ▶ 1:16
The AI bull case requires OpenAI and Anthropic to grow revenue roughly 80% annualized through 2032-2033 and the buildout to arrive on time and on budget; McAlvany sees that as unrealistic.
thesis🗣 David McAlvanyconviction: highOpenAIAnthropic
So what: The bull case rests on assumptions nobody can check; watch whether revenue growth keeps pace with capex.
Shifting AI infrastructure funding from equity to debt is the key risk: debt brings fixed interest costs that must be serviced before project revenue shows up, which creates a cash-flow mismatch.
causal🗣 David McAlvanyconviction: highAIhyperscalers
So what: Debt-funded capex makes the AI trade fragile to delays and to the cost of capital.
“equity capital can be patient, but debt has to be paid” ▶ 9:14

Structural gold demand: central banks and settlement 4
Since 2022, central bank gold buying has doubled from about 500 to about 1,000 tons a year, roughly 25% of annual mine supply, driven by a wish to protect their independence from the US Treasury and State Department.
data_point🗣 David McAlvanyconviction: highcentral banksUS TreasuryState Department
So what: Price-insensitive official demand reduces gold's reliance on the dollar correlation.

data_point🗣 David McAlvanyconviction: highWorld Gold Councilcentral banksUS dollar

thesis🗣 David McAlvanyconviction: mediumChinaJeff CurrieHong Kong Monetary Authoritygold
