How dark money is routed 5
Dark money flows through three main routes: 501(c)(4)s/501(c)(6)s/LLCs running ads directly, those entities donating to super PACs, and pop-up super PACs formed in the final weeks that don't disclose donors until after election day.
framework🗣 Michael Beckel (Issue One)conviction: high501(c)(4)501(c)(6)super PACsLLC
So what: Useful map for tracing who is really funding a political campaign.
Aggressive lawyers advise that 501(c)(4) nonprofits can spend up to 49% of their money on express vote-for/vote-against ads while claiming the other 51% as their primary purpose.
data_point🗣 Michael Beckel (Issue One)conviction: medium501(c)(4)
So what: Explains why nonprofit 'social welfare' groups are a major vehicle for anonymous electoral spending.
“if you've got a good lawyer, an aggressive lawyer, they might even say you could spend like 49% of your money on ads that say vote for vote against a candidate” ▶ 19:21
Pop-up super PACs that appear in the final couple of weeks before an election need not disclose donors until after election day, because campaign finance laws are antiquated; pending federal legislation would require near-real-time reporting like candidates face.
causal🗣 Michael Beckel (Issue One)conviction: highsuper PACsCongress
So what: Late-cycle spending is the least transparent; disclosure reform would close this window.
“our campaign finance laws are so antiquated, they're not going to have to disclose their donors until after election day” ▶ 20:56
Super PACs can take any name except a candidate's name, enabling deceptive branding such as Elon Musk's 2024 'RBG PAC', which claimed Ruth Bader Ginsburg would have agreed with Trump on abortion without Musk's name visible to viewers.
anecdote🗣 Michael Beckel (Issue One)conviction: highElon MuskRBG PACRuth Bader GinsburgDonald Trump
So what: Ad messaging is often deliberately misattributed; the true sponsor matters for interpretation.
“In uh 2024, Elon Musk funded a group called RBGPAC, which uh was very successful in Pennsylvania.” ▶ 23:17
Candidates coordinate with nominally independent super PACs through 'red boxing', posting the messaging they want emphasized on their websites, which super PAC ads then copy.
framework🗣 Hasan Minhajconviction: highsuper PACs
So what: The independence requirement is largely a formality.
“this is a technique where candidates feature information on their websites sometimes in a red box like this” ▶ 6:53
Broken referees: Supreme Court and FEC 4
The FEC is a six-member commission that currently has only two members and needs four votes to investigate or issue rules, leaving it on autopilot as a disclosure-only agency with no enforcement.
data_point🗣 Michael Beckel (Issue One)conviction: highFECDonald Trump
So what: Effectively zero federal campaign finance enforcement heading into the midterms, which emboldens aggressive spending tactics.
“it's a six-member commission that only has two members on it and it needs four people to agree on anything” ▶ 25:34
The Roberts court has, decision after decision, loosened rules in ways that favor big money, and Beckel agrees that it has effectively legalized corruption by defining it narrowly as quid pro quo.
thesis🗣 Michael Beckel (Issue One)conviction: highRoberts courtSupreme CourtCitizens United
So what: Judicial relief is unlikely; reform must come through states, ballot measures or constitutional amendment.
“The Roberts court has been very okay with the loosening of these rules and decision after decision after decision has made it more favorable for big money” ▶ 26:37
Citizens United's key change was permitting direct corporate independent expenditures, effectively rolling back the 1907 Tillman Act ban on corporate money that followed the Gilded Age.
causal🗣 Michael Beckel (Issue One)conviction: highCitizens UnitedTillman ActTheodore Roosevelt
So what: Today's system resembles the Gilded Age, which historically preceded a major reform wave.
“Citizens United made things uniquely bad by allowing direct corporate independent expenditures in politics.” ▶ 15:54
Presidential campaign costs rose from about $114 million in 1976 to $1.4 billion in 2000, driven by soft money, before Citizens United accelerated things further.
data_point🗣 Hasan Minhajconviction: mediumsoft money
So what: Shows the long-run compounding of political spending through every regulatory loophole.
“The 1976 presidential election that cost about $114 million. But by 2000, fueled by soft money, that cost had gone up to $1.4 billion.” ▶ 4:57

Jeff Yan's Plan to Bring the Entire Financial Industry Onto Hyperliquid | Odd Lots61 min34 insights2026-10-15
▶ Watch on YouTube
Hyperliquid Labs CEO Jeff Yan explains Hyperliquid as a neutral on-chain layer aiming to 'house all of finance', detailing HIP-3 permissionless deployers (500,000 HYPE stake, 31-hour Dutch auctions), oracle rules (1% clamp), and on-chain clearing. He argues perps can lead price discovery over thin underlyings, incumbents will become deployers like AWS adopters, and regulators may oversee deployers rather than the protocol as the CFTC weighs US access.
Hyperliquid vision: neutral layer to house all finance · 6Protocol mechanics: HIP-3 deployers, oracles, auctions · 10Derivatives over the underlying: price discovery and funding · 8Access, pre-IPO exposure and democratization · 3Regulation and US access · 4Incumbents as future deployers · 3
Hyperliquid vision: neutral layer to house all finance 6
Jeff Yan describes Hyperliquid as a protocol whose aspirational goal is to 'house all of finance', a neutral layer that is a step-function improvement over electronic but fragmented markets, analogous to electronic trading's leap over pit trading.
thesis🗣 Jeff Yan (co-founder & CEO, Hyperliquid Labs)conviction: highHyperliquid
So what: The pitch is market-structure consolidation onto one global ledger, not just another crypto venue.
“We view Hyperliquid, the protocol as a sort of step function improvement over electronic but fragmented markets.” ▶ 5:14
Yan says capturing even 90% of all finance would be a huge success, but concedes Hyperliquid is a long way from that goal.
prediction🗣 Jeff Yan (co-founder & CEO, Hyperliquid Labs)conviction: mediumHyperliquid
So what: Ambition is total-market scale; current share is small, so the thesis is long-dated optionality.
“I think even 90% of all of finance would be a huge success. And we're a long way ways away.” ▶ 30:33
Yan claims no other blockchain besides Hyperliquid has the precise vision of a global financial system running on one single global state.
contrarian🗣 Jeff Yan (co-founder & CEO, Hyperliquid Labs)conviction: highHyperliquid
So what: Differentiation vs general-purpose chains rests on a finance-specific single-state design.
“I don't think any of them, other than Hyperliquid, you know, has this precise vision of a global financial system can actually just have one global state.” ▶ 34:53
Yan insists Hyperliquid is not trying to displace incumbents; whether markets are perpetuals or dated futures is irrelevant because what matters is an order book, a clearing system, global distribution and concentration of liquidity.
framework🗣 Jeff Yan (co-founder & CEO, Hyperliquid Labs)conviction: highHyperliquid
So what: Positions Hyperliquid as infrastructure vendor to incumbents rather than competitor, easing adoption and regulatory friction.
“Like we are not we're not trying to displace anything.” ▶ 30:53
The founding thesis in 2023 was that centralized crypto venues offering perpetual futures had clear product-market fit, and the opportunity was to combine centralized-exchange UX with on-chain self-custody to remove exchange counterparty risk.
causal🗣 Jeff Yan (co-founder & CEO, Hyperliquid Labs)conviction: highHyperliquidperpetual futures
So what: Hyperliquid's growth thesis is self-custodied perps with CEX-grade UX; counterparty-risk events at centralized venues favor it.
“I think the the kind of counterparty risk of using exchange is always going to be there, whether it's in a crypto or non crypto context.”
Hyperliquid did not raise VC money, and Yan (formerly at Hudson River Trading) found that no existing chain could house all of finance with incremental improvements, forcing the team to rebuild down to the consensus layer.
anecdote🗣 Jeff Yan (co-founder & CEO, Hyperliquid Labs)conviction: mediumHyperliquidHudson River Trading
So what: Vertical, ground-up build explains performance claims and a lean, founder-controlled cap structure.
Protocol mechanics: HIP-3 deployers, oracles, auctions 10
Under Hyperliquid's permissionless deployment system (HIP-3), a deployer must stake 500,000 HYPE tokens (not $500,000 worth) to unlock the ability to deploy markets, inheriting the protocol's order book, clearing and USDC integration.
data_point🗣 Jeff Yan (co-founder & CEO, Hyperliquid Labs)conviction: highHyperliquidHYPEHIP-3USDC
So what: Deployer staking creates structural demand/lockup for HYPE that scales with the number of markets.
“they have to stake 500,000 currently.” ▶ 16:02
Deployment slots are scarce because each order book and clearing system consumes network compute, and they are allocated by an on-chain Dutch auction run every 31 hours (chosen because 31 is relatively prime to 24 so the reset rotates across time zones).
data_point🗣 Jeff Yan (co-founder & CEO, Hyperliquid Labs)conviction: highHyperliquidHIP-3
So what: Slot scarcity plus auction pricing gives deployment rights an observable market value tied to demand for new markets.
“in every 31 hour interval, currently 31 is simply chosen so that the the time at which the auction resets, is the changes, time zones, as it's relatively prime to the number 24.”
The Dutch auction price decreases linearly from a starting value, the first bidder wins, and the starting price resets to twice the last clearing price, creating a self-correcting approach to fair value that avoids sniping and penny-bidding games of normal auctions.
framework🗣 Jeff Yan (co-founder & CEO, Hyperliquid Labs)conviction: highHyperliquid
So what: Auction clearing prices are a transparent on-chain signal of demand for new listings.
“it resets to twice the last clearing price.” ▶ 22:42
Yan expects deployment resources to become less scarce as the network scales, but says compute will always be some limiting factor for deployers.
prediction🗣 Jeff Yan (co-founder & CEO, Hyperliquid Labs)conviction: mediumHyperliquid
So what: Slot prices may fall over time with scaling, diluting the value of early deployment rights.
A perpetual contract ultimately reduces to one exogenous number, the oracle index price, which the deployer defines and publishes; Yan predicts that as spot markets move onto Hyperliquid, oracles could be derived on-chain rather than published externally.
prediction🗣 Jeff Yan (co-founder & CEO, Hyperliquid Labs)conviction: mediumHyperliquidperpetual futures
So what: On-chain spot liquidity would reduce oracle-manipulation and off-chain dependency risk.
“as spot markets move on to Hyperliquid, you can imagine a system where the Oracle is actually no longer published, but rather derived from ongoing problems.” ▶ 17:32
The protocol enforces a hard rule that consecutive oracle price updates cannot differ by more than 1%; a push 2% away is clamped to 1%, breaking big jumps into near-continuous moves so liquidations proceed orderly.
data_point🗣 Jeff Yan (co-founder & CEO, Hyperliquid Labs)conviction: highHyperliquid
So what: Gap risk is smoothed mechanically, which matters for leveraged traders around weekend/news gaps.
“between two oracle price updates, the relative difference between those two prices cannot be more than 1%.” ▶ 46:46
The protocol makes no subjective liquidity judgments about whether a deployed market makes sense; like an order-book software vendor, it only enforces mathematical constraints.
framework🗣 Jeff Yan (co-founder & CEO, Hyperliquid Labs)conviction: highHyperliquid
So what: Market-quality risk sits with deployers and traders, not the protocol; due diligence must be per-deployer.
“We can't do subjective liquidity, judgments.” ▶ 47:38
Liquidation and the default-waterfall analog are rules-based and executed autonomously by HyperCore, the on-chain clearing submodule, rather than by anyone setting liquidation prices.
framework🗣 Jeff Yan (co-founder & CEO, Hyperliquid Labs)conviction: highHyperliquidHyperCore
So what: Transparent deterministic clearing is the core differentiation vs opaque centralized risk engines.
Consensus assumes two-thirds of stake is non-Byzantine; about 30-something validators exist and the top 27 by stake operate the network at any time, on modest hardware requiring about 128GB of RAM.
data_point🗣 Jeff Yan (co-founder & CEO, Hyperliquid Labs)conviction: highHyperliquid
So what: Low node requirements support decentralization claims, though a 27-validator active set is still concentrated.
“right now, you need 128 gigs of Ram.” ▶ 36:57
Joe Weisenthal notes the auction process means Hyperliquid lacks the thousands of spam 'dog coins' found on Solana or Ethereum, since the protocol itself sets exchange listing rules.
contrarian🗣 Joe Weisenthal (host)conviction: mediumHyperliquidSolanaEthereum
So what: Curated-by-price listings make Hyperliquid look more like a governed exchange than an open chain.
Derivatives over the underlying: price discovery and funding 8
Tracy Alloway's 'inverse pyramid' thesis: as in media, the underlying asset is now the smallest layer of markets, with derivatives, leveraged ETFs, tokenized versions and prediction markets stacking ever larger on top.
thesis🗣 Tracy Alloway (host)conviction: mediumprediction marketsETFs

Yan argues perp volume need not be capped relative to underlying volume, citing index futures and ETFs vs their components: what matters is that liquidity providers stand ready to trade against mispricings, so price discovery can happen on the derivative.
contrarian🗣 Jeff Yan (co-founder & CEO, Hyperliquid Labs)conviction: highperpetual futuresfuturesETFs

“And so you get this effect where the price discovery can happen on a derivative.” ▶ 46:15

thesis🗣 Jeff Yan (co-founder & CEO, Hyperliquid Labs)conviction: highUSDCHyperliquid

Capital Allocators · Ted Seides 1 episode(s) · 31 insights

Erlina Yeo Terwelp: From Banking to PE to Family Office to Columbia Endowment69 min31 insights2026-10-14

Erlina Yeo Terwelp, Columbia endowment MD overseeing privates, says Columbia is growing private markets on offense (little legacy venture, ample unfundeds, publics have outrun privates) while guarding against 2026 venture/AI froth via vintage diversification and concentration monitoring. Private markets positioning: offense amid liquidity worries 4
Columbia's endowment still believes in private markets and is actively growing its private portfolio despite the industry-wide liquidity squeeze from companies staying private longer and fewer exits/distributions.
thesis🗣 Erlina Yeo Terwelp (Columbia Investment Management)conviction: highColumbia Endowmentprivate markets
So what: A large endowment is a net buyer of private exposure while many peers are liquidity-constrained, supporting GP fundraising and secondary-pricing resilience.
“the attractiveness of private markets is very much still there. So we believe in it. We're growing it.” ▶ 43:50
Columbia is liquidity-advantaged versus peers because it entered the 2020s with very little venture exposure, does not have a huge illiquid portfolio, and holds a comfortable level of unfunded commitments.
data_point🗣 Erlina Yeo Terwelp (Columbia Investment Management)conviction: highColumbia Endowmentventure capital
So what: Endowments that were underweight venture into the 2021 peak now have dry powder to deploy into a dislocated private market.
“we actually have a very comfortable amount of unfundeds today” ▶ 44:14

causal🗣 Erlina Yeo Terwelp (Columbia Investment Management)conviction: mediumpublic marketsprivate markets

Vintage-year diversification is the most crucial defense right now: Columbia does not want to concentrate in a 2026 AI-euphoric vintage and wants exposure to 2027 and 2028 vintages too, since the next Nvidia may not be built until 2028.
framework🗣 Erlina Yeo Terwelp (Columbia Investment Management)conviction: highvintage diversificationAINvidia
So what: Pace commitments steadily rather than front-loading into the hottest AI vintage; the biggest AI winners may come later.

Buyout underwriting in a higher-rate world 7
Higher interest rates are the biggest left-tail risk for buyouts because they change deal math, making revenue growth and margin expansion far more important to returns.
causal🗣 Erlina Yeo Terwelp (Columbia Investment Management)conviction: highbuyoutinterest ratesprivate equity
So what: Leverage-driven PE returns are impaired; favor managers with demonstrated operational value creation.
“The biggest one particularly for buyout is higher interest rates. It makes the math different.” ▶ 47:51
Columbia now re-underwrites buyout managers on the value creation bridge (leverage, operating margins/growth, multiple expansion) and favors firms that have proven value creation in the operating piece, with a repeatable, durable playbook.
framework🗣 Erlina Yeo Terwelp (Columbia Investment Management)conviction: highvalue creation bridgebuyout
So what: Ask GPs to decompose returns; discount returns driven by leverage and multiple expansion.
“We really like firms that have proven a lot of value in the mult in the operating piece of it.” ▶ 49:31
Five years ago Columbia deliberately set out to build a durable buyout portfolio, adding a healthy and growing allocation to HALO (high asset, low obsolescence) strategies rather than relying only on tech buyouts.
data_point🗣 Erlina Yeo Terwelp (Columbia Investment Management)conviction: highHALO strategiesbuyouttech buyouts
So what: HALO (hard-asset, low-obsolescence) buyouts are positioned as a hedge against concentrated tech/AI venture bets and AI disruption.
“the high asset low obsolescence type uh strategies um uh the halo strateg IES” ▶ 50:59
The HALO buyout allocation is explicitly intended to act as a buffer against the concentrated bets in the venture portfolio.
causal🗣 Erlina Yeo Terwelp (Columbia Investment Management)conviction: mediumHALO strategiesventure capital
So what: Pairing hard-asset buyouts with AI-heavy venture is a deliberate intra-private diversification approach.
“So that does act as a buffer to some of the bets that we feel are very concentrated” ▶ 51:17
Sector-specialist buyout firms (e.g., healthcare or consumer) can have operational edge because they repeat the same playbook, know the executives and reputations in their arena, and have 'seen the same movie before.'
thesis🗣 Erlina Yeo Terwelp (Columbia Investment Management)conviction: mediumspecialist PEhealthcareconsumer
So what: Specialization is a source of operating alpha in a world where financial engineering is less effective.
“you get really good at executing the same playbook. you've seen a lot of the same movie before.” ▶ 51:52
Buy-and-build is a widely claimed but very hard strategy because integration is difficult; she presses managers with many follow-up questions, but those who execute well can generate exceptional results.
contrarian🗣 Erlina Yeo Terwelp (Columbia Investment Management)conviction: mediumbuy and buildprivate equity
So what: Be skeptical of roll-up pitches; verify integration capability before underwriting multiple-arbitrage stories.
“people say they did dream buy and build. Um, I always have a lot of follow-up questions because it's such a it's such a difficult strategy to execute.”
She sees the era of low interest rates as over, calling it 'the olden days', and frames the current environment as a new world order shaped by AI disruption and fast change.
thesis🗣 Erlina Yeo Terwelp (Columbia Investment Management)conviction: highAIinterest rates
So what: Strategies dependent on cheap money need re-underwriting; adaptation speed becomes an allocator edge.
“The olden days of uh of uh you know uh low interest rates and uh certain certain things that we've been used to in the last 10 20 years are are just not there anymore.”
Risk-factor portfolio construction (pre-TPA) 6
Years before 'total portfolio approach' became common vernacular, Columbia managed its portfolio through risk factor allocation, managing to an equity beta target or range as dynamic risk management, much like a hedge fund.
framework🗣 Erlina Yeo Terwelp (Columbia Investment Management)conviction: hightotal portfolio approachrisk factor allocationColumbia Endowment
So what: TPA's core idea (managing to equity beta rather than asset buckets) has a long track record at Columbia; peers adopting TPA are catching up.
“It was less common back then to manage to an equity beta or a range as more of a form of dynamic risk management which I think that's what TPA is bringing into the system.” ▶ 20:06
Under the generalist risk-factor model, every opportunity was evaluated in its cleanest form (equity beta, liquidity and its cost, leverage) with very little bucket filling and a bottom-up, first-principles approach.
framework🗣 Erlina Yeo Terwelp (Columbia Investment Management)conviction: highrisk factor allocation
So what: Comparing all investments on beta, liquidity, and leverage avoids filling allocation buckets with mediocre managers.
“it was very little bucket filling it was very much a bottoms up let's talk about let's go back to the first principles of what an investment was” ▶ 21:23
She believes TPA is very difficult to apply to some private markets and private asset classes, even though it works well on the public side.
contrarian🗣 Erlina Yeo Terwelp (Columbia Investment Management)conviction: mediumtotal portfolio approachprivate markets
So what: TPA adopters should be cautious about the false precision of beta estimates for illiquid assets.
“I think TPA is very difficult to do with uh some private markets and some private asset classes” ▶ 20:53
Columbia frames portfolio construction as a set of explicit tilts (geography, early versus late stage, specialist versus generalist, tech versus biotech) rolled up from each asset class to the total portfolio; it is currently very US-tilted and debates whether that is acceptable given American exceptionalism.
framework🗣 Erlina Yeo Terwelp (Columbia Investment Management)conviction: mediumAmerican exceptionalismUSportfolio construction
So what: US concentration across asset classes is a conscious, debated bet at a major endowment; watch for any ex-US rebalancing.
“our portfolio in general is very use tilted. Are we okay with that?” ▶ 46:46
Moving from generalist to asset-class specialist taught her that portfolio construction, planning tilts and researching areas to inject into a portfolio, was the piece she had most underappreciated.
anecdote🗣 Erlina Yeo Terwelp (Columbia Investment Management)conviction: lowportfolio construction
So what: Specialist structures add deliberate portfolio planning that pure manager-picking generalist models can miss.
Under CIO Kim Lew, Columbia shifted from a generalist structure to specialists (two on privates, two on publics) while retaining generalist heritage: every fund is still discussed by the whole team.
data_point🗣 Erlina Yeo Terwelp (Columbia Investment Management)conviction: mediumKim LewColumbia Endowment
So what: Hybrid specialist/generalist structure is a template for small endowment teams; she calls the dichotomy false.
“we still keep a lot of generalist heritage so uh you know we discuss every fund together” ▶ 25:54
Manager selection and exit discipline 8
She argues there is little fundamental difference between evaluating a business and a fund manager: both require assessing the beta they are in, edge, positioning, moats, and the leadership team's ability to hire, inspire and execute.
framework🗣 Erlina Yeo Terwelp (Columbia Investment Management)conviction: mediummanager selection
So what: Direct-investing skills transfer to LP work; treat GPs as businesses.
“I don't think there is a huge huge difference in the fundamentals of what you're looking at between a business and and a manager.” ▶ 17:00
Columbia's diligence process runs from a team-wide one-pager to a five-pager on the roughly five make-or-break questions unique to each investment, then a final investment proposal, mirroring the process at Catterton.
framework🗣 Erlina Yeo Terwelp (Columbia Investment Management)conviction: mediumCattertonColumbia Endowment
So what: Focusing diligence on a handful of decisive questions is more productive than exhaustive checklists.
“I believe that there's probably five things unique to every every investment that will make or break the deal.” ▶ 32:09
Off-list references only work with warm connectivity; roughly 70% of the time Columbia's references come through people it already knows, and it has declined funds due to a very negative, corroborated off-list reference.
data_point🗣 Erlina Yeo Terwelp (Columbia Investment Management)conviction: highoff-list references
So what: LP network depth is a real diligence edge; cold reference calls rarely surface the truth.
“I mean we have absolutely said no to funds um because of an offus reference that we got that was” ▶ 36:02
The top reason Columbia exits a manager is fund size growing beyond what suits the strategy, which raises the question of whether the GP is now chasing AUM rather than returns, though even doubling or tripling fund size is not an automatic no.
framework🗣 Erlina Yeo Terwelp (Columbia Investment Management)conviction: highfund sizeAUM
So what: Fund-size creep is the main signal of style drift; analyze capacity rather than reflexively exiting.
“are you now searching for AUM versus returns um question” ▶ 37:37
The second exit trigger is a manager late in its life cycle, marked by high turnover and carry allocated in ways that don't reflect where the talent is, independent of track record.
framework🗣 Erlina Yeo Terwelp (Columbia Investment Management)conviction: mediumcarried interestGP succession
So what: Carry distribution and turnover are leading indicators of future GP performance that past returns won't show.
“you have a lot of turnover you have carry that's allocated in ways that we don't think reflects uh you know the talent that's in your organization” ▶ 38:09
The third, rarest exit trigger is a red flag: a manager sending inaccurate information, since honesty and transparency are core to the relationship.
framework🗣 Erlina Yeo Terwelp (Columbia Investment Management)conviction: highmanager selection
So what: Integrity breaches are terminal regardless of performance.
Portfolio-level overweights (e.g., too many tech buyout managers) usually lead Columbia to trim commitments at the margin or not add new managers, with larger cuts happening only during broader portfolio reviews.
framework🗣 Erlina Yeo Terwelp (Columbia Investment Management)conviction: mediumtech buyoutsportfolio construction
So what: Re-ups from large LPs in crowded sub-strategies may be smaller even for well-regarded GPs.
“if we have too many, I don't know, tech buyout managers, maybe we won't add a bunch more.” ▶ 39:58
Her biggest pet peeve is intellectual dishonesty, including people mistaking identifying risks for a lack of conviction; she tells her team she'd be upset if they don't find warts in a deal.
contrarian🗣 Erlina Yeo Terwelp (Columbia Investment Management)conviction: mediumintellectual honesty
So what: A strong investment culture rewards surfacing risks rather than selling the deal.
“people mistake risk for lack of conviction, identifying risks for lack of conviction” ▶ 1:07:38

No Priors · Elad Gil & Sarah Guo 1 episode(s) · 25 insights

Revolutionizing Cybersecurity and AI-Driven Science with OpenAI President Greg Brockman39 min25 insights2026-10-15
▶ Watch on YouTube
OpenAI president Greg Brockman argues AI now generates genuinely new knowledge (almost 400 open math problems resolved), ranks drug discovery as the top science target while flagging biology's data and regulatory bottlenecks, and makes the contrarian case that security spend should vastly outstrip software spend during a time-limited 'defenders window'. He also describes the Dots proactive agent, GPT-6 rollout, and OpenAI's strategy of unifying consumer and enterprise.
Cyber: the defenders window · 9AI-generated math and new knowledge · 3AI for drug discovery and regulation · 6OpenAI product strategy: Dots, GPT-6, no-UI · 6Human agency mission · 1
Cyber: the defenders window 9
Brockman argues security spending should vastly outstrip software spending because AI is making software itself nearly free, so reliability and security become the scarce value.
contrarian🗣 Greg Brockman (OpenAI President)conviction: highOpenAI
So what: Implies a structural re-rating of security budgets versus the traditional low-single-digit share of IT spend.
“cyber spend, security spend should actually vastly outstrip SEC software spend. Like I really think that that's a statement.” ▶ 34:17
OpenAI describes the current period as a 'defenders window': peak frontier cyber capabilities are not yet broadly diffused, but the broadly available capabilities ratchet up with every model release from providers worldwide, so the window will close.
framework🗣 Greg Brockman (OpenAI President)conviction: highOpenAI
So what: Time-limited urgency for enterprises to adopt AI-driven vulnerability discovery now, before attackers have equivalent tools.
“we have peak frontier AI capabilities that can help defenders secure themselves. They're not broadly diffused yet, but that the broadly diffused capabilities are ratcheting.” ▶ 27:26
Brockman cites high-profile hacks of banks in South Korea that were driven by AI models as early evidence of AI-enabled offensive cyber impact.
data_point🗣 Greg Brockman (OpenAI President)conviction: mediumSouth Korea
So what: Financial institutions are an early target class for AI-driven attacks.
“You're starting to see, for example, in South Korea that there were some high-profile hacks of of of banks that were driven by different uh models.” ▶ 27:50
OpenAI runs a Daybreak program that gives defenders responsible access to its peak-intelligence models to find and fix vulnerabilities, backed by a billion-dollar commitment to help frontline defenders use its models.
data_point🗣 Greg Brockman (OpenAI President)conviction: highOpenAIDaybreak
So what: OpenAI is positioning itself as a direct participant in the cyber defense market, not just a model supplier.
“we have a billion-dollar commitment that we've made to help frontline defenders get access to our models” ▶ 29:51
OpenAI put about 25% of its production engineers' projects on hold to use its then-peak model to probe all its infrastructure, built tools to repeat the process, fixed issues until the model's findable vulnerabilities were saturated, and expects to repeat this with every new model.
anecdote🗣 Greg Brockman (OpenAI President)conviction: highOpenAI
So what: Suggests a new operating cadence for security teams: re-audit and saturate with each frontier model release, implying recurring demand for AI security tooling.
“we took about 25% of our production engineers and said all of your projects are on hold. You are just securing now.” ▶ 31:02
Brockman frames the internet's insecurity as accumulated human-made tech debt (security bolted on, built for resilience not security) that AI will now surface and allow the world to pay down.
causal🗣 Greg Brockman (OpenAI President)conviction: highOpenAI
So what: Expect a near-term spike in discovered vulnerabilities and incidents before the debt is paid down.
“Like the internet's really been built for resilience, not necessarily for security.” ▶ 29:32
AI makes formal verification of software practical: models are strong at proving things, so they can prove security properties of software, something the technology allowed before but no one did for anything significant.
prediction🗣 Greg Brockman (OpenAI President)conviction: mediumOpenAI
So what: Formal verification could move from niche to mainstream, a potential category for security startups.
“Why not prove security properties of software?” ▶ 30:39
Brockman says an organization's security will increasingly depend on both one-time fundamentals and the ongoing level of AI cyber capability it applies to defend itself, and that defenders can outstrip attackers only with a coordinated response including resourcing critical infrastructure.
framework🗣 Greg Brockman (OpenAI President)conviction: mediumOpenAI
So what: Security becomes a function of compute/model access spend, not just headcount and controls.
“the security of or organization is related to both the fundamentals that they've gotten right which is kind of onetime investment but also the level of cyber capability they've used to defend themselves” ▶ 32:45
Software supply chain auditing, previously impossible due to scarce human expertise, can now be done with AI; third-party tools like Socket scan open-source supply chains for vulnerabilities and OpenAI partners with companies like CrowdStrike.
thesis🗣 Greg Brockman (OpenAI President)conviction: mediumSocketCrowdStrikeOpenAI
So what: Supply chain security vendors and AI-native incumbents stand to benefit from the defenders window.
“All of that needs to change and it was totally impossible to change with human software developers” ▶ 35:24
AI-generated math and new knowledge 3
Brockman says OpenAI's math results make it 'impossible to deny' that AI can generate new knowledge, resolving hundred-year-old problems, though AI is not yet producing whole new theories.
thesis🗣 Greg Brockman (OpenAI President)conviction: highOpenAI
So what: Marks a shift in AI value proposition from automation to scientific discovery.
“we're not yet coming up with whole brand new theories, but we are able to resolve these hundred-year-old math problems.” ▶ 2:38
OpenAI published almost 400 very important open math problems that are now resolved, and domain experts reviewing them report surprising, insightful applications of ideas rather than incomprehensible brute-force proofs like the four-color theorem.
data_point🗣 Greg Brockman (OpenAI President)conviction: highOpenAI
So what: Quality of AI proofs (insight, not just correctness) strengthens the case for AI as a research collaborator.
“we published about almost 400 very important open problems that are now resolved” ▶ 5:30
Brockman attributes math progress mainly to data availability rather than checkability, since natural-language proofs are hard to check; the deep learning equation is compute plus data plus algorithms equals capability.
causal🗣 Greg Brockman (OpenAI President)conviction: highOpenAI
So what: Predicts which domains AI will crack next: those with abundant data (math, theoretical physics) before data-poor ones (biology).
“the thing that actually I think is the core insight is really about data, right?” ▶ 10:17
AI for drug discovery and regulation 6
Brockman ranks drug discovery as OpenAI's clearest, top-priority science domain, arguing from first principles that neural nets have no inherent reason to be better at natural language than biology, so they should crack the 'language of biology'.
thesis🗣 Greg Brockman (OpenAI President)conviction: highOpenAI
So what: Signals OpenAI investment and competition in AI-bio, relevant to biotech and AI-drug-discovery players.
“from first principles, you should be able to really be able to crack the language of biology.” ▶ 14:13
Biology is data-bottlenecked, so some areas will accelerate fast while others stall where data does not exist, making wet-lab and simulation innovations critical.
prediction🗣 Greg Brockman (OpenAI President)conviction: highOpenAI
So what: Value accrues to whoever can generate biological data (wet labs, simulation), not just models.
“there are going to be places where the fundamental data just does not exist and so then innovations on wet labs innovation on how you actually do simulations all of this will become incredibly important” ▶ 12:00
Non-scientific steps like regulatory filings are a major drug-development bottleneck (about a year to write up a clinical trial case) that AI can compress.
causal🗣 Greg Brockman (OpenAI President)conviction: medium
So what: Near-term AI ROI in pharma may come from documentation and process steps rather than molecule design.
“regulatory filings huge bottleneck right it takes like a year to write up your whole um sort of case for clinical trial” ▶ 11:43
A host cites drug development economics of about 15 years and $1.5 billion, with roughly $50 million preclinical and $1.4 billion-plus in clinical trials, arguing early AI focus on molecule design misses the costly downstream steps.
data_point🗣 Host (No Priors)conviction: mediumAlphaFold
So what: The largest cost pool for AI to attack is clinical trials, not discovery.
“It's $ 1.5 billion. 50 million or so tends to be pre-clinical mouse studies, the molecule, and then 1.4 billion plus tends to be the clinical trial.” ▶ 12:36
Short-run impact is possible in n-of-1 diseases, where a cure may already exist buried in a paper, and AI can massively scale up literature search to find it.
prediction🗣 Greg Brockman (OpenAI President)conviction: medium
So what: Literature-mining for rare disease treatments is a near-term AI application.
“there's even like lots of people who the drug to cure their particular condition exists it's just that it's just out there somewhere in the literature” ▶ 14:39
If AI proves the physics of drug discovery have changed, regulation should adapt as with self-driving cars; trials are already moving to jurisdictions like Australia with more favorable rules, though neural nets do not yet beat clinical trials.
prediction🗣 Greg Brockman (OpenAI President)conviction: mediumAustralia
So what: Regulatory arbitrage across geographies may accelerate AI-enabled drug development.
“you're already seeing a lot of of of of people moving their their trials to places where the regulatory environment makes more sense” ▶ 16:58
OpenAI product strategy: Dots, GPT-6, no-UI 6
GPT-6 has been rolled out to over a billion people, and ChatGPT has 1.2 billion consumers per Brockman.
data_point🗣 Greg Brockman (OpenAI President)conviction: highGPT-6ChatGPTOpenAI
So what: OpenAI's consumer distribution scale is a core moat.
“one of the strengths of OpenAI is the fact that we have 1.2 2 billion consumers on chatbt” ▶ 22:06
Dots is OpenAI's proactive, always-on agent with its own cloud computer that keeps working when the user stops, integrating inbox, Slack and calendar; Brockman says its change is form factor, not capability, and calls it a game changer in the workplace.
anecdote🗣 Greg Brockman (OpenAI President)conviction: highDotsOpenAISlack
So what: Proactive background agents are a new product category that threatens task and productivity tools.
“it's a change to the form factor, not the capability” ▶ 19:40
OpenAI's strategy is to unify consumer and enterprise because it uniquely has both 1.2 billion ChatGPT consumers and a leading coding and enterprise business, competing on one front rather than two.
thesis🗣 Greg Brockman (OpenAI President)conviction: highOpenAIChatGPT
So what: Competitors with only one side (consumer or enterprise) face a structural disadvantage.
“no one else has both in house and so that a lot of our strategies to say let's unify these” ▶ 22:16
Brockman says the main difference between consumer and workplace assistants is compute cost: consumer needs free/low-cost scale, while work justifies peak intelligence as long as returns to paying more persist; enterprise also adds admins and security teams.
framework🗣 Greg Brockman (OpenAI President)conviction: mediumChatGPTOpenAI
So what: Expect tiered pricing by compute intensity; enterprise is where premium inference spend lands.
“as long as you're getting returns to paying more, it's worthwhile to throw more comput.” ▶ 21:02
Brockman predicts a renaissance of entrepreneurs, citing people quitting jobs to start companies they would not have started without reliable AI lowering barriers to entry.
prediction🗣 Greg Brockman (OpenAI President)conviction: medium
So what: More, smaller startups; blurring of consumer and enterprise customer categories.
“we're going to have this renaissance of entrepreneurs” ▶ 22:33
Brockman says 'the best UI is no UI', while OpenAI ships generative 'intelligent UI' that builds bespoke interfaces on the fly (e.g., flight booking, a one-off bike-assembly app), and GPT-6 can give a quick answer then keep thinking.
thesis🗣 Greg Brockman (OpenAI President)conviction: mediumGPT-6OpenAI

“I think that the best UI is no UI” ▶ 23:39

Wealthion 1 episode(s) · 25 insights

Housing Market in Crisis: Nick Gerli Warns the Worst Is Yet to Come35 min25 insights2026-10-14
▶ Watch on YouTube
Reventure's Nick Gerli argues US housing is in a multi-year recession with sales below 4M annualized (2009 levels) and the weakest stock-adjusted demand in 40 years, with prices, not 7% rates, the core problem. The market is sharply bifurcated: Northeast/Midwest shortages vs. Sun Belt oversupply and busts (Austin -27%), plus a K-shaped split by neighborhood income and seller mortgage rate, while multifamily faces a refi wall and 2009-level agency delinquencies; he expects bigger discounts over the next year but no banking crisis.
Demand collapse to GFC levels · 3Prices matter more than rates · 6Bifurcated regional market · 6Sun Belt migration reversal · 3Multifamily and investor distress · 6Lennar price crash and Berkshire bet · 1
Demand collapse to GFC levels 3
US existing home buying fell below 4 million annualized in August 2026, roughly 2009 sales levels, and adjusted for housing stock size demand is the lowest in 40 years.
data_point🗣 Nick Gerliconviction: highUS housing market
So what: Transaction volume, not price, is the clearest signal of a housing recession; brokers, mortgage originators and title businesses face GFC-level volumes.
“the number of people buying homes in the US just fell below 4 million annualized in August 2026. That's roughly at 2009 levels of sales.” ▶ 0:48
With prices and 7-7.5% mortgage rates, the typical US monthly mortgage payment including taxes and insurance is about $2,850 (~$35-36K/yr) versus median household income of ~$87K, a near-40% payment-to-gross-income ratio, and renting the same house is 30-40% cheaper than buying it.
data_point🗣 Nick Gerliconviction: highmortgage ratesmedian household income
So what: Affordability is mathematically broken for the median buyer; demand cannot normalize without a large price/rate adjustment.
Millennials and Gen Z, having grown up post-GFC and living in amenity-rich luxury apartments, view homeownership more as a burden and are less willing to become house poor than mid-2000s buyers.
causal🗣 Nick Gerliconviction: mediumMillennialsGen Zluxury apartments
So what: A generational preference shift could structurally lower the ownership rate and the price buyers will pay.
“I think the millennial and Gen Z population kind of grew up post GFC. and I think looks at owning a home almost sometimes as a burden” ▶ 14:04
Prices matter more than rates 6
Mortgage rates have ranged roughly 5.8-7.6% over the last four years with no discernible change in demand, so incremental rate moves matter less than people think.
contrarian🗣 Nick Gerliconviction: highmortgage rates
So what: Fed or rate-cut hopes alone are unlikely to revive housing activity; don't trade housing names purely on rate moves.
“demand has just been really low the last four years regardless of whether the rates 75 or 59.” ▶ 15:39
A strong rebound in housing demand would require both mortgage rates falling to about 5% and prices dropping about 20%; short of that, the market stays stuck for the next couple of years.
prediction🗣 Nick Gerliconviction: highmortgage ratesUS housing market
So what: Sets a concrete trigger for a housing bottom call: watch for combined ~5% rates and ~20% price declines.
“we really would need to see mortgage rates go to five and prices drop 20%. Like if those two things happened, I think there would be a big rebound in demand.” ▶ 17:01
On a 130-year chart, 6.5-7.5% mortgage rates are historically normal while inflation-adjusted home prices are above the 2006 bubble peak, so the core problem is prices, not rates.
framework🗣 Nick Gerliconviction: highinflation-adjusted home pricesmortgage rates
So what: The 2010s low-rate era was the anomaly; mean reversion likely comes via real prices falling.
“I totally agree with the assertion that prices matter way more than rates.” ▶ 17:32
Builders, about 15% of home sales, run a separate market via aggressive rate buydowns; D.R. Horton said its sales backlog carries a 4.9% mortgage rate, often ~2% below market for the full term, yet demand is only okay even with price cuts.
data_point🗣 Nick Gerliconviction: mediumD.R. Hortonhomebuildersmortgage rate buydowns
So what: Even subsidized sub-5% rates aren't unlocking strong demand, a warning for builder margins and volumes.
“I think Dr. Horton, the biggest builder in the US, said in a recent earnings call that their backlog on their uh sales is at a 4.9% mortgage rate.” ▶ 16:24
Austin values are down 27% over four years, the biggest US housing bust, yet sales remain low, showing price declines alone do not quickly revive demand due to buyer inertia and lag.
data_point🗣 Nick Gerliconviction: highAustin
So what: Falling prices may not mark a bottom; expect a long lag before volumes recover in bust markets.
“Austin has gone down 27% the last four years on value. Austin's the biggest housing bust in the US right now.” ▶ 19:03
Gerli bought a house in 2026 for $330,000 that sold for $490,000 in 2023 via a lowball offer, a 35% discount, arguing buyers need no-brainer deals before re-engaging.
anecdote🗣 Nick Gerliconviction: mediumNick Gerli
So what: Motivated sellers in oversupplied markets accept deep discounts; aggressive lowball offers can work now.
“I bought I bought this house that sold for 490,000 in 2023. I bought it for 330,000 today” ▶ 21:21
Bifurcated regional market 6
This is one of the most bifurcated US housing markets ever: the Northeast and Midwest have shortages and rising prices while the South and West have supply surpluses and falling prices.
thesis🗣 Nick Gerliconviction: highNortheastMidwestSun BeltNew Jersey
So what: National averages mislead; regional exposure determines outcomes for homebuilders, lenders and REITs.
“This is one of the most bifurcated housing markets we have ever seen in US history.” ▶ 3:08
The 3-5 million unit national housing shortage narrative is super regional and is often used as an excuse for high prices, while markets like Nashville have record inventory.
contrarian🗣 Nick Gerliconviction: highnational housing shortageNashville
So what: Be skeptical of shortage-based bull cases for Sun Belt housing.
“They say we're three to five million homes short in the US. And you know, that's often used as an excuse for why prices are still high, but that's actually super regional” ▶ 3:44
Within metros there is a K-shaped housing market: half of Atlanta's home price map is down 15-25% over four years while the other half is up 5-10%, with higher-income neighborhoods more resilient and lower-income, investor-heavy areas falling most.
framework🗣 Nick Gerliconviction: highAtlantaK-shaped housing market
So what: Neighborhood income and investor share are predictors of price resilience for buyers and lenders.
“half of the Atlanta home price map is down 15 to 25% the last four years. The other half is still up 5 to 10.” ▶ 23:25
Gerli's research finds the high/low-income price bifurcation is driven more by supply than demand: demand is down similarly, but supply has grown far slower in high-income areas where owners have stock wealth and locked-in low mortgage rates.
causal🗣 Nick Gerliconviction: mediummortgage lock-in
So what: Track listing inventory growth by neighborhood income tier as the leading indicator of price declines.
“is it more demand or supply that's influencing that bifurcation? And what I found that it's actually more supply.” ▶ 24:54
Sellers with 3% mortgages implicitly value their homes at market value plus the interest savings, while 7%-rate sellers who bought in the last four years are strained and far more willing to cut prices, so buyers should find out the seller's mortgage rate.
framework🗣 Nick Gerliconviction: mediummortgage lock-in
So what: Seller loan vintage is an actionable negotiating signal and a predictor of where discounts appear.
“people who are looking to buy should like look out for that like what's the mortgage rate that the owner and the seller has because that's going to reveal a lot about their willingness to negotiate.” ▶ 26:41
Condos and townhomes, especially in Florida with high HOA fees, are seeing some declines of 40-50% from peak pricing.
data_point🗣 Nick Gerliconviction: highFloridacondosHOA fees
So what: Florida condos are the most distressed residential segment; avoid catching that knife early.
Sun Belt migration reversal 3
The 2010s and early-pandemic exodus from the Northeast and Midwest has faded in the last three years and a reverse migration is starting; inventory in Florida, Las Vegas, and Texas is breaking 10-15 year records and Las Vegas home sales are down 40% over four to five years despite the California in-migration narrative.
contrarian🗣 Nick Gerliconviction: mediumSun BeltNortheastMidwestFloridaLas VegasTexas
So what: Legacy Northeast/Midwest housing markets may outperform Sun Belt boom towns for years.
“I would actually argue a reverse migration trend is starting where people are starting to move back to some of these cities in the Northeast and Midwest” ▶ 5:34
Sun Belt migration runs in roughly 10-12 year cycles; after the mid-2000s peak, net migration dropped 60-80% for five to six years until prices reset, and a similar drop-off is happening now.
framework🗣 Nick Gerliconviction: mediumSun Belt
So what: Implies a multi-year Sun Belt reset before the next boom; Gerli is not a long-run Sun Belt doomsayer.
“Every 10 to 12 years there's kind of a new cycle that kickstarts itself.” ▶ 7:18
Baby boomers with assets and mortgage-free homes are resilient sellers today, but the silver tsunami of boomers aging out over the next 10 years will structurally increase supply in Sun Belt vacation markets.
prediction🗣 Nick Gerliconviction: mediumbaby boomerssilver tsunamiOrlandoSun Belt
So what: Long-run supply headwind for retiree/vacation markets regardless of the near-term cycle.
“we're just going to see a structural increase in supply in these uh baby boomer vacation sunb belt markets over the next 10 years due to simple aging demographics.” ▶ 10:38
Multifamily and investor distress 6
The Wall Street Journal reported a $2 trillion multifamily refinancing wall; landlords with 3-4% debt on 5-10 year terms are rolling to ~6-6.5%, doubling interest burdens in some cases while rents fall (Austin -20%, Nashville ~-10%).
causal🗣 Nick Gerliconviction: highThe Wall Street JournalmultifamilyAustinNashville
So what: Expect rising forced sales and losses for 2021-vintage Sun Belt apartment owners and their lenders.
“the Wall Street Journal just ran an article a couple days ago about how there's a$ two trillion dollar refinancing wall hitting for multif family.” ▶ 27:07
Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac multifamily delinquency rates already match or exceed 2009 levels and CMBS delinquencies approach them, despite only 4% unemployment.
data_point🗣 Nick Gerliconviction: highFannie MaeFreddie MacCMBS
So what: Commercial real estate credit is fragile; even a small rise in unemployment could sharply worsen losses.
“the Fanny and Freddy multif family delinquency rates are already matching or exceeding 2009 levels.” ▶ 32:07
Corporate single-family investors stopped buying in 2023 and the Road to Housing Act bans corporate investors owning more than 350 homes from buying more existing homes starting January 2027.
data_point🗣 Nick Gerliconviction: highRoad to Housing Actcorporate investors
So what: Removes a marginal bid from single-family markets, particularly in investor-heavy Sun Belt metros.
“the Road to Housing Act bans corporate investors who own more than 350 homes from buying more existing homes.” ▶ 28:57
With the 10-year Treasury above 5% and single-family rental cap rates around 4.8%, a government bond yields more than a rental property, so the math fails even for smaller investors.
data_point🗣 Nick Gerliconviction: high10-year Treasurysingle-family rental
So what: Negative risk premium on rentals implies cap rates must rise, meaning prices fall, to attract investors.
“Cap rate to buy a single family rental is 4.8, meaning that you can make more money just by sitting on your couch and buying a US government bond” ▶ 29:19
Unlike 2008-2011, when foreclosures reached about 5% and delinquencies 8-9%, single-family mortgage quality is better, banks less exposed and homeowners have more equity, so Gerli does not expect a banking crisis, though values can still fall sharply.
contrarian🗣 Nick Gerliconviction: highbanksforeclosures
So what: Price downside without systemic contagion: a real estate trade, not a bank-crisis trade.
“I don't think we're going to have some huge offshoot banking crisis that comes out of a housing downturn this time around” ▶ 32:45
Gerli says this is the first time in 15+ years that real distress creates opportunity, and he expects even bigger discounts over the next year.
prediction🗣 Nick Gerliconviction: mediumUS housing market
So what: Buyers and investors may be better served waiting 6-12 months while building dry powder.
“I think we're going to see even bigger discounts over the next year.” ▶ 0:04
Lennar price crash and Berkshire bet 1
Lennar has cut prices about 30% from its 2022 peak (typical house ~$510,000 net of incentives to ~$360,000 via price cuts plus rate buydowns); Berkshire Hathaway's reported large Lennar position may reflect a view that Lennar has already done its painful restructuring and price cuts, but Gerli still expects more housing pain over the next year or two and was surprised by the timing.
contrarian🗣 Nick Gerliconviction: mediumBerkshire HathawayLennar
So what: Builders that cut early may bottom before the broader housing market; but Gerli sees near-term downside.
“I I just personally still think there's like more pain coming over the next year or two.” ▶ 34:16

All-In Podcast 1 episode(s) · 18 insights

Elizabeth Holmes' Husband Billy Evans: The Trial, Prison & Nathan Fielder’s Documentary42 min18 insights2026-10-15
▶ Watch on YouTube
Interview with Billy Evans, husband of imprisoned Theranos founder Elizabeth Holmes, mostly personal (their relationship, trial, prison life, a delayed documentary) with a defense-side counter-narrative: acquittal on patient-fraud counts, Walgreens contract defaults behind missed revenue, and a conspiracy charge that more than doubled her sentence. Market-relevant signal is limited to sentencing mechanics and Evans' startup Hemanthes, which builds low-cost Raman spectroscopy sensors to feed biological data to AI.
Theranos case counter-narrative · 8Sentencing mechanics and restitution · 4Media narrative and the documentary · 2Hemanthes and post-Theranos diagnostics · 4
Theranos case counter-narrative 8
Billy Evans says Theranos ran about 12.5 million patient blood tests over an 18-month period, and that the following year's plan was roughly 100x that volume.
data_point🗣 Billy Evansconviction: mediumTheranos
So what: Scale claims from the defense side frame the patient-fraud counts as statistically tiny relative to volume.
“Liz ran about 12 and 1/2 million tests on patients at Theranos over an 18-month period.” ▶ 19:40
Evans claims Theranos was on track to become the third-largest US lab company after Quest and LabCorp, and to surpass both the year after.
contrarian🗣 Billy Evansconviction: lowTheranosQuestLabCorp
So what: Unverified insider projection; signals how the family will frame Theranos' lost potential in coming narratives.
“they were going to be the third largest lab company in the country.” ▶ 19:57
Of 13 counts, Holmes was acquitted of the patient-fraud counts (four patients cited by the government) and convicted of defrauding three investors plus conspiracy.
data_point🗣 Billy Evansconviction: highElizabeth HolmesTheranos
So what: The conviction rests on investor fraud, not patient harm, a distinction Evans says the public misses.
“she was convicted of defrauding three investors um and then colluding with her partner at the time to do so, so conspiracy.” ▶ 21:18
Evans argues diagnostics is inherently inaccurate (90% accuracy good for some tests, 70% for others), so four bad results out of 12.5 million tests is not statistically meaningful evidence of systemic problems.
framework🗣 Billy Evansconviction: mediumTheranos
So what: A useful lens for judging diagnostics startups: error rates must be benchmarked against test-specific norms, not perfection.
“diagnostics is like an inherently inaccurate space. Um 90% accuracy for some tests is good, and for other really inaccurate tests 70% is really good.” ▶ 20:57
Evans attributes Theranos' missed revenue numbers to Walgreens defaulting on contractual obligations (around 800 stores) after the Carreyrou reporting, not to fabricated projections.
causal🗣 Billy Evansconviction: mediumTheranosWalgreensCarreyrou
So what: Shows how negative press can trigger counterparty contract defaults that turn into the 'missed numbers' later cited as fraud.
“they had contractual obligations with Walgreens to pay them that amount of money. And then when bad press came out, then they defaulted on those obligations” ▶ 16:05
Evans calls Holmes' choice to stay silent and litigate only in the courtroom, per the judge's instruction, the single biggest mistake she ever made, because her power was in telling her story.
contrarian🗣 Billy Evansconviction: highElizabeth Holmes
So what: For founders under investigation, ceding the public narrative can be costlier than legal risk of speaking.
“And I think that was the single biggest mistake that Elizabeth has ever made, to be like really clear.” ▶ 24:11
Evans argues the press framing of Holmes as manipulating investors with her eyes and voice is sexist, noting a male founder securing a $100 million commitment from a major family office in a 45-minute meeting would be praised as a great storyteller.
contrarian🗣 Billy Evansconviction: mediumElizabeth Holmes
So what: Highlights asymmetric narrative treatment of founder persuasion that shapes reputational risk.
“It would be a 45-minute meeting. They'd commit $100 million by the end of the meeting.” ▶ 14:22
Evans says the Holmes family will publish a document within roughly two weeks that he expects people to find 'pretty shocking' about the case.
prediction🗣 Billy Evansconviction: mediumElizabeth Holmes
So what: Watch for a forthcoming release that could reopen the Theranos narrative and the documentary's reception.
“in the next 2 weeks I ask you to read something that we are going to put out.” ▶ 22:36
Sentencing mechanics and restitution 4
Holmes was sentenced to 135 months (11.25 years); Evans says adding 10 investors as victims via the conspiracy charge raised the guideline sentence from roughly 4.5-5 years to 11 by increasing loss amount and triggering the 10-or-more-victims enhancement.
causal🗣 Billy Evansconviction: mediumElizabeth HolmesRupert Murdoch
So what: Conspiracy counts can sharply expand loss and victim enhancements in white-collar sentencing.
“So, that raised her sentence from something like 4 and 1/2 or 5 years to 11.” ▶ 37:52
Holmes owes $435 million in restitution, and 70% of money sent to her commissary account is taken; the victims include families such as the DeVos, Walton and Niarchos families.
data_point🗣 Billy Evansconviction: highElizabeth HolmesDeVosWaltonsNiarchos
So what: Restitution far exceeds any realistic repayment capacity; collections hit even small commissary transfers.
“Yeah, $435 million.” ▶ 37:13
Holmes recently received a one-year sentence reduction after sentencing guidelines changed, and Evans says the government conceded there was no evidence any investor faced financial hardship.
data_point🗣 Billy Evansconviction: mediumElizabeth Holmes
So what: Guideline changes can retroactively shorten white-collar sentences.
“recently we got a 1-year sentence reduction for Elizabeth because they changed the sentencing guidelines.” ▶ 38:11
Under the First Step Act, inmates doing recidivism-reducing programming earn 16 days off for every 30 served; Evans expects Holmes could reach a halfway house in Q2 of next year after 1,205 days at Bryan Federal Prison Camp.
data_point🗣 Billy Evansconviction: mediumFirst Step ActDonald TrumpBryan Federal Prison CampElizabeth Holmes
So what: Effective time served is far below headline sentences; Holmes' public re-emergence could come in 2027.
“she can earn 16 days off of her sentence for every 30 days that she's there if she's doing programming.” ▶ 36:34

The documentary featuring Holmes and Evans (filmed with "Nathan and Lance") was meant to release within a year of filming but has taken three years; Evans has not seen it and admits concern given a trailer the host says makes her look 'insane'.
anecdote🗣 Billy Evansconviction: mediumElizabeth Holmes

“It was supposed to wrap. Like so it was supposed to come out within a year of the time in which they first started shooting.” ▶ 34:46
Evans says the documentary's intent was to scale the experience of meeting Holmes in person, since skeptical friends and family consistently changed their views after spending time with her.
thesis🗣 Billy Evansconviction: mediumElizabeth Holmes

“that was the intention of the film is to help scale that up and allow other people to sit with her and to make up their own minds.” ▶ 34:27
Hemanthes and post-Theranos diagnostics 4
Evans' startup Hemanthes is building low-cost Raman spectroscopy sensors, shrinking lab devices that cost millions and are many feet long, to generate rich biological datasets for AI.
thesis🗣 Billy Evansconviction: highHemanthesRaman spectroscopyLuminar

Evans argues that after Theranos failed, very few companies have tried anything ambitious in making diagnostics cheaper and more accessible, leaving the space open.
contrarian🗣 Billy Evansconviction: mediumTheranosHemanthes

“in the wake of Theranos failing, very few people have tried to do something incredibly ambitious in that space.” ▶ 39:51
Evans frames the opportunity as building biological data for AI rather than for human doctors, letting models find correlations across disparate signals.
framework🗣 Billy Evansconviction: mediumHemanthes
So what: Signals a shift in diagnostics design from human-readable outputs to machine-consumable datasets.
